# Open Relay Portal

[![License: AGPL-3.0](https://img.shields.io/badge/License-AGPL_3.0-blue.svg)](LICENSE)
[![Python 3.11+](https://img.shields.io/badge/Python-3.11+-3776AB.svg?logo=python&logoColor=white)](https://python.org)
[![Release](https://img.shields.io/github/v/release/dustinm16/portal)](https://github.com/dustinm16/portal/releases)

**A privacy-first, self-hosted streaming and community platform.**

Open Relay Portal runs on a server you control and gives a community live streams with recordings and a schedule, chat, voice and notifications, with the moderation tools to run it. The same server can also give you browser-based SSH and VNC, a file manager and a proxy to your other services.

What you get:

- **Live streaming.** Go live from OBS or any RTMP app, viewers watch in the browser over HLS, every stream is recorded as VODs to your own storage, streams are private unless you make them public, the streamer gets a stream health panel, and a chat overlay puts the stream's chat over the picture in OBS.
- **Chat and voice.** Channels, direct messages, threads, search, reactions, and voice rooms that run between the participants' browsers.
- **Community tools.** Invite codes, roles, moderators, auto-moderation, announcements, anonymous posting, per-channel retention and an audit log.
- **The tools to run it.** Browser terminals and desktops (SSH, VNC and other connection types, 75 in all), a file manager, a proxy for your other web services, backups and system monitoring.

> **Dev/example instance:** [https://portal.dddvm.xyz](https://portal.dddvm.xyz) — a live development server running the latest build.

### Privacy by design

What the software does, and where its limits are:

- **No telemetry.** The portal has no analytics or tracking code and does not report usage to anyone, and you need no account on someone else's platform to run it.
- **Your server holds the data.** Messages, files and the database are on the machine you run it on. VOD recordings go to the SFTP storage the streamer configures, and a stream goes to Twitch, YouTube, Kick or another destination only if its owner sets up a relay. Chat and direct messages are encrypted at rest and the web interface is HTTPS only (publishing over plain RTMP, which is optional, is not encrypted), but nothing is **end-to-end encrypted**: the portal server, and so its administrator, can read chat, and the search index keeps the words of messages in readable form.
- **Private by default where it matters.** A stream is private until its owner makes it public. The health numbers of a live stream (bitrate, relay and recorder state) can be read only by its owner and the administrators, and they are kept in memory only and gone at a restart. Only the streamer who owns a VOD can play it in the portal; not even an administrator can play another member's. Who follows a stream is never listed, viewer counts are a number and nothing more, and a member's saved messages are visible only to that member.
- **The chat overlay link is a secret with a narrow reach.** It is a random token stored only as a SHA-256 hash and carried in the part of the address after the `#`, which a browser never sends to a server. It reaches a read-only view of one stream's chat and nothing else, and the content security policy of the overlay page allows no other host. It ends when the owner revokes or regenerates it, and when their password is changed or reset. What the overlay shows is broadcast to everyone who can see the video, also for a stream that is not public (see the limits below).
- **Notifications that say little.** A phone notification never carries message text, a sender's name or a stream title, and no alert leaves the machine until a superadmin sets up a webhook for it.
- **You decide how long things are kept.** Chat, direct messages, notifications, logs and recordings have retention settings, a channel can have its own, and moderators can prune a date range. Backup archives made with **Include member uploads** (off by default) keep their own copies of uploaded files until those archives are pruned, so deleting a file or a message does not remove it from them.
- **The optional Web Browser is not private from the server.** It hides the member's address from the sites visited, not from the portal: the portal keeps no destination log, but its administrators have root on the server and could watch a running browser, and the picture passes through the portal process (and Cloudflare, when you use it). Off by default; see [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side).
- **Anonymous posting has limits.** Ordinary members never learn who wrote an anonymous post, but moderators, administrators and the author can see it.
- **What does reach other hosts.** The pages' own scripts and styles all come from your server, including the libraries (hls.js for playback, Chart.js, xterm.js, highlight.js, marked, DOMPurify and noVNC, kept under `static/vendor/`; before 1.24.1 they came from the jsDelivr CDN). Voice calls ask a STUN server for each participant's address, and the default is Google's (change it with `STUN_SERVER` in `.env`). Phone notifications pass through your browser maker's push service. A link in a chat message can make a member's browser contact the linked site (preview image, YouTube embed), and the server fetches the page for the preview. With the optional Web Browser (off by default, 1.25.0) the server fetches every page a member opens, sends the name lookups of those pages to Cloudflare's DNS-over-HTTPS resolver at `1.1.1.1`, and uses DuckDuckGo as the home page and search engine; the sites see the server's address. An administrator's update check calls the GitHub API (and a daily check runs only if an update alert is set up), and the Cloudflare address list is refreshed weekly unless you switch that off. See [Security](#security) for the full list.
- **Limits you should know.** An administrator of the instance is effectively root on that host. A web application that an administrator proxies runs on the portal's own address with the sessions of the people who open it, so proxy only applications you trust. If you put Cloudflare or another proxy in front, it terminates TLS and can see the traffic. Your hosting provider can read what is on the disk. Portal records the IP address and browser of sign-ins in its security log, keeps the last address of each session and the addresses a member signed in from for 30 days, its device metrics keep the remote addresses connected to the host (14 days by default), and its activity feed keeps up to 500 events (`retention_activity_max`) that carry the address of the member's action. A chat overlay shows the stream's chat to everyone who can see the video, also when the stream is not public and its chat on the portal is closed to members, because the owner chose to put it on their own broadcast. It is not an anonymity tool and makes no promise to protect anyone from the operator of the server, from a host provider or from a legal demand served on either.

**Is it for you?** It suits a creator, a club, a gaming group or a small team that wants its own stream and chat on hardware it chooses, and is willing to run a server. It is not the right tool if you need messaging that the server operator cannot read, or if you do not want to maintain a server.

## Why This Exists

Most of the tools a community uses to stream, chat and talk are run by someone else. That means someone else holds your messages, your recordings and your audience, and sets the rules. If they change their terms, raise prices or shut down, you lose what you built there.

Open Relay Portal puts a streaming server, chat, voice and the tools to moderate them on a server you own, so the data and the rules are yours. It is one Python process and one SQLite file, with no Docker required, and the same server can also give you remote access to your own machines.

### For communities
- **Content creators** who want to own their stream, their recordings and their audience
- **Friend groups and gaming communities** who want a private space they control
- **Clubs, teams and organizations** that want internal chat, voice and streaming without handing the data to a third party

### For people with higher privacy needs
- **Journalists, activists, organizers and NGOs** can use a server they host themselves, so chat and recordings are not held by a chat or streaming platform. That is all the software provides. It does not hide who you are from the server's operator or your hosting provider, it is not end-to-end encrypted, and whether it is safe for your situation is a judgement you have to make with your own threat model

### For infrastructure operators
- **Homelab enthusiasts** who want one portal to manage a setup from anywhere
- **Small businesses** that need remote access to internal services without maintaining a VPN
- **IT teams** who want browser-based SSH, VNC and database access with centralized authentication

## What You Get

### Live Streaming
Broadcast video to your community — like having your own private TV channel. Stream from OBS or any RTMP-compatible app, with support for hardware-accelerated encoding (NVENC, AMF, x264). Viewers watch in their browser over HLS. Every stream is automatically recorded as VOD clips and uploaded to your own storage, and you can watch your own recordings in the browser (Play in My VODs) without downloading them. Relay your stream simultaneously to Twitch, YouTube, Kick, or any custom RTMP destination. The stream settings open a health panel (bitrate with a small graph, uptime, source and codecs, relay and recorder state, warnings) and make a link for a chat overlay, a transparent page you add to OBS as a browser source. You keep full control — no platform cut, no algorithm deciding who sees your content, no surprise policy changes.

Streams are private unless you make them public. The chat of a stream that is not public can be read, joined and written to only by its owner and the admins, and its channel is left out of everyone else's channel list. **Rotate Share Link** in the stream settings replaces the viewing link, so old links, embeds and open watch pages stop working; making a public stream private rotates it for you. Private means private for the video as well: the playlist, segments, thumbnail, WebRTC playback and the info route all use one rule. The owner and the admins may always watch, a signed-in member may watch a public stream, and somebody who is not signed in may watch only a public stream whose **Allow unauthenticated access** switch is on, with its current share key. Everything else answers the same "Stream not found", so a private stream's existence and live state are not revealed. Viewing keys never appear in notifications, webhooks, cards or logs. **Allow unauthenticated access** is a separate switch that shows the video on the public live page and lets the share link play without a login.

**Following and go-live notices.** A member follows a stream from the streams page, the watch page or the bell beside a stream channel in chat, and can follow only a stream they may watch. Who follows whom is private: you see your own follows, the owner of a stream sees only a number of followers, and no one, admins included, gets a list of names. Making a stream private removes every follow of it. When a stream goes live, the owner's **Who is told** setting decides the audience: **followers** (the default for a new stream) or **everyone** who may watch it. Members in the audience get an in-portal notification, never the streamer. A stream is announced once per 10 minutes, however often the encoder reconnects, and the owner can write a **go-live message** of up to 200 characters with `{streamer}`, `{title}` and `{url}`; empty uses "{title} just went live".

**Schedule.** A stream's owner can schedule slots (one-off or weekly, in their own time zone, so a weekly slot keeps its local time across daylight saving); members who may watch the stream see a countdown on the offline watch page and an Upcoming strip on the Streams page, can mark a slot Interested and get one reminder 10 minutes before it starts, and can put the schedules they follow into a calendar application through a personal read-only link (User settings). A slot is visible exactly to those who may watch the stream: for a public stream every signed-in member who is not banned from it, for a stream that is not public only its owner and the administrators; nobody else sees an entry, a count or a reminder for it. The reminder says "<streamer> starts in 10 minutes" and nothing more for a public stream, and "A stream you are interested in starts in 10 minutes" for a stream that is not public, in the notification list, the desktop alert and the phone notification alike. The calendar link contains a secret token: proxies and servers between your calendar application and the portal can log it, so treat it like a password and use **Regenerate** or **Revoke** in User settings if it may have leaked. See the [notes for 1.23.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1230).

**Cards and the live strip.** A go-live also posts a card (the streamer, the title and a Watch button) into the stream's own chat. An admin can pick one **announce channel** (Admin > Settings > User Permissions) that receives the card of every public stream; it must be a channel every member can read. A card is removed from every channel when its stream becomes private, and is never posted for a private stream into a channel members can read. The chat page has a **Live now** strip above the messages with the streams that are live and that you may watch. Go-live notices to the outside (the `stream.live` webhook) carry the watch address and message but no stream key.

**Viewer counts, title and game.** The number of viewers on a stream card, in the live list and on the watch page is counted by the portal: a member is a viewer while their player passes the portal's playback check, and one account is one viewer however many tabs or devices it uses. Nothing is kept about who is watching, only a number. Somebody who reads the stream straight from the RTMPS or RTSP port is not counted. When a stream ends, its owner sees the highest count and the number of different viewers of that session as "Last session" in the stream settings; nobody else gets those figures. A stream can have a **game** of up to 60 characters, set when you create the stream or in its settings, with suggestions from the games you used before and those of public streams. It is shown with the title on cards, on the watch page and in go-live cards, and can be used as `{game}` in the go-live message. If you change the title or game while you are live, the watch page, the streams page player and the Live now strip update without a reload.

**Stream health and live controls.** The stream's owner (and an administrator) gets a **Stream health** button on the watch page, and a link to it in the stream settings, that opens a small panel: uptime, the relays and what state each is in, the recorder with the number of finished chunks still waiting to upload, the server's load, and warnings the server works out over a window so one late reading does not flap them (a relay failing, the recording stopped or refused by the disk guard, and, when the numbers exist, no data for 75 seconds and a bitrate that fell by more than half). Error texts are one of a few fixed sentences, never a destination address, URL, key or path. Nobody else can read it, a stream moderator included (the answer is the same "not found" as for an unknown stream), and nothing is stored: it lives in the server's memory and is gone at a restart. **Where the ingest numbers come from, and how coarse they are:** the panel's bitrate, graph, source and codecs, and the no-data and bitrate warnings, are worked out from MediaMTX's list of paths. The check that already asks MediaMTX every 30 seconds whether it is running reads that list, and the first page of its answer is reused for these numbers; the portal asks for further pages only when MediaMTX reports more than one (one call each, at most 10 pages in all), so with a short list there is no call of its own (the five-second viewer tick still asks MediaMTX nothing). A reading is therefore at most one every 30 seconds, so the graph has one point per 30 seconds, a stream shows no bitrate until its second reading (up to a minute after it went live), and a stopped encoder is reported after 75 seconds without growth of the received-bytes counter, not at once (the panel says so too). While MediaMTX is not running or does not answer, there are no readings. Administrators get a **Live streams** tab (Admin) listing live streams with owner, uptime, viewers, relays and recording, and two actions per stream, each asking first and each written to the audit log: **Stop relays** (the relays stay stopped until the stream ends) and **End now** (disconnects the encoder through MediaMTX, optionally replaces the stream key so it cannot reconnect with the old one, and ends the session the normal way; recordings are finished and uploaded, never deleted, and the stream chat is cleared as at any stream end). Details are in the [notes for 1.24.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1240).

**Chat controls on the watch page.** A stream owner can appoint up to 20 **stream mods** in the stream settings (Chat Moderators). The owner and the mods carry a Streamer or Mod badge and, in that stream's chat only, can pin a message (it shows as a banner above the chat, with an Unpin button for them), time out a member, delete messages, start and close polls, ban and unban, and set **slow mode** (off, 5, 15 or 30 seconds, from a selector in the chat header; they are not held back by it). A mod cannot act on the owner, another mod or site staff, and the owner cannot act on site staff. Appointing a mod does not let that person watch or read a private stream. A member who is banned from a stream can no longer watch it while signed in: playback answers "Stream not found" and the watch page says they are banned. Anybody who may read the chat can vote in its polls. Every one of these actions is written to the audit log.

**Watching your recordings and keeping the disk safe.** In My VODs the **Play** button on a chunk opens a player page that plays that chunk, and **Play whole session** plays all chunks of a recording as one continuous video with seeking across the whole session. Nothing is re-encoded (the portal repackages the chunk, H.264 video with AAC or MP3 audio plays, anything else is download only), and the portal keeps a size-capped playback cache on its own disk. Only the streamer who owns a VOD can play it: another member, and an administrator, get the same "not found" as for a recording that does not exist. A recording is stopped, or refused, when the disk that holds the waiting chunks is nearly full or too many chunks of one streamer wait for upload; the stream itself carries on, My VODs says what happened, and an administrator can be alerted. Chunks left over after a restart are delivered under new numbers and never replace a file on your storage. The limits are set under Admin > Settings > Data Retention. Details and what an administrator should know before upgrading are in the [notes for 1.21.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1210).

**Maintenance mode and announcements.** An administrator can put one service or one connection in maintenance (Edit service, Edit connection: a box, an optional end time and a short message): a member who opens it sees "Down for maintenance until 20:00" with the message and a countdown instead of an error, and the administrator still gets through. A superadmin can also switch on a global maintenance mode under Admin > Settings that does the same for everything reached through `/proxy`, the embedded browser and `/search`; sign-in, the admin pages, the API, chat and every other page keep working, and `server.py maintenance-off` clears it from the command line. Administrators can also publish announcements (info, warning or outage, with an optional start and end) that show as a banner at the top of every page until the member dismisses them or they end; the text is never turned into markup apart from bold, italic, code, http and https links and line breaks, and it is not shown on the sign-in page unless "Show before sign-in" is ticked. Details and what to know before upgrading are in the [notes for 1.22.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1220).

**Reference IDs on block and rate-limit pages.** A visitor who is blocked (a manual block, a failed-sign-in ban or a scanner block) or who hits a rate limit sees a short reference such as `ABCD-EFGH` and one sentence, "Give this reference to an administrator.", instead of a bare error; the page never says why, which rule or whether the block is permanent. An administrator pastes the reference into Admin > System > Network > Reference lookup, sees the stored event and whether the block is still active, and can unblock from there. Details are in the [notes for 1.22.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1220).

**Organising the dashboard.** Each member files the connections they can reach, their own and the ones shared with them, in their own folders and tags; nobody else sees that filing. The My Connections tab has a filter box that narrows the cards as you type (`/` focuses it from anywhere on the page, Enter opens the first match, Escape clears it), tag chips, collapsible folders, a **Recently used** row of the last five opened, a pin on every card and an **Open in** choice per card (default, same tab, new tab or the embedded browser). It works with a keyboard only and has a phone layout. Details are in the [notes for 1.22.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1220).

**Connection health checks.** Optional and off by default: a superadmin switches them on under Admin > Settings > Connection health checks, and a card of a pinned or recently opened connection then shows a small dot (up, down since a time, or unknown). The portal makes a plain TCP connect or a `HEAD` request to the connection's own target every minute or so, through the same refusals as a real connection (loopback, private, link-local and metadata addresses are never contacted), sends no cookie, header or secret, and never follows a redirect. Down and up are alerted once per change, with no name and no host in a third-party format. Details are in the [notes for 1.22.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1220).

**Request IDs and friendly error pages.** Every answer carries a short request ID (`X-Request-ID`) and every log line written while it was handled carries the same ID, so an administrator can find what happened with `journalctl -u portal | grep 'req=abcd2345'`. When a proxied service cannot be reached, the member sees a page with the connection name, that ID and a retry countdown instead of a bare error; it never shows the upstream host, address or error text. Details are in the [notes for 1.22.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1220).

**Chat overlay for OBS.** In the stream settings the owner can make an overlay link for the stream's chat: add it to OBS as a Browser source and the chat of that stream is drawn on a transparent background over the picture, newest line at the bottom, each line fading after a time you can set. The link carries a secret token that reaches one thing, a read-only copy of that one stream's chat as its viewers see it: the name they see (an anonymous post stays "Anonymous"), the text, the time and the owner or moderator badge, nothing else. It is not a login, no other page or socket accepts it, and it needs no cookie. A message that is deleted, moderated, pruned or wiped when the stream goes offline disappears from the overlay. Text is drawn as text only: a link in a message is not clickable and nothing in a message is loaded, so OBS never fetches an address because of what a viewer wrote. The owner has one link per stream, can regenerate it (the old one stops at once and its sockets are closed) or revoke it, and the link is shown once when it is made. The link also ends, like the calendar link, when the owner's password is changed or reset or an administrator signs them out everywhere. **Whatever the overlay shows is broadcast to everyone who can see your video**, also when the stream is not public and its chat on the portal is closed to members: the link works anyway, because you chose to put your own chat on your own broadcast. The stream settings also give the address of the compact chat window (`/watch/{id}?chat=only`) for OBS's Custom Browser Docks: a dock is your normal signed-in chat, so it uses the portal sign-in and no overlay token. At most three browser sources can use one link at the same time.

### Chat
Message your team or community in real time. Message bodies are stored encrypted on the server (encryption at rest, with a key kept on the server), but the search index holds the words of each message in readable form so that search works, and the portal itself, and so its administrator, can read the chat: it is not end-to-end encrypted. Organize conversations into channels, reply to specific messages, start threads, react with emoji, @mention people, share images, embed links and videos, and pin important messages. Scroll up to read older messages of a channel, 50 at a time, and jump from a reply, a pin, a search result or a mention straight to that message in context; a **Jump to present** bar brings you back to the newest messages. History reaches back as far as the chat retention keeps messages (7 days by default, set under Data Retention). An administrator can give one channel its own retention (and a shorter one for its uploaded files), and the moderators of a chat can prune a date range from it, with the count shown first. Search across everything with Ctrl+K. Every channel has a **bell menu** (hover the channel): choose All messages, Mentions only, Nothing or the default, or mute it for 1, 8 or 24 hours. The choice is kept on the server, is private to you and follows you to every browser. Channels you have not opened show an unread badge that updates live, a short tone can play for the channels you set to All and for mentions (with quiet hours that you set in your own browser), and desktop alerts reach you when the tab is in the background. Write `@everyone` or `@here` to notify a whole channel, in the channels where the sender is allowed to (staff, stream owners, and every member of a channel where staff switched it on), with a limit of one a minute for ordinary senders. A private channel, a direct message or the chat of a stream that is not public never shows its name or activity to anyone who may not read it. It's a full-featured chat system that lives entirely on your hardware.

### Direct Messages
Have private conversations with one person or a small group (up to 10 people), stored encrypted on the server like the rest of the chat. Supports reactions, replies, message editing, typing indicators, unread badges, and muting — everything you'd expect from a modern messaging experience, delivered instantly over WebSocket. If someone is offline, they'll see a notification when they return.

### Phone Notifications
Get a notice on your phone or in your browser when a direct message, a mention or a stream you follow needs you, even with the portal closed. Switch it on per device under **User settings > Phone notifications**, choose which of the three kinds you want, see your devices and remove any of them. On an iPhone or iPad install the portal to the home screen first (iOS 16.4 or later); on Android and in desktop browsers the browser's own permission prompt is all it needs. A push is sent only when you have no portal tab open, never carries message text, a sender's name or a stream title ("New direct message", "You were mentioned in #channel"), and is encrypted to your device; an anonymous poster is never named. It travels through the push service of your browser's maker (Google, Mozilla, Apple or Microsoft), which can see that a notification was sent to your device and when, and how big it was, but not what it says. Administrators can switch the feature off, add a push service, send a test push to their own devices and replace the push key in **Admin > Settings > Push notifications**. Nothing is sent to anyone who has not enabled it. Details and what an administrator should know before upgrading are in the [notes for 1.19.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1190).

### Saved Messages and Reminders
Right-click a message (or focus it with the arrow keys and press the menu key; on a phone or tablet tap the small **...** button that every message shows) and choose **Save message**; **Remind me...** saves it and asks when: in 1 hour, tomorrow 9:00, next week (Monday 9:00) or a date and time of your choosing, all in your own time zone. The **Saved** button above the chat lists what you saved with the newest first, with Jump to message, Remind me and Remove, and a saved message carries a small "Saved" marker that only you see. At the time you chose you get one notification, "Reminder for a saved message" (and a phone notification if you use them), that opens the message in context for a channel message; a reminder for a direct message opens the Saved view, where Jump to message opens the conversation, not the message in context (there is no per-message jump for direct messages). Reminders have no per-kind push switch: a phone notification follows the general push rules (push on, a registered device, no portal tab open). Only you can see what you saved: no other member, not even the author, and no administrator, sees an entry or a count. The portal keeps a reference to the message, never a copy of it, so the list shows what you can read right now: a message that was deleted or aged out, or in a channel you were removed from, shows only "No longer available" and the time you saved it. An anonymous post stays anonymous there. You can keep up to 500 saved messages and 100 waiting reminders, up to a year ahead. A reminder cannot be set for a message that is no longer available. Details are in the [notes for 1.23.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1230).

### Message Search
Find anything across your channels and DMs with full-text search (the Search button or Ctrl+K). Narrow it with operators: `from:name`, `in:channel`, `has:link`, `has:file`, `has:image`, `is:pinned`, `before:2026-11-01`, `after:2026-11-01` and "quoted phrases"; `from:` and `in:` can be negated with a leading `-`. Suggestion chips under the search box offer the operators, channel names and people in the room. A search made only of operators lists up to the 200 newest matches, and clicking a result opens that message in context.

You only find what you are allowed to read. A private channel you are not in, a stream chat you may not read and other people's DMs never match, and they look exactly like channels that do not exist. A search never reveals who wrote an anonymous post. Messages older than the chat retention are deleted and cannot be found.

### Voice Chat
Talk to people in real time. Choose between push-to-talk or automatic voice detection. Audio is encrypted between the participants (WebRTC DTLS-SRTP) and travels directly between their browsers; it is not stored, and it does not pass through the portal server unless you configure a TURN relay, which then carries the still-encrypted audio. The server carries only the call signalling, and the participants' browsers learn each other's network addresses.
A dropped connection (a Wi-Fi change, a brief outage) keeps your seat in the call for 20 seconds so you can rejoin it without starting over; a second tab of the same account cannot take it over.
The voice settings window (the Voice settings button next to the voice controls in chat) lets you pick your microphone and, in browsers that allow it, your speaker, shows a live level meter with the voice-detection threshold on it, can play your own microphone back to you as a test, and can keep the microphone open for a moment after you release the push-to-talk key. These choices stay in your own browser.
Right-click or long-press a name in the voice bar or the online list to set that person's volume (0 to 200%) or mute them for yourself. Like the voice settings, this is stored only in your own browser and never sent to the server.
While you are in a call, each other person's tile shows 1 to 4 small **connection-quality bars** (green is good, red is poor) from the browser's own round-trip, loss and jitter figures; hover for the numbers and whether the path is direct or relayed. Nothing about them is sent to the server.

Voice rooms hold at most 8 people by default (a superadmin sets 2 to 25 under Admin > Settings). Moderators (staff, and in a stream's own chat its owner and stream mods) can lock a room, server-mute a member or kick one from the member's menu; a timed-out or muted member cannot join and is taken out of the call. Server mute is cooperative and a kick is enforced against normal clients only: it cannot cut an existing direct link of a modified client. A penalty (timeout or mute) in a channel also takes the member out of its voice room; the one exception is that a timeout that a stream owner or stream mod placed through an anonymous post does not end a running call (the author is refused at their next join). See the [1.20.0 notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1200).

### Moderation and Anonymous Posting
Moderators and admins can give a channel a **slow mode** of 5, 10 or 30 seconds, or 1, 2 or 5 minutes, from its context menu; staff are exempt, and editing a message does not count as sending one. In a stream's own chat the slow mode is set from the watch page instead, to 0, 5, 15 or 30 seconds, by the stream owner, its mods and staff. **Stream mods.** In a stream's own chat the owner and the **stream mods** the owner appointed (see Live Streaming) moderate with the same tools, in that chat only: pin, time out (from the watch page for 1 minute up to a day), delete, ban, unban, polls and slow mode. A stream mod has no power in another stream's chat or in a general channel, and a refused action always gets the same "Not allowed" answer. Moderators and admins keep their site-wide powers, and an administrator can lift any stream ban, including one on staff.

The **Prune** button of a channel header (administrators, global moderators and, in a stream's own chat, its owner and stream mods) removes the messages of a date range, optionally of one member, keeping pinned messages unless you include them. It shows a **dry-run count** first and removes nothing until you confirm; for named posts it only removes what the same person could delete one message at a time, and it is audited. An anonymous post is never named in the dialog, the frames or the audit entry, a user filter never matches one, and anonymous posts follow their own rule: a moderator's range prune removes anonymous posts whoever wrote them, while an administrator's range prune leaves them in place whenever the rank rules exclude any author, for example another administrator (see the [1.20.0 notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1200) for the rules). In voice, moderators can kick, server-mute and lock (see Voice Chat above).

**Auto-moderation** rules (Admin > Auto-Mod) filter words (contains, exact word or regex), links (a block list of domains, or all links), excessive capitals and mention spam. A matching message is blocked and its author is told which rule stopped it; a rule can also time the author out or mute them. Edited messages are checked too, regex rules run in a separate time-limited process, and staff are stopped by a rule but never timed out or muted by it. Every trigger is recorded in the Auto-Mod action log (View Action Log).

Members can switch on **Hide my username** in the chat sidebar to post anonymously. Ordinary members never receive the account, role, nickname or avatar behind an anonymous post: not in history, search, reactions, reply previews, polls or webhooks. Moderators, admins and the author still see who wrote it, so anonymous does not mean unaccountable, and a stream owner, a stream mod or staff can ban or time out an anonymous author by message, without being shown who it is.

### Private Search
Search the web without the trackers. Portal can run [SearXNG](https://github.com/searxng/searxng) — a privacy-respecting metasearch engine that aggregates results from 70+ engines while stripping the profiling — as an optional managed service, reachable at `/search/` by logged-in users. No search history, no cookies handed to upstream engines, no ads. The SearXNG UI is re-skinned to your portal theme and carries the portal navbar, so it feels like a native page. The dashboard has a unified search bar with a **Portal** / **Internet** toggle — Portal searches your indexed chat history, Internet runs a private web search. It's a normal managed service: enable it from the Services panel to turn it on, disable it and the portal carries on exactly as before (the search bar quietly drops the Internet option). A JSON API (`/search/search?q=...&format=json`) is available for automation; scripts authenticate with a personal API key (`Authorization: Api-Key portal_...`), and any valid key can search.

### Web Browser (server-side)
A browser that runs on the portal's own server and reaches the member as a picture, at `/rbrowser` and from the dashboard card **Web Browser (on this server)**. It is for sites the embedded browser cannot handle (heavy JavaScript, sites that refuse to be framed) and for looking at the internet from the server's address instead of your own. It is **optional and off by default**: an install that does nothing gets no new process, package, mount, firewall rule or unit, the dashboard card is not drawn, and `/rbrowser` says the browser is not available.

> The Web Browser runs on the server, not on your device. Websites see the server's address, not yours. The portal has no function that records, logs or stores the addresses you open, what you type, or what is on the screen, and it keeps no history: the browser's profile lives in memory and is destroyed when the session ends. The portal does log that you started and ended a session, when, and how much data it moved; administrators can see that you have a session and whether it is active. This is not protection from the people who run this portal: its administrators have full access to the server and could observe a running browser there, including sites you are signed in to. The picture of the browser travels through the portal process and through Cloudflare's network like every other page of the portal (encrypted in transit, readable at those two points). Name lookups go to a DNS-over-HTTPS resolver (Cloudflare's 1.1.1.1), which sees the names of the sites opened from the server. Do not use it for anything you would not open on a computer that belongs to someone else.

That is the same text the page shows under **About this browser**. In plain terms, and checked against the code:

- **Pages are fetched by the server.** A site sees the server's address and location, not the member's, and a fresh browser profile every time (more CAPTCHAs, a sign-in on every visit, and some sites refuse data-centre addresses). The server's address is the operator's: every member browses from it, and a site that trusts that address trusts all of them.
- **Name lookups go to Cloudflare.** The resolver is DNS over HTTPS to `1.1.1.1`, fixed in the code (it is not a setting). The home page and the address-bar search are DuckDuckGo (`https://duckduckgo.com/`); the home page can be changed, the search engine cannot.
- **What the portal keeps.** No destination log, no page titles, no key codes, no pointer positions, no clipboard text and no picture of a session, in the database, the logs or the security events. It keeps that a session started and ended (the activity log: who, when, how many minutes, why it ended) and, in its log file, how many bytes the picture and the browser's internet traffic moved. Because no destination is kept, **two sessions that run at the same time cannot be attributed to one member in an abuse report**; an operator who needs attribution sets the number of sessions to 1.
- **What an administrator sees.** An administrator sees that a session exists (member, start time, active or idle, whether a window is attached, memory, CPU time, data in and out) and can end it. The portal has no function that lets anybody watch, join or take over a session: only the browser sign-in that created it can attach. But every administrator of this portal has a root shell on the server through the web terminal, so in practice administrators could observe a running browser, including sites a member is signed in to.
- **The picture** travels over the portal's own WebSocket (`/ws/rbrowser/{id}`), which accepts the browser sign-in only (no API key, no bearer token), through Cloudflare when you use it, like every other page.
- **Not anonymity, and not end-to-end encryption.** The portal and its operator see everything the member sees. The member's own address is hidden from the sites visited, not from the portal.

**What it needs.** Linux with systemd 249 or newer and cgroup v2, the portal running as root (it starts the units), Chromium from the distribution (a snap does not work), `xvfb`, `x11vnc`, `xauth`, `x11-xserver-utils` (for `xrandr`), `xsel` and `python3`, a kernel that lets the portal load a small BPF filter, and unprivileged user namespaces (Chromium's own sandbox needs them; `--no-sandbox` is never used). `iptables` and `ip6tables` are optional and give only the upload limit. The admin card lists what is missing and prints the `apt-get` line; the setup wizard offers the install (step 10, default No).

**How to switch it on** (Admin > Settings, the card **Web Browser on the server**; every administrator can read it, only a superadmin signed in with the password can save):
1. Install the packages: the setup wizard offers it as an optional step (default No; re-running the wizard on an existing install rewrites the configuration from your answers, with a backup, so there run the `apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends ...` line the admin card shows instead). Nothing is installed without you.
2. Read the card: **This server** says whether the host can run it and why not, with the Chromium version (it warns when the binary is older than 45 days; the browser does not update itself, so keep Chromium and the kernel current).
3. Tick **Switched on** and save. The portal prepares the host (two small memory-backed areas under `/run`, a resource slice, the BPF filter and, with `iptables`, one firewall chain) and runs the **sandbox check** in the background: a probe unit with the same limits as a session tries the containment from the inside (blocked networks, listening sockets, hidden paths, mounts, the display, Chromium's policy and, since 1.26.2, that every renderer of a headless Chromium runs in Chromium's own namespace sandbox). **No session can start until the check has passed**; **Run check** repeats it (it can take about 40 seconds), and a setting that changes the unit makes it run again.
4. Choose **Who may use it** (administrators only until you change it) and the limits below. The dashboard card appears for members who may use it.

**Settings** (ten keys, all under `rbrowser_`; the superadmin saves them in the card, `PUT /api/admin/rbrowser`): `enabled` (off), `min_role` (`admin`; `moderator` or `user` widen it), `max_sessions` (2; 1 to 8), `idle_minutes` (15; 5 to 120), `memory_mb` (2048 per session; 1024 to 4096), `cpu_percent` (150 per session; 50 to 400), `clipboard` (`both`; `in` or `off`), `home_url` (`https://duckduckgo.com/`; an https address of at most 300 characters), `upload_kbps` (4000; 0 for no kernel rule, or 500 to 50000) and `extra_deny` (empty; at most 32 networks in CIDR form). The seats that really exist are `min(max_sessions, max(1, memory / 3 / memory_mb))`, and the CPU per session is also held to what the host has beyond one and a half CPUs, so a small host gets fewer: with the defaults a host needs about 12 GB of memory for two sessions. The card shows the effective numbers. A change never alters a running unit; switching the feature off, or raising **Who may use it** above a member, ends the sessions affected.

**Using it.** The page starts a session (one per member, a few seconds) and shows the browser; **Fit**, **Quality** (Low, Balanced, Sharp), **Fullscreen**, **Keyboard** (a phone's on-screen keyboard), **Text** (shown where `clipboard` is not `off`: type a block of text into the browser, up to 5000 characters at a time; with `clipboard` set to `both` also **Copy from the browser**, plain text, at most 262144 bytes), **About this browser** and **End session**. A session ends when you end it, when the last browser tab in it is closed, after the idle minutes without a key or pointer move (a warning with **I am here** comes first), 5 minutes after the last window left, 4 hours after it started, when you sign out, when an administrator ends it, switches the feature off or raises **Who may use it** above you, when the portal restarts or stops, or when it uses up its data allowance (512 MiB sent or 8 GiB received, or a rate far above the upload limit or 40 Mbit/s received over a minute; a warning shows at 80%). A second sign-in of the same member (another device) cannot attach to a running session: it is offered to end it and start anew. Starting is limited to 6 sessions per 10 minutes per member.

**Theme.** The remote Chromium takes the member's portal theme when a session starts: the page sends the colour of its own toolbar and whether the theme is light or dark, and nothing else (no theme name). Chromium's tab strip then takes that colour, its toolbar row and menus a tint of the same hue, and it picks light or dark text for it; a dark theme also makes Chromium's own menus and settings dark and lets pages see `prefers-color-scheme: dark`, while a light theme leaves Chromium at its light default. A page's own content is never recoloured. The theme is fixed for the life of a session: a theme change applies to the next session. It is cosmetic and never stops a session from starting: a value that is not a six-digit colour or exactly `dark` or `light` is ignored. The colour reaches Chromium only as the policy `BrowserThemeColor`, in a small read-only file of its own beside the security policy, which holds no other key.

**What it does not do.** No downloads, no uploads (there is no file dialog), no sound, no printing, no camera, microphone or screen capture, no extensions, no developer tools, no page fullscreen, no password manager and no Chromium account or sync (a member can still sign in to a website inside a session, but the profile is thrown away at the end, so the next session starts clean). Windows other than a browser's own windows and ordinary pop-ups are never shown (a file dialog, Document Picture-in-Picture). Safe Browsing is off, so the browser gives no Safe Browsing warnings about known harmful sites. Text goes in as typed keys and dead keys do not compose (type the finished character); input methods for Chinese, Japanese and Korean work through the phone-keyboard path only. The clipboard of the device and the one in the remote browser are not linked: text goes in only where `clipboard` is `both` or `in`, and comes out only where it is `both`. Scrolling text is about 8 frames a second and video is choppy (the picture is capped at 8 Mbit/s and is a video of a browser: every key and pointer move has latency). A destination in a blocked range gives Chromium's blocked page at once when it is named as an address (`localhost`, a metadata address), and otherwise a connection that times out, because the kernel drops the packets. **At very small window sizes a page's drop-down list can touch the toolbar area, so the remote address bar is not a security indicator there; it never was one** (the same rule as the embedded browser's address bar). The search bar of the dashboard uses SearXNG; the address bar of the Web Browser searches DuckDuckGo.

**How it is contained** (the short version; see [Security](#security) for the limits):
- Every session is its own **transient systemd unit** in a resource slice, with a **dynamic user** that exists only for the session, no ambient capabilities, a read-only system, no `/home`, hidden configuration and storage paths, and memory, CPU, task and I/O limits.
- A **kernel egress deny list** (`IPAddressDeny`) blocks the loopback, private, link-local, carrier-grade NAT, multicast and reserved ranges of IPv4 and IPv6 (including the mapped, NAT64, Teredo and 6to4 forms that can carry an IPv4 address), the cloud metadata addresses, the host's own addresses and the networks they sit on, its gateways and DNS servers, and your `extra_deny` list. Over IP the browser cannot reach the server's own services or your local network (the Security list names what the unit shares with the host besides IP).
- An **ingress BPF filter** drops the first packet of every incoming TCP connection to the unit, so nothing in it can accept a connection; a check after the start and another every 30 seconds look for a listener anyway.
- A **managed Chromium policy** (blocked file, download, extension, sign-in, print, capture and device features, DNS over HTTPS only) is written into the unit; Chromium's **own sandbox stays on**.
- The member's input passes an **RFB client-message filter** in the portal: only six message types, whole and bounded, and nothing that could reach the display server's clipboard; anything else closes the connection and writes a security event.
- Everything the browser writes (profile, caches, `/tmp`) is in memory-backed areas of a fixed size, gone when the session ends. `/tmp` and `/var/tmp` are one **shared 32 MiB area for all sessions**; a session that takes more than 12 MiB of it is ended.

**Set-up steps worth doing.** Add the server's public address to `extra_deny` when it is not on one of its interfaces (behind a NAT, for example), so the browser cannot connect back to the server's services through it; add the addresses of any service that allow-lists the server, because every member then browses as that address. Set `kernel.yama.ptrace_scope=1` and keep the kernel and Chromium updated: the feature deliberately feeds web content from the internet to your machine, and Chromium's sandbox needs user namespaces, so a browser exploit that also escaped the sandbox would land in a locked-down unit but on your kernel. A portal update or restart ends every running session (the page says why). The card's **Kernel upload limit** line says whether the upload rule is in force (it needs `iptables` and `ip6tables`; without them the browser's upload is still budgeted but not shaped).

### Data Retention
Set policies for how long chat messages, DMs, notifications, activity logs, security events, session recordings, and expired tokens are kept. Automatic cleanup runs on a schedule (default every 6 hours) with optional database compaction. Run cleanup on demand anytime from the admin panel. An administrator can also give one chat channel its own retention in days, and its uploaded files a shorter one, from the **Retention** window of the channel header (an empty field follows the global setting; a shorter value needs a confirmation that shows how many messages and files the next cleanup would remove). The recordings folder has a total size budget (`recording_max_total_mb`, default 2048 MB, `0` for none).

### Running the Server: Remote Access, Files, Services and Administration
The parts below are for running the portal and reaching your own machines: remote access, files, services, game servers, metrics, themes, administration, access control and backups.

### Remote Access
Control other computers from your browser — no special software needed on your end. Connect to SSH terminals, VNC desktops, SPICE consoles, databases, and more — all through a secure WebSocket connection. 75 connection types are supported across 17 categories, each with a setup guide. A Quick Add bar lets you create common connections (SSH, VNC, MySQL, PostgreSQL, Proxmox, HTTP Proxy) with one click. Each member files connections in their own folders and tags (also ones shared with them), finds them with the filter box (`/` focuses it, Enter opens the first match) and sees the last five opened under Recently used.

On a phone or tablet the web terminal shows a **key bar** above the on-screen keyboard: Esc, Tab, sticky Ctrl and Alt (tap once, then type the next key), arrow keys, `|`, `~`, `/`, Paste and text size buttons. Pasting more than one line asks for confirmation first, and when the browser will not let the page read the clipboard a text box takes the paste instead. The bar is shown by default only on touch screens; the keyboard button in the terminal header turns it on or off and the choice is remembered in the browser.

SSH and SFTP connections verify the remote server's host key. The first time you connect, Portal shows the key's fingerprint (SHA256) instead of connecting; compare it with the server's, then choose **Accept this key**. If the key later changes, the connection is refused until the connection's owner accepts the new one. Portal sends a remote host only the credentials stored on the connection, never the server account's own SSH keys or agent. The connection form has a **Do not verify host key** option; it is off by default and not recommended.

**Session recording (SSH).** An administrator can switch on **Record sessions** on an SSH connection of their own; the session's output (never keystrokes; what the remote side echoes back, such as typed commands, is output and is recorded) is then saved as an asciicast file, and the terminal shows **This session is recorded** to whoever is using it, before anything is captured. Recordings can contain secrets that were shown on screen, so only administrators can list, play, download or delete them, in the **Recordings** tab of the admin page (every play, download and delete is written to the audit log, and a disk budget keeps the folder in check). It is off for every connection until it is switched on. Details are in the [1.20.0 notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1200).

For web-based services, Portal includes a built-in embedded browser with tabbed browsing and keyboard shortcuts (Ctrl+T for new tab, Ctrl+W to close, Alt+arrows for back/forward). Browse web dashboards, admin panels, or any website through Portal's secure proxy. Every user gets a default **Web Browser** connection; its home page is Portal's own private search (SearXNG) when that service is enabled, otherwise DuckDuckGo. From the private-search home, clicking a result opens it in a new tab *inside* the embedded browser (multi-site connections) or in a real browser tab (single-site connections). The embedded browser is session-aware — log out anywhere and any open browser window returns to the login page. Add it from the connection form's **Web Browser (private search)** preset. The address bar of the embedded browser is a convenience, not a security indicator: the portal marks an address it could not confirm in two cases (a page that reports an address on another site than the one the portal requested, and a page that does not answer the portal (an image, plain text, a file, or a page that stops the portal's script)), but a hostile page may still find a way to show a wrong address, so check a site's identity by opening it in a real browser tab before entering a password. For general browsing, the optional [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side) (1.25.0) runs a real Chromium on the server instead; the embedded browser stays for proxied applications and this release does not change it. No VPN required — if you can reach your portal, you can reach everything behind it.

### Sharing Access
Give another Portal user access to a connection you set up — click **Share** on the connection card, type their username (multiple people allowed), and they'll see it on their own dashboard and can use it through the embedded browser, web shell, xterm or VNC. They can't edit or delete it, and access is revoked the moment you remove them: their open sessions on it are closed too. A web connection opens for the people it is shared with in an isolated sandbox that cannot act as them in the portal (they are asked once before it first opens), unless it is an administrator's own connection, which runs on the portal's own address as each person who opens it; the share dialog says which ([notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1241)). A share can end by itself: the dialog asks how long it lasts (30 days preselected; 1 hour, 8 hours, 1 day, 7 days, 90 days or until you revoke it), the card shows the time left, and **All my shares** lists every share with who gave it, when and until when. Admins can likewise grant a non-admin user the ability to **start / stop / restart**, **view logs**, or (for Portal-deployed game servers) **browse and edit the server's files and download backups** — jailed to that server's own directory tree — for a single managed service — no full admin rights needed, and a grant can carry an end time just like a share (an admin sees every grant and share on the portal under **All grants on the portal**). Every action they're allowed shows as a labelled button on that service's card on their own dashboard.

An SSH connection with a password can **store the password** (the connection form's "Store password", with "Prompt each time" turned off). The password is kept encrypted, is never shown again to you or to anyone you share the connection with, and is used without asking by the web shell and the file browser, so a person you share it with opens a shell as that account in one click, **without knowing the password**: the share dialog warns you of that. It is sent only to a host whose key you have accepted, and changing the connection's host, port or username removes it. With "Prompt each time" (the default) the terminal asks every time, as before.

### File Management
Browse, upload, download, and edit files on your server or on remote machines through the web. The file manager works with your server's local filesystem (for admins) and with any SFTP connection you've set up. When you have multiple SFTP connections, a dual-pane commander-style view lets you move files between remote machines side by side.

### System Monitoring
See what's running on your server at a glance — active processes, systemd services, network interfaces, and open ports. Start, stop, or restart services. Kill runaway processes. All integrated directly into the admin panel as a tab, so you don't need to open a terminal for routine server management. Portal also watches managed services: after three failed health checks in a row a service counts as down, and after two good ones as up again. A service you stopped on purpose, or a game server stopped for an update, stays quiet. Starting, stopping and restarting from the panel is written to the audit log.

You can also promote any installed systemd unit — a game server, a database, a file share — to a **managed service** of type `systemd`. Portal then shows it alongside MediaMTX and SearXNG with the same start/stop/restart controls, health, and (journal) logs, and reflects the unit's state even when it's changed outside Portal. Portal only ever stops such a unit when you explicitly click Stop — never on its own restart or when you remove the entry.

### Game Servers
Deploy dedicated game servers straight from the admin panel. The **Game Servers** tab has a searchable catalog of ~85 games (Palworld, Valheim, Project Zomboid, Rust, ARK, CS2, Team Fortress 2, Arma 3, DayZ, Satisfactory, 7 Days to Die, and many more — admins can add their own entries too); pick a game, name the instance, and Portal runs the SteamCMD install, generates a systemd unit, and registers it as a managed service. From there you get start/stop/restart, live journal logs, a one-click **SteamCMD update** (with an "already up to date" check), an **editor for the launch arguments and stop signal** (no shell runs them — arguments reach the game verbatim), and a **commander-style file browser** jailed to that server's own directory tree — click the server's card to open it. Navigate the install dir (and, for games like Project Zomboid that store config elsewhere, the config/saves dir), sort the list by name, size, or modified date, right-click a file for a context menu to edit, download, rename, or delete it, upload new files (multi-select or drag-and-drop), or pull a full `.tar.gz` **backup** of the config and save/world files. The editor has syntax highlighting for the config formats you'll actually run into (INI/CFG, JSON, XML, YAML, TOML, Lua, properties). The game's known settings files are pinned at the top for quick access. Everything runs under the same unprivileged account as a hand-rolled setup would — and, as of the same tab's **Storage Locations** card, more than one disk: register additional directories, pick which one new deploys default to, or point an individual deploy at a specific one. No `.env` edit or restart needed to add a second disk once the first is configured.

Each server is a managed service, so you can **grant** a non-admin user `Control`, `Logs`, and/or `Files` — every action they're allowed shows up as a clearly-labelled button on that server's card on their own dashboard (start/stop/restart, view logs, edit config, download a backup), and nothing else. Already running servers via hand-rolled systemd units can be adopted onto this machinery without a reinstall.

Servers with no Linux build, such as Enshrouded, run under Wine in their own prefix. A deploy detects a Windows-only game and sets this up itself, including installing Wine on Debian-based hosts. Only some of these games have been tested; see [Windows-only game servers](docs/WINDOWS-GAME-SERVERS.md) for the limits.

### Device-Wide Metrics
The Metrics tab keeps a persisted, whole-host history — not just Portal's own traffic. Every 60 seconds Portal samples CPU and memory per service/process, every listening port and the connections sitting on it, and which remote IPs are talking to the box (with the ports they're hitting and the process serving them). Charts cover anywhere from 6 hours to 14 days; history survives restarts. Retention is configurable under Settings → Data Retention (default 14 days). Counts are point-in-time samples, so a connection that opens and closes between ticks won't appear.

### Visual Theming
Twenty-eight built-in themes — switch with the palette icon in the navbar. The core set: **Dark** (navy, default), **Black** (OLED), **Grey**, **Slate**, **Light**, and **High Contrast**; accent hues **Blue**, **Purple**, **Cyan**, **Green**, **Red**, **Rose**, **Orange**, **Amber**; developer palettes **Nord**, **Dracula**, **Solarized** (dark & light), **Gruvbox**, **Tokyo Night**; light readers **Sepia** and **Nord Light**; vibrant **Synthwave**, **Matrix**, **Cyberpunk**; and three animated gradients — **Aurora**, **Nebula**, **Ember** (which hold a static mid-blend under `prefers-reduced-motion`). Your preference persists across sessions via localStorage and applies before the first paint so there's no flash. The theme follows you everywhere — the login page, every public page, and the SearXNG search UI, which is re-skinned to the active portal theme and wears the portal navbar.

### Administration
Manage users with a clear role hierarchy — Super Admin, Admin, Moderator, and User. Create invite codes to let people register (daily rotating, single-use, or time-limited). Require two-factor authentication. Issue API keys for automation, scoped to what the script needs, expiring after 90 days by default and optionally bound to the networks they are used from. Manage SSH keys, monitor traffic patterns, and scan for vulnerabilities. Administrators also play, download and delete SSH session recordings in the **Recordings** tab, and nobody else can see that they exist. Administrators put a service or a connection in maintenance and publish announcements, and look up the reference ID a blocked visitor reports (Admin > System > Network > Reference lookup); a superadmin switches the global maintenance mode and the connection health checks on. All administration happens through the admin panel in your browser.

### Sessions
**Dashboard > Profile Settings > Sessions** lists every browser login and token of your account with the browser and system (read from the User-Agent by a small local parser, nothing is looked up online), when it was signed in and last used, and its last IP address. Sign out one device, or choose **Sign out other devices**; a signed-out session loses its chat, voice call and proxied downloads at once. API keys are listed separately and are not touched. Admins can sign a user out everywhere from Manage Users, within the role hierarchy.

### API Keys
Scripts and integrations sign in with a personal **API key**, made in the dashboard's **API Keys** window (shown once, stored only as a hash). The key window asks for what the key may do and where it may be used from, so a key can be as small as the script needs:

- **Scopes.** A scope picker lists every scope the portal checks (`*`, `admin`, `services:read`, `access:*`, plus any a service requires, for administrators). A member who is not an administrator always gets `services:read,access:*`. A key with `*` and no expiry shows a warning.
- **Expiry.** A new key expires in 90 days by default; choose 30, 90, 180 or 365 days or Never (the API takes `expires_days`, 1 to 3650, or `"never"`). An expired key stops working and is cleaned up with the other expired keys.
- **Address binding.** A key can be limited to up to 10 addresses or networks (for example `203.0.113.0/24`). From any other address it answers the same `401` as a wrong key, and the refusal is a security event. Binding relies on the portal seeing real visitor addresses: check that Admin > Overview shows no Proxy Trust warning first.
- **Where it was used.** The key list shows the address the key was created from and the address it was last used from, and gives an administrator's own keys a **Stale** badge when they have not been used for 90 days.
- **Only a sign-in creates keys.** A request authenticated with an API key cannot create another key, so a bound or expiring key cannot be used to make an unbound one.

See the [notes for 1.18.0](#upgrade-notes-for-1180) before upgrading: a script that made keys without an expiry now gets 90-day keys.

### Security Events and Audit Trail
A **security event log** records sign-ins and failures, two-factor events, logouts and session sign-outs, API key, password, role, user and invite changes, IP blocks and unblocks, SSH host key accepts, rate-limit trips and changes to the log's own settings. Each row stores the IP address and user agent it came from. Passwords, codes, tokens and keys are never recorded, and a failed login for a name that is not an account does not store the typed name. Only a superadmin can read it (Admin > Audit Log, with Security and Auth filters and CSV or JSON export), and it is kept 180 days by default (Data Retention). Floods from outsiders are aggregated per source and minute, so they cannot fill the database.

The moderation **audit log** next to it records what staff do: moderation actions, service control, backup downloads and settings changes.

### Address Blocking
Admins can block IP addresses, permanently or for a time. Automatic blocks (the optional scanner blocking) are lockout-safe: they skip loopback, private networks, this host's own addresses, trusted proxies, Cloudflare and a **never-ban list** you edit in Admin > Settings (or set with `NEVER_BAN_CIDRS`), and they never override a manual block. If you lock yourself out, stop the portal and run `server.py unblock <ip>` or `clear-bans`. **Failed sign-in blocking** is optional and off by default (Admin > Settings > Failed sign-in blocking): when turned on, an address that fails 20 sign-ins in 10 minutes is blocked for 15 minutes, doubling on a repeat within 7 days up to 24 hours, never permanently. Blocks are stored (they survive a restart) and show in the block list with a Kind column (`login` or `probe` for an automatic block, a dash for one made by hand) and their expiry. A failure from an address that any account signed in from within the last 30 days is not counted, whichever account it names, so only the general rate limit of 100 requests a minute per address slows guessing from such an address; the never-ban list applies as above. Scanner (probe) blocks do not have the sign-in exemption: only the never-ban rules protect an address from them. A **logged-out request limit** (Admin > Settings > Logged-out request limit, on by default, 5 to 600 requests a minute, 30 by default) answers `429` to a client with no valid sign-in that sends too many requests to `/proxy`, `/search` or `/browser`; it blocks nothing and never touches `/login`. A weekly job refreshes the built-in Cloudflare range list (on by default, can be switched off, sends no portal data); a list that fails the safety checks is held for a superadmin to approve or dismiss, and **Use the built-in list** undoes it. The portal also warns when `TRUSTED_PROXIES` looks wrong. A blocked visitor, and one held by a rate limit, sees a page with a **reference ID** such as `ABCD-EFGH` and no reason; the **Reference lookup** field in Admin > System > Network shows the stored event and can unblock from there ([notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1220)).

### Backups and Restore
Daily backup archives are on by default (the newest 7 are kept). An archive holds a consistent copy of the database, the files needed to read it and a manifest of hashes; you can add an extra destination directory, run **Back Up Now**, and download an archive (superadmin, browser login only). `server.py restore-archive` verifies an archive and unpacks it into an empty directory. Every update takes a backup first and does not start if it fails. Archives can optionally carry the member uploads (off by default, 1.24.2). Treat an archive like root access to the portal. Details are under [Backups and Rollback](#backups-and-rollback).

### Notifications and Alerts
Besides signed generic JSON, outgoing webhooks can send to **ntfy, Gotify, Discord and Slack**. A superadmin can create **system webhooks** (Admin > Webhooks) and subscribe them to fourteen kinds of alert, plus a notice sent whenever alerts were held back by the limits: failed sign-in bursts, two-factor turned off, API key created, role changed, IP blocked, update available, service health changes, certificate expiry and renewal failure, game server crashes, backup success and failure, moderation actions and automod triggers. Each alert can also appear as an in-portal notification for admins. When a stream you follow (or, if its owner chose everyone, any stream you may watch) goes live you get an in-portal notification with the owner's go-live message, linked to the stream. The `stream.live` webhook goes to the hooks subscribed to it for a public stream, and only to system webhooks (generic format) for a private one; its payload carries the watch address and message and no stream key.

The defaults are cautious. **No alert leaves the machine until a superadmin creates a system webhook and picks alert events**; `*` never includes an alert. A message to ntfy, Gotify, Discord or Slack carries a title, a one-line summary and the portal name, and no message text, IP address or token. IP addresses, user agents and rule names are added only when a hook's **include_details** switch is on (off by default). `message.created` goes to such a hook only when listed by name, and never for private channels, DMs or non-public stream chats. An anonymous post is sent with its author shown only as "Anonymous". Credentials are stored encrypted and never shown again, and a hook cannot be aimed at private or loopback addresses unless a superadmin allows it for a self-hosted ntfy or Gotify. Alerts are rate-limited, and a notice says what was held back. The formats were tested against local test receivers only, so press **Test** on a new hook before relying on it.
## Usage Examples

Here are a few ways people use Open Relay Portal:

- **"I want to stream for my friends without a public platform."** Go live from OBS to your portal's RTMPS address. Your friends open your portal URL in their browser and watch. Recordings are saved automatically.

- **"Our team needs a private group chat with voice."** Create channels for different topics, invite your team with an invite code, and use voice chat when you need to talk. Everything stays on your server.

- **"Our organization wants chat and voice on infrastructure it runs itself."** Deploy Portal on your own infrastructure. Chat is encrypted at rest and voice goes between the participants' browsers, so a third party does not hold the data; whoever administers the server can still read the chat.

- **"My friend needs help with their computer."** Open a VNC connection in your browser and see their screen in real time — walk them through the fix or take control directly.

- **"I need a file from my home server while I'm traveling."** Log into your portal from any browser, open the file manager, and download what you need. No VPN setup, no special app.

- **"I manage a home server with several services."** Add each service as a connection in Portal — Proxmox, Home Assistant, Grafana, Pi-hole, whatever you run. Access all of them through one authenticated dashboard in your browser, from anywhere.

## Quick Start

```bash
git clone https://github.com/dustinm16/portal.git
cd portal
sudo python3 server.py setup
```

That's it. The setup wizard handles everything automatically:
1. Creates a virtual environment and installs all dependencies
2. **Auto-generates a self-signed TLS certificate** (server starts immediately)
3. Generates a JWT secret and writes `.env` configuration
4. **Downloads MediaMTX** streaming server (for RTMPS/HLS live streaming)
5. Creates the database and initial admin user
6. Installs and starts a systemd service

Step 10 of the wizard is optional: it explains the server-side Web Browser and offers to install its packages (the default answer is No); it never switches the feature on.

Open `https://your-hostname` in a browser. Self-signed certs show a warning — click "Advanced" > "Proceed" to continue. Switch to Let's Encrypt or a custom certificate later via the Admin Panel or by re-running setup.

### Manual Setup

```bash
# Create virtual environment and install dependencies
python3 -m venv venv
source venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt

# Configure (or copy .env.example and edit)
cp .env.example .env
chmod 600 .env
# Edit .env: set JWT_SECRET, HOSTNAME, SSL_CERT, SSL_KEY

# Generate a self-signed cert if you don't have one
python -c "import cert_manager; cert_manager.generate_self_signed_cert('localhost', 'certs')"

# Install MediaMTX for streaming (optional, Linux amd64)
sudo python server.py install-mediamtx

# Install fail2ban with an SSH jail (optional host hardening, Debian/Ubuntu)
sudo venv/bin/python server.py install-fail2ban --ignore-ip <your-ip>

# Install SearXNG for private web search (optional, Debian/Ubuntu)
sudo ./install-searxng.sh
# then, in the admin Services panel: add a managed service of type "searxng" and enable it

# Packages for the optional server-side Web Browser (Debian/Ubuntu; it stays off until a superadmin switches it on in Admin > Settings)
sudo apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends chromium xvfb x11vnc xauth x11-xserver-utils xsel

# Initialize database and create admin user
python server.py init

# Run (port 443 requires root)
sudo venv/bin/python server.py serve
```

### Requirements
- Python 3.11+
- Linux (systemd for service management features)
- Port 443 (HTTPS) — requires root, or use a higher port in `.env`
- Optional Web Browser (server-side): the portal running as root on Linux with systemd 249+ and cgroup v2, plus the packages `chromium`, `xvfb`, `x11vnc`, `xauth`, `x11-xserver-utils` and `xsel` (the setup wizard offers them, default No); see [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side)

## Architecture

```
Python/aiohttp backend ──── SQLite database (FTS5 search)
       │                         │
       ├── WebSocket relay ──── Plugins (SSH, VNC, SPICE, ...)
       ├── HLS streaming ────── MediaMTX (managed process)
       ├── Private search ───── SearXNG (optional managed process)
       ├── Web Browser ──────── Chromium in a locked-down systemd unit per session (optional, off by default)
       ├── Chat engine ──────── Fernet encryption at rest
       ├── Direct messages ──── Encrypted 1:1 & group DMs
       ├── Message search ───── FTS5 full-text index
       ├── Voice signaling ──── WebRTC P2P (no server-side audio)
       ├── File manager ─────── Local + remote SFTP
       ├── System monitor ───── psutil + systemd
       └── Data retention ───── Configurable auto-cleanup
```

Single binary. No Docker required. No microservices. No external databases. One Python process, one SQLite file, one `.env` config.

## Security

- **HTTPS only** — TLS 1.2+ with HSTS preload, no HTTP fallback
- **Encryption at rest** — Chat messages, connection configs, stream keys, TOTP secrets all encrypted (Fernet/PBKDF2)
- **Machine-bound keys** — Encryption keys derived from hardware ID + random salt; cloning the repo cannot decrypt data
- **Argon2id** password hashing
- **Voice** — WebRTC DTLS-SRTP between the participants' browsers; the portal server carries signalling only (a TURN relay, if you configure one, carries the encrypted audio)
- **Path traversal protection** — File manager validates all paths with `Path.resolve()`
- **Input validation** — All user input sanitized, no shell injection vectors
- **Upload content security** — Uploaded files sanitized: only safe types (images, video, audio, PDF) render inline; HTML/JS/SVG forced to download to prevent stored XSS. Since 1.24.1 an upload (custom emotes excepted: they are public by design) is served only through the signed-in route, judged by the file its address resolves to (any other way of spelling the path is refused), with `Cache-Control: private`, and a chat attachment sent as a generic binary keeps its extension only when it is on the allowed list (any other is stored as `.bin`) ([notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1241))
- **API credential redaction** — Passwords and keys never returned from API endpoints; since 1.24.1 every stored secret of a connection (the Proxmox API token, extra HTTP headers, GitHub tokens, a VPN key and the rest) is write-only too, and a test reads every connection type's schema so a new credential field cannot be forgotten ([notes](#upgrade-notes-for-1241))
- **Stream key encryption** — SHA-256 hashed for lookups, encrypted at rest
- **Opaque connection IDs** — URL-safe tokens replace sequential integers, preventing enumeration
- **Proxy isolation** — Embedded browser strips session cookies and auth headers from upstream requests. Since 1.24.1 a proxied page runs in the portal's own origin, as the person viewing it, only when an administrator owns the connection, the viewer owns it, or a superadmin marked it trusted; every other page, and every page of browser mode, runs in an opaque-origin sandbox that cannot call the portal as the viewer. **A limit you should know:** an application an administrator proxies runs with that administrator's session, and with the session of every person it is shared with, on the portal's own address, and a connection a superadmin marks trusted does the same for everybody who opens it.
