- **Rollback.** Going back to 1.26.1 needs nothing.
- **Not tested.** Measured on Chromium 156.0.8078.108 as an ordinary user outside a unit, with Chromium's sandbox on: the check passes there (three renderers, each in its own user and pid namespace; the zygotes, the GPU process and the network service as expected), and it fails when the browser is started with `--no-sandbox` and when it is started with `--disable-seccomp-filter-sandbox`. The process picture inside a real session unit comes from a measurement of a production session made from the host (renderers in their own user, pid and network namespaces; the GPU process and the network service in the browser's). The new probe has not run inside a real probe unit in this release: `G26` of the live test (the probe once as it is, and once with `--no-sandbox` added for the probe only, which has to fail) is written and has not been run, so whether the probe can read every renderer's namespace links under the unit's `ProtectProc` setting is confirmed only by the one check of a real host after the upgrade. A renderer that starts later than a second and a half after the others and is not in its own namespaces would not be seen.

### Upgrade notes for 1.26.1

1.26.1 fixes typed characters getting lost in the [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side). **Nothing has to be changed and nothing is migrated**: no setting, no database column, no route. One static file changes (`rbrowser.js` v4). The server side of the fix is in the supervisor that runs inside each session's unit, so it applies to sessions started after the upgrade; a session that is already running keeps its old keyboard map until it ends, and a page that is still open keeps the old `rbrowser.js` until it is reloaded. Going back to 1.26.0 needs nothing.

- **What was wrong.** Every typed character reaches the remote browser as a key event with one keysym. A keysym that is not on the X server's keyboard map is put on a key without symbols by x11vnc, which gives those keys back only when it exits. The Xvfb of this host has 18 such keys, so the 19th different non-US character a session had ever typed was the first one dropped, silently and for the rest of the session. On a rig, 5000 characters of Russian text (64 different letters) lost 72 percent: they never reached the X server. Typing the occasional accented letter never hit the limit, which is why it went unnoticed.
- **Two typing paths, one keysym per character.** What a member types on the picture with their own keyboard goes through noVNC's keyboard code, which maps a character with `core/input/keysymdef.js`: the legacy X11 keysym where one exists (Cyrillic, the Polish letters, the euro sign, typographic punctuation) and `0x01000000` plus the code point otherwise. The page's own paths (Send text, paste as typing) used to send `0x01000000` plus the code point for everything above Latin-1, so the same letter had two different keysyms and each used up its own key. `rbrowser.js` now asks the same noVNC module (`keysymdef.js`, the very module noVNC's keyboard imports, loaded once) for the keysym, so both paths agree for every character. An older page that is still open sends the old form; it still types, but only until the free keys are used up (below).
- **What changed on the server.** Before x11vnc and Chromium start, the supervisor puts the usual characters on the keyboard map itself, in exactly the keysyms noVNC sends: the Latin-1 letters with their capitals, sharp s, the euro sign, typographic quotes and dashes, Russian, Ukrainian and Belarusian Cyrillic, and the Polish letters, two characters on one key (plain and with Shift). The keys it uses are keys that carry only media and launcher symbols (`XF86...`, 89 on this host; the unit has no sound and no desktop) and keys with no symbols; the browser navigation keys (back, forward, reload, stop, search, home, favourites) are kept. 16 keys stay free for x11vnc's own additions (Greek, Czech and so on, until those are used up; CJK, emoji and the like are still limited to about those 16 different characters per session; the 17th is the first one lost). The supervisor cannot read JavaScript, so the table's keysyms are data in `rbrowser_unit/session.py` (`KEYMAP_KEYSYMS`), generated by `tools/keymap_table.py` from the vendored `keysymdef.js`; a test reads that file and fails if any value differs, and another test runs the page's `keysymFor` for every code point of the table. The step is not fatal: if it fails, one line `the keyboard map was not prepared` goes to the journal and the session runs as before.
- **x11vnc's arguments did not change**, so the clipboard flags (`-nosel`, `-noprimary`, `-nosetprimary`, `-noclipboard`, `-nosetclipboard`, `-input KMB`) are as in 1.26.0 and no flag was added that lets a member change server state beyond keys and pointer. What the new step lets a member influence: nothing; it runs once, from the supervisor's own X connection, before any member input exists, with a fixed table.
- **What was measured, and on which path.** A rig, not a real unit: own Xvfb (no window manager), x11vnc with the supervisor's exact argument list, the host's Chromium with its sandbox on, the host under a load of 8 busy loops on 4 cores for the "load" rows. Two ways of typing: (1) a raw RFB client of my own that sends key events as noVNC does (down and up, 40 per 30 ms), in noVNC's legacy keysym form (the numbers below), and in the old `0x01000000` form for the old-page question; (2) once, the real page `/rbrowser` with the real noVNC in headless Chromium on a staged portal whose fake session was replaced by a relay to the rig's x11vnc (so the real bridge and its client filter were in the path), typing 43 characters on the canvas with real key events and then through Send text. An XInput2 logger told "reached X" from "reached the field". **Before the fix:** Russian text, 5000 characters, 3 runs: 28 percent arrived (4244 of 15000), the rest never reached X; the real page: 17 of 43 canvas characters arrived. 60-key bursts of 12 accented and Cyrillic characters on a fresh x11vnc: 1 key of 3600 lost in Chromium after reaching X; mixed 5000-character text: 2 keys lost in 2 of 6 runs; fresh sessions typing four accented characters: 1 key of 120 lost in Chromium. **After the fix:** Russian text 15000 of 15000; the real page 43 of 43 on the canvas and the Send text line exact; bursts 0 of 3600; mixed text 0 of 30000; fresh sessions 0 of 120; all 176 characters of the table (both cases) typed in one run came out exactly. Characters that are not in the table (Greek, Czech and so on, added by x11vnc on the fly) are as before: 1 key of 2400 lost in Chromium and, once in 40 runs, one wrong character with the right length. The loss in Chromium after a key map change is rare (about 2 percent of runs) and the samples are small: the claim is "not seen again for the covered characters in these runs", not "cannot happen". Plain ASCII, 5000 characters, 10 runs, lost nothing with or without the fix. A single first accented character on a fresh x11vnc lost nothing in 400 Chromium runs. **An old page (the `0x01000000` form) against the new map:** it still types, but each character it uses takes a free key: 5000 Russian characters gave 25 percent (2 runs), the same as before the fix. **Not run:** a real unit (the production feature is on and the live gates need it off), the built-in window manager, a real phone keyboard, a real Windows or macOS keyboard; typing on the canvas after a pointer click did not type in the rig (the rig has no window manager) and was replaced by focusing the canvas, so the real page was measured without a click; the live gates `G17` and `G21` were changed but not run.
- **The two sightings in the live gates.** `G17` lost 60 of 5000 ASCII characters once: on the rig ASCII never lost a key and the field fell seconds behind its input under load (a snapshot taken 1.5 s after the last key read thousands of characters too few), so `G17` now waits until the field has the full length (or has not changed for 4 s) before it counts; that is the most likely explanation, not a proven one. `G21` lost its first character once in four runs; the rig did not reproduce that exact case (0 of 400 first-used characters). `G21` now types only after the page has recorded the click on the field (the text area has `autofocus`, so being the active element proves nothing) and shows `document.hasFocus()` (it goes on and says so if the window manager never makes that true). The keymap fix removes the one rig-measured loss of a first-used character in Chromium for the covered characters. If `G21` still fails after this, it is a real result.
- **One sandbox check after the upgrade.** The preflight key covers the supervisor's source and the portal version, so the first start after the upgrade runs the sandbox check once (as after 1.26.0).
- **Static files.** `rbrowser.js` v4 (`rbrowser.html`); `rbrowser.css` is unchanged at v2.
- **Rollback.** Going back to 1.26.0 needs nothing.

### Upgrade notes for 1.26.0

1.26.0 makes the remote browser of the [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side) follow the member's portal theme (the **Theme** paragraph there). It is cosmetic and applies per session, at session start. **Nothing has to be changed and nothing is migrated**: no setting, no database column, no new route, and a session that is already running keeps the look it started with. Going back to 1.25.1 needs nothing.

- **What changed.** The page sends the colour of its own toolbar (`--bg-secondary`, as `#rrggbb`) and `dark` or `light` with the request that starts a session (`POST /api/rbrowser/session`, optional `theme`; see `docs/API.md`). The server and the registry both validate it strictly and a bad value means Chromium's neutral look, never a refused start. For each session the portal writes one extra root-owned read-only policy file that holds only `BrowserThemeColor`, binds it read-only into the unit next to the security policy, and removes it when the session ends (a stale one is also removed by the sweep at start). The supervisor adds `--force-dark-mode` for a dark theme. The shared policy tree stays the single source of every security policy.
- **How Chromium merges policy files.** Chromium reads every `*.json` file in the managed policy directory and merges them; for a key that two files both set, the file that sorts later by name wins (measured). The theme file is called `portal-theme.json`, which sorts before `portal.json`, and holds the single key `BrowserThemeColor`, which no security setting uses, so it can neither add to nor override the security policy. A unit test pins the exact key set of the generated file.
- **What the member sees.** On Chromium 156 the tab strip takes the portal toolbar colour (exact for the 24 dark themes, within about 22 per colour channel for the light themes and Nord), the browser's own toolbar row and menus a derived tint of that hue (nearly white for the light themes), a dark theme makes the browser's own interface dark and pages report `prefers-color-scheme: dark`, and a light theme leaves Chromium at its light default. The remote toolbar row is therefore **not** the same pixel colour as the page's toolbar; Chromium derives it. Page content is never recoloured. The About panel says that a theme change applies to the next session.
- **Hosts prepared before this release.** The placeholder file for the theme is written when the host is prepared (at the portal's start). Until it has been, a session starts without the colour (the dark or light switch still applies) instead of failing. The preflight key changes once because the supervisor and the unit properties changed, so one sandbox check runs after the upgrade; the check itself uses no theme, and the theme never changes the key.
- **Static files.** `rbrowser.js` v3 (`rbrowser.html`); `rbrowser.css` is unchanged at v2. `docs/API.md` and `api-docs.html` describe the optional `theme` field; `about.html` and `guides.html` say that the remote browser follows the theme.
- **Not tested.** Measured on Chromium 156.0.8078.108 on the test host, headful under a private Xvfb in an unprivileged mount namespace with the production policy minus `DeveloperToolsAvailability`: what `BrowserThemeColor` does to the tab strip, toolbar row and menus for all 28 portal colours, that `--force-dark-mode` makes the interface dark and `prefers-color-scheme` dark without recolouring a page, that the later file name wins a duplicate key, that the three-dot menu is the same with and without the file, and that a read-only file bind over a placeholder inside a read-only directory bind works at kernel level. The page, the route, the registry and the launcher were tested with fakes and the staged fake launcher; Chromium in a real portal page was driven with headless Chromium against the fake RFB server. **Not run in this release's tests:** a real unit with the theme bind (systemd's own handling of the file bind inside the read-only `/etc/chromium` bind is proven only by the argument list and the kernel check; the bind is written `BindReadOnlyPaths=-<file>:<destination>`, so a source that is gone is skipped, and a unit that does not come up with the bind is taken back and started once more without it; that retry is counted (`theme_fallbacks` in the registry counters) and logged as one neutral line, but it is itself untested against real systemd, and a unit that is only slow to come up is not retried, so such a start can still fail); the live gate `G25` of `tests/test_rbrowser_live.py` was written and not run, a real unit run of it is the release gate: it is a hard gate of that test (a FAIL blocks the release), it uses the production argument list, and it refuses to run on a host whose rbrowser state is prepared, so it needs a planned teardown of that state or another host. The policy key `BrowserColorScheme` had no observable effect and is not used; GTK settings were not tried.

### Upgrade notes for 1.25.1

Two layout fixes to the Web Browser page, found by running a real session in Firefox and in phone emulation. No setting, route or database change; going back to 1.25.0 needs nothing.

- **Toolbar.** Between 641 and about 1000 pixels of width (a phone held sideways, a narrow desktop window) the row of controls was wider than the window and End session was partly out of sight. The row now wraps there too.
- **Phone held upright.** The remote window was asked for with the stage's height in pixels although the picture is scaled down to fit the width, which left an empty band above and below. The page now asks for a window of the stage's own shape (560 wide, height scaled, at most 1200).
- **Static files.** `rbrowser.js` v2 and `rbrowser.css` v2 (`rbrowser.html`).
- **Tested since 1.25.0.** A real session (real unit, the portal's bridge) was driven end to end on a staged instance with headless Chromium, with Firefox 157 and with Chromium emulating an Android phone (touch, rotation, the Keyboard button). **Still not tested:** Safari and WebKit (it does not start on the test host), a real phone with its on-screen keyboard, and the path through Cloudflare.

### Upgrade notes for 1.25.0

1.25.0 adds one optional feature, the [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side): a Chromium that runs in a locked-down systemd unit on the portal's server and reaches a member as a picture. **It is off by default and nothing changes unless a superadmin switches it on**, after installing packages and passing the sandbox check. No setting has to be changed, nothing is migrated and no existing feature behaves differently. The old embedded browser (browser mode and proxied applications) is unchanged in this release. If you do not want the feature, you need do nothing; if you do, read **Switching it on**, **Privacy and limits** and **Rollback**.

**Breaking changes**

None expected. Nothing an existing script, API client or page uses was changed. The new routes and settings are only added.

**Switching it on**

- **Off by default.** An install that is upgraded and left alone starts no unit, mounts nothing, loads no firewall rule or BPF filter and asks for no package. The dashboard card is not drawn and `/rbrowser` says the browser is not available.
- **Packages.** Chromium (the distribution's package, not a snap), `xvfb`, `x11vnc`, `xauth`, `x11-xserver-utils`, `xsel` and `python3`; `iptables` and `ip6tables` are optional and give only the upload limit. The setup wizard has a new optional **step 10** (the later steps are numbered 11 to 13) that explains the feature, lists what is missing, and offers one `apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends` line with the default answer No; it never switches the feature on and writes no setting. The admin card prints the same line.
- **Preflight.** After a superadmin ticks **Switched on** in Admin > Settings > **Web Browser on the server**, the portal prepares the host and runs a sandbox check in a probe unit with the limits of a session. No session starts until a check has passed under the current settings, the Chromium version and the host's facts; **Run check** repeats it. The host must be able to run it: the portal as root, systemd 249 or newer, cgroup v2, a loadable BPF filter, unprivileged user namespaces, and the packages above. Since 1.26.2 the sandbox part of the check is strict: a headless Chromium is started with a page, every renderer process it starts must have a user namespace and a pid namespace of its own (different from the browser's), a system call filter and `no_new_privs`, and no sandbox-disabling flag may be on any command line; a renderer the probe cannot see into counts as unproven and fails the check.
- **Who.** Administrators only until the superadmin changes **Who may use it** to moderators or everybody. Two seats by default; the effective number depends on the host's memory (about 12 GB for two sessions with the defaults).
- **Wizard renumbering.** A script that waits for "STEP 10: Admin User" and the rest of the wizard's prompts will meet the new step; later steps moved by one.

**What you will notice**

- **Members who may use it** get a dashboard card **Web Browser (on this server)** under My Connections and the page `/rbrowser`. Others see nothing.
- **Administrators** get the card in Admin > Settings: whether the host can run it and what is missing, the last sandbox check, the ten settings, the seats the host really has, the Chromium version (with a warning above 45 days) and the running sessions (member, since, active or idle, attached, memory, CPU time, data in and out) with an **End** button behind a question. There is no way to see a session's page.
- **Maintenance mode** also covers it: while a global maintenance flag is on, starting a session and opening `/rbrowser` are refused and an attach closes with code 4503.
- **A portal update, restart or stop ends every running session** (the units are bound to the portal's own unit); the page says why and offers a new session.

**Privacy and limits**

The privacy statement is the paragraph under [Web Browser (server-side)](#web-browser-server-side), word for word the one the page shows under **About this browser**. What it means, checked against the code: pages are fetched by the server, so sites see the server's address and not the member's; name lookups go to DNS over HTTPS at Cloudflare's `1.1.1.1` (fixed in the code, not a setting); the home page and search are DuckDuckGo; the portal keeps no destination log, no page titles, no key codes, no pointer positions and no clipboard text, and records only that a session started and ended (activity log: who, when, minutes, why) and in its log file how many bytes moved; the browser profile lives in a memory-backed area and is thrown away at the end of a session; an administrator can see that a session exists and end it, and the portal has no function to watch or join one, but **administrators of this portal have a root shell on the server through the web terminal and could observe a running browser**; the picture travels over the portal's own authenticated WebSocket (the browser sign-in only: no API key, no bearer token) and through Cloudflare when you use it. It is not anonymity and not end-to-end encryption. **Because no destination is logged, two sessions that run at the same time cannot be attributed to one member in an abuse report.**

The limits a member meets: no downloads, no uploads (no file dialog), no sound, no printing, no camera, microphone or screen capture, no extensions, no Chromium account or sync, no password manager, no page fullscreen; sessions end on a portal restart, after the idle minutes, after 5 minutes without a window, after 4 hours and when the data allowance (512 MiB sent, 8 GiB received) is used up; seats are limited and one session per member; the clipboard has three modes (`both`, `in`, `off`); dead keys do not compose; scrolling text is about 8 frames a second; a destination in a blocked range times out (or gives Chromium's blocked page when named as an address); `/tmp` is one shared 32 MiB area; windows other than a browser's own windows and pop-ups, such as a file dialog or Picture-in-Picture, are never shown; and at very small window sizes a page's drop-down list can touch the toolbar area, so the remote address bar is not a security indicator there. Safe Browsing is off. The security design in short: a transient systemd unit per session with a dynamic user, a kernel egress deny list for private and local ranges, an ingress BPF filter against listeners, a managed Chromium policy, Chromium's own sandbox kept on, and an RFB client-message filter in the portal. The set-up steps that go with it (the server's public address in `extra_deny`, `kernel.yama.ptrace_scope=1`, keeping Chromium and the kernel current) are in the feature section and the Security list.

**New settings, routes and events**

- **Settings** (ten rows of the existing `settings` table, written only when a superadmin saves the card; until then the defaults apply): `rbrowser_enabled` (false), `rbrowser_min_role` (`admin`), `rbrowser_max_sessions` (2), `rbrowser_idle_minutes` (15), `rbrowser_memory_mb` (2048), `rbrowser_cpu_percent` (150), `rbrowser_clipboard` (`both`; `in`, `off`; the older name `to_browser` is read as `in`), `rbrowser_home_url` (`https://duckduckgo.com/`), `rbrowser_upload_kbps` (4000) and `rbrowser_extra_deny` (empty).
- **Routes:** `GET /api/admin/rbrowser` (administrator), `PUT /api/admin/rbrowser` (superadmin, browser sign-in), `POST /api/admin/rbrowser/preflight` (superadmin; `409` while the feature is off), `POST /api/admin/rbrowser/sessions/{sid_prefix}/end` (administrator, body `{"confirm": true}`), `GET /rbrowser` (the page), `GET /api/rbrowser/status`, `GET`, `POST` and `DELETE /api/rbrowser/session`, `POST /api/rbrowser/session/resize`, `POST /api/rbrowser/session/clipboard` (the member routes need the browser sign-in) and the WebSocket `/ws/rbrowser/{sid}`. Close codes: `4003` not allowed, `4004` no such session, `4012` opened in another window, `4013` the session ended (the reason is the close text) or the connection was too slow or broke the rules, `4503` maintenance, `1001` the stream ended (the page reconnects). The API is in [API.md](docs/API.md) and `/api-docs`.
- **Security events** (new names, superadmin's Security Events list): `rbrowser_settings_changed`, `rbrowser_preflight_failed`, `rbrowser_listener_found`, `rbrowser_socket_mismatch`, `rbrowser_display_mismatch`, `rbrowser_protocol_violation`, `rbrowser_traffic_limit`. Their details are fixed words, never an address, title or text. **Audit log actions:** `rbrowser_start`, `rbrowser_end`, `rbrowser_admin_end`, `rbrowser_settings_update`. An administrator who ends a session sends the member a notification, "An administrator ended your Web Browser session".
- **Static files.** `dashboard.js` v62 (the card; `index.html`), new `rbrowser.js` v1 and `rbrowser.css` v1 (`rbrowser.html`), new `rbrowser-admin.js` v1 (`admin.html`); noVNC 1.4.0 is also used by the new page; `sw.js` no longer treats a `/rbrowser` window as a portal window (a push click does not focus it). The new page has a stricter content security policy than the other pages (no inline script, no `blob:` script) and its own `frame-ancestors 'none'`.

**Database**

No table, no column, no migration and no index is added; the first start after the upgrade writes nothing. The ten rows above appear in `settings` only after a superadmin saves the card. The feature keeps its runtime state under `/run` (two small memory-backed areas, a state folder with the cached check result) and in the kernel (a resource slice, a pinned BPF program and, with `iptables`, a firewall chain named `PORTAL-RB`), all of which a reboot clears.

**Rollback**

Going back to 1.24.2 works on the same database: the ten rows are ignored. **Switch the feature off first** (the card, **Switched on** unticked): that ends the sessions, stops the units, unmounts the two areas under `/run`, removes the firewall chain, the slice and the BPF pin (also after a start that failed half way). If the old version is already running, the units end by themselves at their time limit and a reboot clears the mounts, the chain and the pin. The policy tree (`/run/portal-rb-static`) and the state directory (`/run/portal-rb-state`) under `/run` are not removed by the switch-off and stay until the next reboot. The packages stay installed; remove them yourself if you want.

**One more thing is not safe to leave.** The audit entries of the feature (`rbrowser_start`, `rbrowser_end`, `rbrowser_admin_end`) name the member, the time and the length of each session, and `rbrowser_settings_update` carries the settings "was" and "now" values, including `rbrowser_extra_deny`. Only 1.25.0 hides them from a moderator (and the settings entry from a plain administrator); 1.24.2 does not know them and shows them in Admin > Audit log to a moderator and above. Before going back, delete them with this one statement, run against `portal.db`: `DELETE FROM audit_log WHERE action IN ('rbrowser_start','rbrowser_end','rbrowser_admin_end','rbrowser_settings_update');` (an administrator keeps no record of these actions afterwards), or accept that moderators will read them. A rollback by update snapshot restores the code only, so it does not remove them either.

**Not tested**

No phone took part (no Android or iOS keyboard, no touch device other than Chromium's emulation), and neither Firefox nor Safari was run: the page was exercised in one headless Chromium against a **fake stream** (a stand-in RFB server that sends a tiny demo frame, so the page, its controls, its text paths and the admin card were checked, but not what a real session looks like on it). The real sandbox and containment (the unit's properties, the network rules, the window rules, the sandbox check) were exercised by the live gates of `tests/test_rbrowser_live.py` on **one host: Debian 12, systemd 252, kernel 6.1** (Debian 12.15, Chromium 156 from the distribution, x11vnc 0.9.16). Another distribution, another systemd or kernel, another Chromium, a host without `iptables`, a snap Chromium and a host with other network layouts (several interfaces, IPv6-only, a public address that is not on an interface) were not tried. Not tested: a real browser exploit against the sandbox; the behaviour through Cloudflare (idle limits of the WebSocket, throughput, key latency) when these notes were written; a real session with a member on a slow link; the frame rate and latency figures are measurements from that one host, not guarantees. The unit tests and the staged smoke run use the fake launcher and prove the portal's side (routes, settings, the filter on the member's bytes, the page), not the host's.

### Upgrade notes for 1.24.2

1.24.2 is a fix release: about 120 small fixes to things recorded while 1.24.1 was built and reviewed, and nothing new except optional member uploads in backup archives, which are off by default. No setting has to be changed and nothing is migrated by hand. If you run a production instance, read **Breaking changes** (what a script or API client can meet), **Uploads in backups** and **Rollback** before you decide when to upgrade; **What you will notice** is the rest of the list.

**Breaking changes**

Nothing here locks a member out. Where a stored value is removed, the member enters it once again.

- **The address of an isolated page is longer and encrypted.** It keeps its shape (`/proxy/<id>/~pc1.<body>.<mac>/...`) but is about 40 characters longer, and its body can no longer be read by the page: it holds no member id, session id or connection id that script can see. A page that was open when 1.24.2 was installed carries the old address, which no longer verifies; reload the tab once.
- **A state change from a sibling subdomain is refused.** A `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH` or `DELETE` that carries the session cookie and an `Origin` naming another hostname of the portal's own domain (not the configured `HOSTNAME` or `STREAM_HOSTNAME`) answers `403` with `Cross-site request refused`. Requests with no `Origin` (curl, scripts, OBS) and the portal's own pages are not affected. The log line for a refused sign-in form is now `Refused a cross-site sign-in POST (...)`; for other paths the old wording stays and a new reason, `same-site foreign origin`, appears. A log alert rule that matches the old `/login` wording needs the new text. A form posted from a portal page that sends no referrer (`Origin: null` with fetch metadata saying same-origin) is no longer refused for that reason alone; a sandboxed page is never same-origin, so isolated pages are not let through by this.
- **Sign-out ends the session of the cookie.** `POST /logout` with a session cookie and also a bearer token, API key or `?token=` ends the cookie's session; before, it ended the other credential and left the cookie's session live. With no cookie it still answers `200` and ends nothing.
- **Ending sessions is limited.** `DELETE /api/me/sessions/{id}`, `POST /api/me/sessions/revoke-others` and `POST /api/users/{id}/sessions/revoke` answer `429` (with a `ref` and `Retry-After: 10`) after 30 calls in a minute for one signed-in account. The three routes share the count, and the admin route counts against the acting administrator, not the target.
- **At most 50 live API keys per member.** Creating one more answers `400` with `You already have 50 active API keys; revoke one before creating another`. Revoked and expired keys do not count and nobody loses a key. A real key that shares its prefix with others is no longer refused with `503` because a check on another candidate was busy; `503` now appears only when nothing verified and a check was skipped.
- **Stored connection secrets are removed more often.** Changing a connection's type (even to another type of the same plugin) removes its stored secrets unless the same request sends them again, and so does turning `verify_ssl` from on to off (or removing it; a missing value counts as off). The `PUT` answer lists what was removed in `stored_secrets_cleared`. Turning it on keeps them.
- **`PUT /api/connections/{id}`** with a body that holds `user_id`, `conn_id` or another key that is not a connection field saves the real fields and answers `200`; the extra keys are ignored (it answered `500`).
- **Sessions in the token list.** `GET /api/tokens` rows have a new field `session_id`, and `POST /api/token/revoke` accepts it in `token_id` for the caller's own tokens, from a browser session or a full-scope credential. An administrator cannot reach another member's token by `session_id` (`404`); the full token id works as before.
- **New fields.** `GET /api/me` has `granted_service_expiry` (service id to seconds left, or `null` when the grant never ends; `{}` for administrators). Every row of `GET /api/chat/channels` has an integer `unread_mentions`. The `{"type": "error"}` frame a chat socket gets when it can no longer read its channel carries an integer `channel_id`. `GET /api/admin/backups` and the other backup routes have the fields listed under **Uploads in backups**.
- **Service host key fingerprint.** `POST` or `PUT /api/services` with a `config.host_key_fingerprint` that is set but is not a SHA256 fingerprint (`SHA256:` and 43 base64 characters; a lowercase `sha256:` prefix and a trailing `=` are accepted) answers `400` `Invalid host key fingerprint`; before, it was dropped and the call answered `200` or `201`. An empty value from the API clears the pin.
- **Stream info addresses come from the configured name.** The `hls` and `webrtc_whep` addresses in `GET /api/stream/{key}/info` are built from `PUBLIC_BASE_URL`, else `https://` and `HOSTNAME`, not from the `Host` header of the request; a client that read them through a LAN address now gets the public name, and without either setting they are relative paths.
- **The `?token=` viewer of a shared web connection.** A page request that authenticates with `?token=` to a shared connection whose first-open notice has not been accepted gets a short `403` text page saying to sign in, open the connection once and choose Continue, instead of the sign-in page. The token is never copied into a redirect address. A cookie session still goes to the notice page.
- **The automod pattern field.** In Admin > Auto-Mod the blocked words are one per line (a regex or phrase can contain a comma). A single line without a line break is still a comma list in a word rule, but in a regex rule it is one pattern now, so `a,b` typed on one line in regex mode is saved as one pattern, not two. Stored rules are unchanged until they are saved again. A regex rule may hold at most 100 patterns; a rule saved with more before the cap can be edited only while its pattern list stays exactly as it is.
- **Chat requests and frames.** `POST /api/chat/channels/{id}/members` answers `400` (not `500`) for a body that is not a JSON object and for a `user_id` that is not a number or is out of range. A socket frame `react`, `edit_message` or a `dm_*` frame with an invalid or out-of-range message or conversation id gets an error frame (`Invalid message_id` or `Invalid id`) and the socket stays open; before, a huge id ended it and non-numeric ids were dropped silently. More than 20 `voice_kick`, `voice_server_mute`, `voice_server_unmute`, `voice_lock_room` or `voice_unlock_room` frames in 10 seconds on one socket get `Rate limit exceeded`. `GET /api/chat/link-preview` answers `400` when the host name takes longer than 5 seconds to resolve.
- **The audit log and security events.** Automod triggers are now written to the audit log as `automod_trigger`, filed under the member the rule acted on (actor name `AutoMod`, `"automatic": true` in the details), so a `GET /api/admin/audit-log` filtered by that member's `actor_id` also returns them. A sign-in block that runs out is recorded as `ip_unblocked` with result `expired` (the address in the `ip` column; more than 50 in one sweep write the first 50 and one summary row with `details.more`). Failed sign-ins, failed second factors and rate-limit trips are counted per address, event, result, minute and member, so two members behind one address in the same minute are two rows.
- **Push settings.** A bearer client can `PUT /api/admin/push` with the `allowed_hosts` it just read (only `enabled` or `subject` changed) and get `200` instead of `403`; a changed list still needs a superadmin browser session, and an invalid list from a bearer client answers `400`.
- **Alert rate limit.** An alert hook now has its own window for alerts and one for ordinary events (30 each per 60 seconds), so chat traffic on a hook that also subscribes to alerts no longer drops its alerts; the ceiling of a hook that gets both doubles. The held-back notice says `rate limit of the hook` when the hook's own limit dropped the alerts.
- **Recordings overshoot.** A running terminal or SSH recording checks the recordings budget and the free-space floor after every 1 MB, so the folder can pass `recording_max_total_mb` by about 1 MB for each running recording instead of up to 50 MB each; one that hits it ends with the usual `recording truncated: size limit reached` marker (also when the cause was the free-space floor) and a warning in the log.
- **SSH services.** A config port stored as text (`"2222"`) is dialled as 2222 in the terminal and in the health check; a bad or out-of-range config port dials the service port, or 22.

**What you will notice**

Most noticeable first.

*Proxied pages and the embedded browser*

- **Isolated pages send no `Referer`** to other sites. A site that checks `Referer` for hotlink protection may refuse some requests. Trusted connections are unchanged.
- **Isolated pages can make credentialed requests to their own address.** An application that uses SignalR, axios with `withCredentials` or `fetch` with `credentials: 'include'` is answered with `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: null`, `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true` and `Vary: Origin` when the request's `Origin` is `null` and its capability verifies, and the response's own header names are listed in `Access-Control-Expose-Headers`. Other origins and requests without a valid capability get no credentials. The cookies are kept by the portal; the portal session cookie is never sent to the connection.
- **A stale page finds its way back.** In the embedded browser, clicking a link in a page whose address has expired reloads that tab through a fresh address; the window goes to the sign-in page only when `/api/me` answers `401`. In a plain tab, or a navigation from another site, the page says `This page expired. Reload the tab.` (a signed-out member who follows a stale address from another site sees that page instead of the sign-in page).
- **The address bar of the embedded browser is a convenience, not a security indicator.** The portal cannot read the address of an isolated frame. What the bar shows is the address the portal gave the frame, and what a script the proxy puts into every HTML page reports, a script the page it sits in can also reach. The portal marks an address it could not confirm in two cases, with a tag next to the bar: a page that reports an address on another site than the one the portal requested, and a page that does not answer the portal (an image, plain text, a file, or a page that stops the portal's script). (The first report a page sends while it loads counts, after a link and after a redirect too.) When a report names another site the tag reads `reported by the page, not confirmed`, the bar shows only what the page reported and Open in new tab is off. When the page only stays silent the tag reads `requested address, not confirmed by the page`, the bar shows the address the portal last requested for the tab, and Open in new tab opens that address. A hostile page may still find a way to show a wrong address with no tag, so check a site's identity by opening it in a real browser tab before entering a password. Five rounds of fixes and reviews each found another way, which is why this is stated as a limit and not as a protection.
- **An isolated page's answer carries exactly one `Content-Type` (a 204, 205 or 304 answer that had none gets none).** A browser reads repeated `Content-Type` headers and a comma list differently from a proxy that reads the first value (it takes the last valid one) and sniffs an answer that has none, so a page could be shown as HTML with scripts and without the script the embedded browser relies on. For every answer to an isolated page the proxy now sends one header made from what it parsed (a 204, 205 or 304 answer that had none gets none), the media type in lower case with the charset the upstream named, if it named one (and the boundary of a `multipart` type), together with `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. When the upstream names no charset, none is sent: the proxy reads such an HTML page as UTF-8 while the browser chooses the encoding itself from a byte-order mark or a `<meta>` tag, so the two readings can still differ there, and a page built for that can run without the script the embedded browser relies on. This is one reason the address bar is no security indicator (see above). **A missing, empty, comma-listed or unparseable `Content-Type`, and several different ones, are served as `application/octet-stream`**: the browser downloads such an answer instead of showing it, so an upstream page that sent no type and was shown by sniffing is now a download (the same repeated value counts as one). XHTML, XML and SVG documents run no script in an isolated page, as before, and now by the same type the proxy read. Pages that run on the portal's own address (the owner's, an administrator's, a trusted one) are passed on byte for byte as before. A `multipart/x-mixed-replace` or `text/xsl` answer to an isolated page gets the scriptless sandbox policy (the one SVG gets), which a browser ignores when the answer is used as an image, so a camera feed in an `<img>` keeps working; whether a browser applies the policy to each part when it builds a document from such an answer was not verified.
- **The trust mark covers more.** A superadmin's trust mark and an accepted first-open notice are asked for once more on a connection that sets a config port, a `Host` header in `extra_headers` or path rewrite rules; connections that use none of these keep their mark and notice.
- The notice's Continue link and new-tab requests from proxied pages ignore addresses with `.` or `..` segments and fall back to `/browser`. An upstream cookie with an empty name and the portal session cookie in its value (`=portal_session=x`, also with a space or tab before the second `=`) is not passed on. Access log lines no longer show a capability when the path spells the tilde as `%7E`.
- Connection health: while the global maintenance flag is on, the status dots of HTTP-proxy connections read `maintenance` and no alert is sent for them; an alert owed at that moment is sent after maintenance ends if the state still holds.

*Sign-in, sessions and API keys*

- Hashing a password (sign-in, registration, password change, turning off two-factor, an administrator's reset) runs off the event loop, at most 4 at a time, so it no longer stalls chat and streaming; answers are unchanged.
- The household check for failed sign-ins reads the sign-in addresses through an index, which the first start builds (see **Database**).
- The browser-side user name check on the register page works again in Chromium; valid names are unchanged. The sign-in field has no pattern of its own, so an older account whose name holds a dot, an at sign or a space can still sign in; the server decides.

*Connections*

- The admin SSH service form has a **Host key fingerprint** field that pre-fills the pin, the SFTP connection form has a **Remove stored password** box (shown only when a password is stored), and an older SSH connection that holds a password but has no credential mode shows a note saying so. A saved pin can be cleared through the API with an empty value, not by emptying the field. The admin Desktop (VNC protocol) preset fills port 5900, not 3389.
- A host key accepted in the admin dialog no longer overwrites a config edit made while the prompt was open.
- A granted service's card on the dashboard shows the time left on the grant.

*Chat and moderation*

- Unread mention counts: a channel set to mentions only shows its badge after a reload for mentions received while the page was closed, and a mention already read in the room does not come back. A timed mute that ends redraws the channel list by itself. A mention in a channel set to All closes the channel's desktop alert when the mention notification arrives, so it usually shows one notification, not two (if the mention notification arrives first, both can still show, and a direct `@name` mention of a member who is in the room still shows the channel alert). With several chat tabs, a background tab that cannot play audio no longer takes the sound lock.
- `@everyone` and `@here` are not offered in a direct message or right after switching rooms. Poll cards keep the member's own choice highlighted. The slow mode select returns to the confirmed value when a change is refused. The Live now strip is at most 5rem tall and updates when a stream goes live or offline in the room that is open. On a phone the watch page's chat header buttons wrap. Double clicks on Add to the schedule post once. An administrator with the legacy admin flag and no role sees the Retention button, and a signed-out session that saves retention goes to the sign-in page.
- Chat search: a double-quoted operator value may contain spaces (`from:"Mom Smith"`), and search pages without gaps. Link previews resolve host names off the event loop.
- Direct messages treat a member whose sockets all closed as offline. A stream owner whose first settings update of an old stream fails no longer leaves a `stream-<name>-<id>` chat channel behind.

*Streams and VOD*

- The watch page brings the player back, and counts the viewer again, when a stream goes offline and later live again without a reload. A viewer banned while watching, or whose stream was removed, is told so and polling stops; the streams page shows the ban explanation as a toast. Voice Join works again 30 seconds after a microphone prompt that was never answered. Turning on phone notifications while the connection fails keeps a subscription the browser already held.
- A VOD download or zip whose storage stops answering for 30 seconds (on open or mid-file) ends instead of hanging; a zip ends with a valid listing of the complete files.
- **A change of the VOD storage path.** A member who changes the remote path of their VOD storage leaves earlier undelivered chunks whose folder lies under the old path where they are: they are not uploaded to the old path, one warning per folder is logged, and they are deleted by the sweep once they are older than `vod_orphan_max_age_days` (default 14; 0 means never). A path that names the same folder in another spelling (relative against absolute) is never aged out. If the old storage was unreachable for longer than that period and the path is changed, the chunks go on the first pass after the change.
- Stale hidden `.live_*.part` and `.sweep_*.part` files older than 24 hours are removed from a member's storage folders the sweep delivers into (the sweep asks again a day later, and gives up after 30 days; a storage that cannot be reached is asked again in a day, not at every pass). The `VOD recording was stopped or not started` notice in My VODs is no longer shown after 24 hours and is dropped when its stream or account is deleted; the stream health keeps reporting a stopped or refused recording until the stream goes. Repeated clicks on the orphan chat upload cleanup wait for the scan that runs. A second unmeasured VOD session of one member answers `busy measuring` (`503`, `Retry-After: 10`) without blocking other members.

*Alerts, webhooks and push*

- A retried `stream.live` or `stream.offline` to a system hook with a format other than generic (Discord, for example) is not sent if the stream has meanwhile turned private or been deleted.
- `update.available` is repeated at the next daily check when no hook accepted it, and a hook that failed once and accepted on a retry within the 25 second wait counts as having received it. On a staged instance without a local sink hook it is no longer counted as announced through hooks.
- Resetting the push key no longer deletes devices while the old key stays if the reset fails midway. An unreadable stored push key is logged once per process and kind, not on every page load. A restart ends the viewer sync, grant sweeper and moderator-level pushes cleanly; a stream disconnect hook that arrives while the server stops is acknowledged and ignored.
- A schedule reminder pass drops the Interested marks of an entry whose time zone cannot be loaded (one warning per entry per pass); when the host can load no zone at all, not even UTC, nothing is dropped. A sweep that ends several shares or grants finishes in about one close wait.
- Expired automatic blocks are deleted and reported by one statement. When a sign-in or probe ban runs out the event is written.

*Admin pages*

- Admin > Retention: emptying any of the eleven plain number fields and pressing Save says `Enter a number for <field>; nothing was saved` instead of resetting the stored value to a default. Admin > Live streams keeps its figures moving while an End now or Stop relays question is open. The status colours of the webhook and VOD retention lines follow the theme (the dark theme's red and green are slightly darker). The chart error names the vendored file, not a CDN.
- The Recordings player requests xterm as `?v=5.3.0`, as the terminal page does. The Activity feed keeps up to `retention_activity_max` events (default 500, applied by the clean-up pass) instead of the 50 newest.
- Test tooling only: tests built on the grant harness use a temporary static folder, a failing harness test removes it, `REQUIRE_BROWSER=1` makes the real-browser proxy test exit 3 instead of 0 when it would skip, and vendored library versions are checked against `static/vendor/README.md`.

**Uploads in backups**

- **The setting.** Admin > Settings > Backups has a new box, **Include member uploads (chat files, avatars, emotes, thumbnails)**, off by default and superadmin only like the other backup settings (`include_uploads` in `PUT /api/admin/backups/settings`, recorded in the existing `backup.settings` audit entry). With it off, an archive has the same members, manifest and keys as in 1.24.1. With it on, scheduled and manual archives also hold `static/uploads` (regular files only). A top-level folder of it that is a link to another disk (`chat`, `thumbnails`) is archived from where it leads, unless it leads into the backups folder, in which case it is skipped; below that, a link is followed only when its target lies inside the uploads folder or inside the same top-level folder's location, and every other link, device or unreadable file is skipped and counted in `skipped_outside` or `skipped_unreadable`. The backup taken before an update stays database-only. The extra destination gets the same archive, so uploads multiply by the number of archives kept plus the extra copy.
- **Size and free-space rule.** Switching it on is refused with `400` when the backup disk cannot hold 1.1 times the size of the uploads folder plus twice the database size. A backup that finds too little space writes the archive without uploads, notes `uploads skipped: not enough space` in the Backups card, and sends a `backup.failed` alert (`The backup was written without member uploads: not enough space`). There is no separate free-space floor like the one recordings use. The card shows how many files and bytes the folder holds and an Uploads column per archive. `GET /api/admin/backups` has `config.include_uploads`, a top-level `uploads_estimate` (`files`, `bytes`, `skipped_outside`, `skipped_vanished`, `skipped_unreadable`) and `uploads` per archive (`null`, or `included`, `files`, `bytes`, `skipped`); `POST /api/admin/backups/run` answers with `uploads` and `uploads_note`. The leading manifest holds the plan only; the final counts and hashes are in the last member, `uploads.index.json`.
- **Restore is the command line only,** and never into the live installation. `server.py restore-archive FILE --into DIR` restores the database as before; `--with-uploads` also restores the uploads into `DIR/static/uploads`; `--uploads-into DIR` restores only the uploads into a folder that may already hold files, and `--overwrite` (only with `--uploads-into`) replaces files that exist (the default keeps them; nothing is ever deleted). Exactly one of `--into` and `--uploads-into` is required, otherwise the command exits with 2. The target (or, if it does not exist yet, its parent) must belong to the account that runs the command and must not be a link or open to others; the live uploads folder is refused even when `static/uploads` is a link to another disk. The command prints how many files were restored, kept and refused. `--with-uploads` on an archive without uploads refuses the whole restore: run it again without the flag. For a disk loss, restore into a new folder and move the files, or restore a whole new installation with `--into ... --with-uploads`.
- **An archive with uploads cannot be restored by 1.24.1 or earlier.** The 1.24.1 `restore-archive` refuses it at the first `uploads/` member and writes nothing; restore it with 1.24.2, or take `portal.db`, `.env` and `.encryption_salt` out with `tar`. An archive made with the setting off, or one whose uploads were skipped for space, restores on 1.24.1. Archives are plain `.tar.gz` files, `0600` in a `0700` folder; uploads have the same protection as the database and no more.

**Database**

- No column and no table is added. One index, `idx_tokens_ip` on `tokens(ip)`, is created at start with `IF NOT EXISTS` (the failed sign-in household check reads it); it is built once, at the first start, and its time on a database of production size was not measured; 1.24.1 ignores it.
- Two settings rows: `backup_include_uploads` (written when the box is saved; missing reads as false) and `vod_part_revisit` (written by the VOD sweep: user ids and remote folder paths of folders to look at again, at most 200 entries, dropped 30 days after they were first noted; only administrators can read settings, and 1.24.1 ignores it).
- Nothing existing is rewritten by a one-time pass. Three things work on existing data. The member recording flag removal (a `recording` key stored on a connection of a member who is not an administrator) now runs at every start instead of once; it reads and decrypts every member connection config and rewrites only the rows that hold the key, and logs one warning per config it cannot decode at each start. The activity log is no longer cut to 50 rows at every insert (there is no trigger): the table grows until the clean-up pass trims it to `retention_activity_max` (default 500), and it holds member IP addresses as before, readers are still limited to 50 and other members' addresses are still hidden from non-administrators. A per-channel chat or file retention below 1 day (set only by editing the database by hand) is no longer a value: the channel follows the global retention, and fractions of 1 or more count as the whole number of days.
- New security-event rows have the member in their aggregation key and `ip_unblocked` rows with result `expired` appear; rows already stored are untouched.
- **Static files.** The pages now load `admin.js` v42 (the Desktop preset port) and `dashboard.js` v61 (the time left on a granted service's card), `user-connections.js` v63 (`index.html`), `chat-notify.js` v2, `chat-retention.js` v4 and `voice.js` v16 (`chat.html`; `voice.js` also by `watch.html`), `push.js` v7 (every page that loads it), `stream-schedule.js` v9 (`index.html`, `streams.html`, `watch.html`), `live-ops-admin.js` v6 (the probe that shows the Live streams tab lets go of the answer's body, so the admin page becomes idle) and `recordings-admin.js` v5 (`admin.html`). The Recordings player asks for xterm as `?v=5.3.0`. Pages served without a version that changed: `about.html`, `admin.html`, `api-docs.html`, `browser.html`, `chat.html`, `files.html`, `guides.html`, `index.html`, `login.html`, `streams.html`, `vod-player.html` and `watch.html`. `portal.js`, `portal.css` and `theme.js` keep their versions. A browser or an edge cache that holds the old files needs the new addresses, which the version numbers give it; purge the Cloudflare cache of `/static/*` if you want the pages to change at once.

**Rollback**

1.24.1 starts on the migrated database; nothing has to be run. What changes on 1.24.1: every isolated page that is open fails to verify (1.24.1 reads the body of the address as plain text, which the encrypted body is not, so it is refused like an expired address) and needs a reload through the portal; the index and the two settings rows are ignored; the activity log is cut back to 50 rows at the first insert; and an archive made with **Include member uploads** on is refused by the 1.24.1 `restore-archive` (see above). Trust marks and accepted notices made on 1.24.2 for a connection with a config port, a `Host` header or rewrite rules do not match on 1.24.1 and are asked for again; a mark made on 1.24.1 is asked for again on 1.24.2 for such a connection. Both directions fail safe (the page stays isolated). What is not restored by going back: secrets removed when a connection changed type or turned certificate checking off, member recording flags removed at start, VOD chunks the sweep deleted after the path change and stale `.part` files it removed. After going back, a log rule written for the new refusal wording needs the old one again.

**Not tested**

- There was no phone, no real proxied production application, no real remote VOD storage, no Cloudflare in front, no real MediaMTX or ffmpeg and no real push service. A staged instance and fakes were used; no rehearsal on a copy of a production database is part of these notes.
- **Real browser.** One headless Chromium (156) with the offline flags of `tools/make_screenshots.py` drove the isolated page tests: the encrypted address, credentialed requests under a capability path, the re-entry of a stale page (the expiry was simulated by wrapping the verifier, not by waiting 6 hours or ending a real session), the address bar with its tag and the one `Content-Type` of an isolated answer (a comma list, repeated headers), and the Backups card picture. The upstream applications were local fakes, not SignalR or axios. Firefox, Safari and mobile browsers were not tried.
- **Fakes only.** The chat, watch and streams page changes, the microphone timeout, the sound lock, desktop notifications, the poll highlight, the slow mode select, the retention button, push turn-on and the SFTP and service form changes ran under Node with a fake page, fake timers and a fake fetch, and not in a browser; autoplay after the player restarts, the look of the pages and the wrapped chat header on a phone were not seen. Remote VOD storage ran against a fake SFTP. The restore command ran on temporary folders as the user who ran the tests, not as `root` on a real host. The refusal of an archive with uploads by 1.24.1 was checked by restoring one with that code. A real archive made by 1.24.1 restores through the new command in a test.
- **Not proven.** That a timed-out storage frees the request on a real host, that the unread mention query behaves the same on other SQLite versions than the one used, that the VOD part clean-up works on a storage with thousands of files, and that the first start on a large production database builds the index quickly.
- The full test run and the staged smoke belong to the release procedure; the staged smoke's backups module cannot run its Include uploads checks on a production-copy rehearsal, which already has a known admin sign-in failure.

### Upgrade notes for 1.24.1

1.24.1 is a security release. It fixes problems that exist in 1.24.0 and earlier, and the classes of them are listed here, without detail of how they were reached, with who could do what and whether an invited member was needed. It adds no feature, but a few things appear on screen because of the fixes: a notice the first time somebody opens a shared web connection, a sign-out page, a superadmin screen for the trust mark of shared web connections, a note in the share dialog, and a two-factor QR code drawn by the portal itself. Nothing has to be migrated by hand. If you run a production instance, read **What you will notice** and **Breaking changes** before you decide when to upgrade.

- **A proxied or embedded-browser page could act as the person viewing it.** A page served through `/proxy/{id}` ran on the portal's own address with the viewer's session, so its script could call the portal as that person, an administrator included. A member who owns a web connection could point it at a server of their own and share it with an administrator, so this needed an invited member and an administrator who opened the share. In browser mode (the embedded browser opened on any site) it needed no member at all: a page of any site that somebody browsed to ran as them.
- **An uploaded chat file could be served as a web page, and uploads could be fetched without signing in.** A member who can post a file in chat could store one that an unusual spelling of its address made the portal serve as a page on the portal's own address, where it acted as whoever opened it (this needed an invited member who can post files). The same addresses were answered to anybody, signed in or not, who knew them, a private stream's thumbnail included (this needed no account, only the address).
- **A former administrator's webhooks kept receiving private chat text.** A member who had been an administrator and had set up a webhook kept receiving the events it was subscribed to, messages of private channels among them, after losing the role. It needed somebody who had once been an administrator.
- **Stored connection secrets were readable through the API and followed a repointed connection.** Several stored secrets (a Proxmox token, the values of extra HTTP headers, GitHub tokens and a VPN key) were returned in clear to any credential of the connection's owner, a key limited to reading included. Whoever could edit a connection (its owner, or a key of the owner that was limited to other scopes) could move it to a host of their choosing and the portal then sent the stored secret there. It needed no second member, only a session or a key of the owner.
- **Smaller ones.** Signing out by following a link; a page on another site making state-changing calls with a member's cookie; a proxied application planting or wiping the portal's cookies and setting headers that act on the whole portal address; page libraries loaded from a CDN, most without an integrity check; End now and Stop relays acting on a bare request; replacing the key of a live stream leaving the old publisher connected; viewers who lost access to a stream still watching over WebRTC; deleting an account leaving its stream chat channels behind as public channels; a webhook with the private-destination option reaching the portal's own services; and several small leaks about anonymous members, shared connections and private streams (reactions, moderation answers, the embedded browser's address bar, stored notifications, thumbnails). Most needed an invited member or a signed-in session; signing out by link needed only that the person open a page, and the library one needed a compromise of the CDN.

**Not tested** says what was not proven and in what way.

**What you will notice**

Most disruptive first. Every item says who sees it. The exact contracts for API clients are in **Breaking changes**.

*Shared web applications and the embedded browser (members and administrators)*

- **Isolated pages lose what an opaque origin loses.** A page is isolated when it comes from a web connection that a member (not an administrator) owns and somebody else opens, and always in browser mode, whoever owns the connection and whoever opens it. It is served with `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts allow-forms allow-popups allow-downloads allow-modals` (no `allow-same-origin`) next to its other policy, so it runs in an opaque origin: its script cannot read portal pages, cannot reach the page that frames it, and no portal cookie is sent with its requests. In exchange the application has **no local storage, session storage or IndexedDB, no `document.cookie`, no service workers (as before), and no frames inside its pages (a nested frame is refused, the portal forbids being framed by an opaque origin)**.
- **The address of an isolated page holds a credential.** Because a cookie cannot carry it, the credential of an isolated page is a signed path segment: `/proxy/<id>/~pc1.<token>/...`. It is made when the connection is opened from the portal (the first request still carries your session), it is tied to your session and the connection, not to your network address (a phone that changes network, or moves between IPv4 and IPv6, keeps its open page; script in the isolated page can read its own address and could already reach everything the credential reaches from your address, so binding it would not stop that page; what it would have stopped is a copy of the address used from somewhere else, and that is what is given up for pages that keep working when the network changes), it works for 6 hours, and every request checks that the session is live and the share is still there. It is accepted by that connection's proxy and nothing else (not `/api`, not chat, not any other connection), and it is removed from logs. The page's own script can read its address, and so can send it to its author. Whoever holds a copy can use it from any network and from any website, as you and with your cookies of that connection, until the 6 hours pass, your session ends (signing out ends it) or the share is removed; it works only for that connection through the portal, but in the embedded browser that is every site you open through it. Treat the address as a password: do not paste it into chat or send it to anyone, and sign out of the sessions you are unsure of. The embedded browser's address bar shows the address without the token. **Since 1.24.2 the body of the credential is encrypted**: the address keeps its shape (`/proxy/<id>/~pc1.<body>.<mac>/...`) but is about 40 characters longer, and the page cannot read a member id, a session id or a connection id from its own address (it can still read, and pass on, the address itself, with the consequences above). A page that was open at upgrade time stops verifying once and needs one reload.
- **Cookies are kept by the portal, not by the browser, and script cannot read them.** A real Chromium 156 run showed that the requests of an opaque-origin page carry no cookie of any kind (a `SameSite=None; Secure` cookie included, for images, scripts, fetch, forms and WebSockets), so the application's cookies cannot live in the browser. The portal keeps them for the page instead: every `Set-Cookie` of an isolated page's upstream is stored on the server, per person's sign-in session, per connection and, in browser mode, per site (one site's cookies are never sent to another), and is sent back to that upstream on the requests of that page. The browser is never sent a `Set-Cookie`. Program clients (a bearer token or an API key) of an isolated connection receive no upstream `Set-Cookie` either, and the portal keeps no jar for them (the jar exists only for a page that carries the credential in its address), so a script that logs in through the proxy must keep the cookies themselves if the upstream needs a session. A login that the server keeps in a cookie (the usual HttpOnly session cookie) therefore works across pages for as long as your portal session does. It ends when you sign out or the session is revoked, when the share ends (and starts empty if the share is made again), when the portal restarts (nothing is written to disk), or after 6 hours without a request through that connection; a new sign-in starts with none. What still breaks is an application that reads a cookie from script, for example to copy a CSRF token out of one: `document.cookie` does not work in an opaque origin. The portal never sends an upstream a cookie named like its own session cookie, a cookie value over 4096 bytes is dropped, and a jar that grows past 60 cookies is emptied; a jar is only kept once it holds a cookie, a person's sessions can hold at most 40 jars (their own oldest goes first, so one person's browsing cannot push out another's logins) and the portal at most 500. A `Secure` cookie from an upstream the portal reaches over plain http is still sent back to it (as a browser on the portal's https address did before). A single-site connection matches cookies by host name. In browser mode every site (scheme, host and port) has a jar of its own, as it had its own cookie path before, so a login that sets a cookie for a parent domain on one subdomain and uses it on another (single sign-on across subdomains) does not work there. **A limit of that separation:** the jars are separate, but the credential in the address belongs to the connection and not to one site, and the answers of an isolated page carry `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *`, so sites opened in one browser-mode connection can read each other's signed-in pages: script of one site can fetch another site's pages and gets them with that site's stored cookies. Binding the credential to one site is a later feature; until then, do not open a site you do not trust in the same browser-mode connection as one you sign in to. Cookies that an upstream sets on a redirect the portal follows itself (single-site connections) are stored too. The WebSocket upgrade of an isolated page carries the stored cookies as well; before 1.24.1 it carried none. The fix that would also let script read its cookies is to serve proxied pages from a separate host name; that is a decision for the owner of the portal and is not part of this release.
- **Isolated pages can make credentialed requests to their own address.** Since 1.24.2 an isolated page (one that uses SignalR, axios with `withCredentials` or `fetch` with `credentials: 'include'`) can make credentialed requests, cookies included, to its own address: the portal answers `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: null` with credentials allowed and `Vary: Origin` when the request's `Origin` is `null` and the capability in the address verifies. The cookies belong to the connection and are kept by the portal; the portal session cookie is never sent to the connection. Requests from other origins, and requests without a valid capability, get no credentials. Proven only against local fake applications and headless Chromium, not against SignalR or axios themselves, Firefox or Safari.
- **A recipient is asked once before a shared web connection first opens** (name of the connection, who shared it, third-party application, isolated or trusted, Continue and Cancel). The choice is kept per person and connection and asked again if the connection's target changes. This also applies to connections an administrator shared: after the upgrade every recipient of a share sees the notice once. A script that calls a shared connection with a bearer token or API key is not asked.
- **Cookie values reach the upstream as it set them.** The portal's stored cookies of an isolated page are sent back byte for byte, as a browser would: a value with `=`, `/`, `,` or a space (a padded base64 session id, say) is not put in double quotes or escaped. A value the upstream itself set inside double quotes still loses those quotes, because the library that reads `Set-Cookie` drops them; that was not changed.
- **The share dialog says which connections are sandboxed.** For an administrator sharing a web connection that is not in browser mode the note now says that it is not sandboxed: its pages run on the portal's address as each person it is shared with and can act as them in the portal. For everybody else the dialog gains a note, shown whatever the connection's type, that a web connection is shown to the people it is shared with in an isolated sandbox, what that takes away, and that they are asked once before it first opens. The dialog's script is `user-connections.js` v62 (`index.html` loads it); no other script or style file changed in this respect.
- **A stale isolated page finds its way back.** When the credential in the address no longer verifies (the 6 hours ran out, the session ended, or the address belongs to another connection), a page navigation (a document or frame load, as the browser reports it) is sent back through the connection's normal address, which checks the real session and gives a fresh credential, or the login page for a signed-out member. A page's own requests (scripts, images, fetches, forms, sockets) still get the plain `401`, and the same page is sent through only once in 5 seconds, so a failing re-entry cannot loop. A tab that was already open on a shared web connection when 1.24.1 was installed has no capability in its address, so it is not a stale page of this kind: its own requests get the first-open notice page or `401` (and a page navigation is sent through the connection's entry) until the tab is navigated or the connection is opened again from the portal. Reload proxied tabs after upgrading. **Since 1.24.2:** A page left open in an isolated tab or in the embedded browser stops working after its address expires (6 hours or sign-out). Clicking a link in it makes the embedded browser reload the tab at the same page through a fresh address (the browser window ends only when `/api/me` answers `401`); in a plain tab it shows "This page expired. Reload the tab." That small `401` page is what a navigation inside a frame (an opaque-origin page) gets when it carries no session cookie; a navigation typed or followed from outside (`Sec-Fetch-Site: none`) is sent through the connection's entry as before. Proven only against fakes: the expiry in the test is simulated, not the real 6 hours or a revoked session, the upstreams are local fake applications, and only headless Chromium was run.
- **The embedded browser page of a member who was only given a connection shows the connection's name in the address bar** instead of its host and port, and holds neither in its HTML or script. Typing the name and a path, or a path, opens that path on the connection; the owner and administrators see the address as before. The pages the proxy serves under `/proxy/{id}/` (plain mode) no longer carry the connection's scheme, host and port either: the script the proxy writes into every HTML page used to hold them (`var TO`, `var FO`), and for a connection shared with a member who is not an administrator it is written without them; such a script rewrites root-relative URLs only, and the origin in the upstream's own text is still replaced by the proxy path on the server, as before. A page script that builds an absolute URL to the upstream's own origin at run time, from a value the server did not rewrite, is no longer redirected to the proxy by the injected script for that member (it was before). In browser mode (a connection that browses any site) the connection's own site is addressed for such a member as `/proxy/{id}/~/path` and not by its origin: the script the proxy writes into the page (its `X` prefix, which replaces the `O` origin constant for every viewer, the owner's page keeps the origin in `X` and behaves as before), every rewritten link, a redirect `Location`, the cookie path and the frame's address carry `~`, and the address bar shows the name and the path. A bare `/proxy/{id}/path` is served as the same site. A page of another site that the member opens keeps that site's origin in the path and the address bar, as before. Cookies the connection's site sets are kept under `/proxy/{id}/~/`, so they are not sent to other sites; a member who had a session cookie of the site under the old path is asked to log in to it again. A link the member saved with the full origin (`/proxy/{id}/https://host:port/x`) still opens. What this does not hide: an origin the upstream writes in a form the proxy does not rewrite (in JSON, in text a script builds at run time, in a response header such as `Link`, or with another spelling of the same host such as an explicit default port), and a `wss://` or `ws://` address the page opens itself; the page source and the network list of the member's browser show it.
- **An embedded browser page of a shared connection is isolated too.** In browser mode every page is isolated (see above), so the addresses above carry the credential in front of the name: `/proxy/{id}/~pc1.<token>/~/path` for a member who was only given the connection, and the address bar shows the name and the path without it. For such a page the portal keeps the cookies the connection's site sets (per site, never sent to another site), and the browser is sent none, so the sentence above about cookies kept under `/proxy/{id}/~/` describes the cookie path of a page that is not isolated; the member is asked to log in to the site again in either case. A path that is `~` or starts with `~/` is the connection's own site only in browser mode for a member who was only given the connection; everywhere else a path that starts with `~` is forwarded to the application like any other path.
- **Browser mode, a shared connection and a redirect to the same host on another scheme.** A member who was only given a browser-mode connection sees its own site as `/proxy/<id>/~/...` with no host. A redirect from that site to the same host on the other scheme at that scheme's default port (an `http` to `https` upgrade, or a site on port 8080 that sends people to `https://host/`) used to be treated as another site and put the host into the member's address bar. It now stays under an alias that holds no host: `~http/...` and `~https/...` dial that scheme at its default port (the connection's own address stays `~/...`, and a redirect back to it goes back to `~`, so an upgrade does not loop). The same host on any other explicit port, and any other host, are still other sites with their origin in the address, as before. Nothing changes for the owner or an administrator.

*Signing out*

- **Signing out needs a POST.** `GET /logout` used to sign the member out, so any link, image or navigation, including one from an isolated proxied page, could do it. It now shows a page with a **Sign out** button when there is a session (or redirects to the login page when there is none) and changes nothing; `POST /logout` signs out, as documented for scripts (`200 {"success": true}`, the push `endpoint` in the body). A cookie-authenticated POST that the browser marks cross-site, or that comes from a proxied or sandboxed page, is refused. A POST that carries no session cookie (such as a form posted from another site, which the browser sends without the cookie) ends nothing and no longer removes the cookie from the browser either. `POST /login` is judged the same way even with no cookie, and a `?token=` in its URL or a bearer header does not exempt it (it signs in by the username and password in its body, never by a token), so a form posted from another site can no longer replace a member's session cookie with a different account's (the sign-in form of the portal's own page, a JSON login and scripts that send no fetch metadata are unchanged). In the portal's own pages nothing looks different: `push.js` (now v6) turns every Logout link into that POST and goes to the login page; the sign-out page appears only when the script did not load or the POST failed (or the link is opened in a new tab). Nobody's session is ended by the upgrade itself.

*Keys, live streams and uploads (administrators, streamers and anyone with a script)*

- **A key limited to some scopes gets `403` on connections.** An administrator's key limited to one service, to `services:read` or to `admin` can no longer create, change, share or open a connection, use its file routes or its sockets, or open it through `/proxy`; a browser session, an all-scope key and a member's default key are not affected (details under Breaking changes).
- **End now and Stop relays ask first.** The Live streams tab of the admin page asks and sends the answer, so people who use the page notice nothing; a script has to send `"confirm": true`.
