- **Regenerating the key of a live stream ends the live session.** The stream settings ask the question first. The old publisher is disconnected, the relays stop, the recording is finished and kept, and the stream chat goes offline; see the entry on replacing a key below and Breaking changes for the script contract.
- **Uploads are fetched only through the signed-in route, by the file they resolve to.** `/static/uploads/<folder>/<file>` is answered to a signed-in member as before, now with `Cache-Control: private`; before, chat attachments carried no `Cache-Control` header and a shared cache such as Cloudflare's could keep a copy and hand it to anybody who had the address, also after a stream was switched to private. The addresses of existing files do not change. Under `/static/uploads/thumbnails/` the custom thumbnail of a stream that is not public is served to the owner and administrators with `Cache-Control: private, no-store`, and everybody else gets `404`, as for a file no stream names. The check judges the file an address resolves to and not how it is written, so that nothing under `/static/uploads/` is served by any route but its own: a path under the uploads folder with a dot or an empty segment gets `400`, and any other spelling that resolves to an upload gets `404` from a guard in front of the plain static route, signed in or not, for every upload (thumbnails and chat files). 1.24.0 answered such addresses to anybody, signed in or not, without the thumbnail check and without `Cache-Control: private`, and an HTML attachment reached that way was shown as a page. Custom emotes under `/static/uploads/emotes/` keep their own route: they are public by design (shared community content, served to anyone without a sign-in, with `Cache-Control: public`), and the statements above about the signed-in route do not apply to them. A Cloudflare cache that already holds a thumbnail of a stream that was private, or a file fetched through one of those other addresses, keeps it until it is purged: purge the whole cache of the site once after upgrading, not only `/static/uploads/thumbnails/*`, because the other spellings are different addresses. Cloudflare was not in the test, so how it treats those addresses is not known here.
- **A chat attachment sent as `application/octet-stream` keeps its file extension only when the extension is on the allowed list** (the one `POST /api/chat/upload` already used to accept other types: documents, archives, code and configuration files, audio, video). Any other name (`.html`, `.svg`, `.xml`, `.js`, `.docx`, `.exe` and so on) is still accepted but is stored, and so linked and downloaded, as `<random>.bin`; the original name is still shown and returned as `filename`. 1.24.0 kept any extension of up to 10 letters or digits. Attachments already stored under such an extension stay on disk and keep their links; the portal's own upload route only ever sends them as downloads.

*Stored secrets in the connection forms*

- **The edit form shows an empty field and "Stored - leave empty to keep it" for every stored secret.** The connection form never receives a stored secret now (it is write-only, see Breaking changes), so saving with an empty field keeps what is stored, and the GitHub, VNC, Proxmox, SPICE and VPN secret inputs are password fields.
- **A SPICE or Proxmox connection's own stored password is cleared when its type, host or port changes** and the same request carries no new one; the answer then has `stored_password_cleared: true`, as for SSH. An edit that moves nothing keeps it. A Proxmox connection's **API token** (`api_token`) is cleared in the same cases, so a moved Proxmox connection needs its token or password typed again; the answer says `stored_password_cleared` when either was removed. The owner must type the password or token again after moving the connection (they used to be sent to the new target). Port is included as for the SSH rule and the host key pin.
- **Every stored secret of every connection type is cleared when the connection is moved.** The rule above was only for SPICE and Proxmox; an http or embedded-browser connection kept its `auth_header` and `extra_headers` when `config.target_url` changed, a GitHub connection kept its tokens and a SPICE connection kept its password when `tls_port` changed, so whoever could edit the connection could make the portal send a stored secret to a host of their choosing. Now a change of anything that decides where the portal dials removes every stored secret of that connection (`password`, `private_key`, `auth_header`, `extra_headers`, `api_token`, `personal_token`, `client_secret`, `webhook_secret`, `pre_shared_key`, `psk`) that the same request does not send again: the row's `host` or `port`, the scheme, host or port of `target_url`, `api_url`, `webrtc_url` or `hls_url`, `tls_port`, the `tls` switch, and a change of type to one handled by another plugin (http to grafana keeps them, http to vnc does not). A changed path or query of a URL, a rename and any other edit keep them. The answer lists what was removed as `stored_secrets_cleared` (for example `["auth_header", "extra_headers"]`) and the edit form shows it; `stored_password_cleared: true` keeps its meaning (a stored SSH password was removed, for any reason) and is now also answered when a moved connection of another type loses its `password` or `api_token`; `stored_secrets_cleared` is answered only for a move. What a member will notice: moving a connection to another host now means typing its token or key again, **including the private key pasted into an SSH connection** (before, only its stored password was removed, and a key is not handed to the host the way a password is; the rule is one rule for every stored secret; a key chosen from the key library on the SSH Keys tab is not stored in the connection and keeps working). Creating a connection stores what it is sent. There is no import route; the one helper is `connection_secrets.clear_on_move`. The same holds while the connection is in use: in browser mode, the stored `auth_header` and `extra_headers` are sent only to the connection's own site (same scheme, host and port as its configured address). A page of another site, another port of the same host, and the same host on the other scheme or its default port (the `~http` and `~https` addresses of a shared connection) get none of them; before, every site the embedded browser was on received the stored `Authorization` header, and `~http` sent an https connection's secret unencrypted. An existing browser-mode connection that relied on its header for a second site will get that site without it (a second site needs its own connection); the connection's own site, by any of its addresses, is unchanged. A connection that is not in browser mode is unchanged.

*Page libraries and the content security policy (members, and administrators with custom pages)*

- **Page scripts no longer come from a CDN.** Before, a member's browser fetched hls.js, Chart.js, xterm.js (and its fit addon), highlight.js, marked, DOMPurify and noVNC from `cdn.jsdelivr.net`, without an integrity check on all but Chart.js, and a mistake or compromise there would have run code in the portal's origin (on the terminal page, which carries the administrator's shell, too). Now they are served from your server and nothing is fetched from a third party for them; loading the libraries no longer tells the CDN which portal page was opened. That is about the libraries only: the GitHub page shows a repository's README through marked and DOMPurify and `img-src` allows any https host, so viewing a repository makes the browser fetch every image host that README names (badges, screenshots).
- **The policy is stricter.** `https://cdn.jsdelivr.net` is removed from `script-src`, `style-src` and `font-src` of the policy every portal page gets; no host is added and nothing else in the policy changed. A self-hoster with a custom page, or a patched copy of a portal page, that loaded a library from jsDelivr will see the browser refuse it; put the library under your own `static/` and load it from there. The policies of proxied sites (they never named the CDN) and of the overlay page (own origin only) are unchanged.
- **Still contacts other hosts, on purpose and not part of this change:** YouTube embeds in chat (`frame-src` names `www.youtube.com` and `www.youtube-nocookie.com`), link-preview and other https images in chat (`img-src` allows any https host), the STUN server of voice calls (`STUN_SERVER`, Google's by default), the push services of the members' browsers, the GitHub releases API, the Cloudflare address list and the other server-side requests listed under Security.
- **Nothing has to be done for the page libraries.** There is no setting, `.env` entry, command or database change for them. `git pull` brings the files; the policy change is in `server.py`, so it takes effect when the server restarts.
- **Versions.** hls.js 1.5.17, xterm.js 5.3.0 (the copy of 1.20.0, now also used by the terminal page), xterm-addon-fit 0.8.0, Chart.js 4.4.7, highlight.js 11.12.0, marked 18.3.0, DOMPurify 3.5.0 and noVNC 1.4.0. Each came once from `registry.npmjs.org` as the npm tarball and was compared with the registry's sha512 before the files were copied unchanged. `static/vendor/<library>/README.md` gives the version, the source URL and the sha256 of every file, and `tests/test_vendor_libs.py` checks them. Only the minified build and the licence files are included (noVNC: the 45 modules `core/rfb.js` imports, in the package's own directory layout).
- **Three of the versions are a choice, not a copy.** The pages named marked, DOMPurify (`@3`) and highlight.js (`gh/highlightjs/cdn-release@11`) without a full version, so what a browser got depended on the day. Today's newest release of each was vendored (marked 18.3.0, DOMPurify 3.5.0, highlight.js 11.12.0 from its npm twin `@highlightjs/cdn-assets`); the bytes were not compared with what the CDN served, because the CDN was not contacted.
- **Two libraries most likely did not load before, and now do or are gone.** (1) marked: the page asked for `marked/marked.min.js`, which the 18.x package does not contain (it ships `lib/marked.umd.js`), so the README view of the GitHub page probably failed with `marked is not defined`; it now renders Markdown (sanitised by DOMPurify as before). This was not verified against the CDN, only inferred from the package. (2) The QR code script of the dashboard (`qrcode@1.5.3/build/qrcode.min.js`): that npm package ships no `build/` directory, so the URL had nothing to serve. The two-factor setup already showed the secret key and hid the QR canvas in that case. 1.24.1 removes the script tag and does not add that library; the setup dialog draws the code with a small encoder of the portal's own instead (see the two-factor entry below).
- **Chart.js lost its integrity attribute.** The old tag carried a sha384 of the CDN's own minified copy of the file; the vendored `chart.umd.js` is the package's file (already minified) and a different byte string. The file is now served from the same origin, so the attribute was dropped, as for the xterm copy of 1.20.0.
- **Version numbers in the addresses.** The pages ask for `/static/vendor/<library>/<file>?v=<library version>`. noVNC's other modules are imported by relative path and carry no `?v=`; after a future noVNC upgrade Cloudflare may serve an old module for up to 4 hours.
- **First visit.** A browser fetches the files from your server instead of from the CDN, so a first view of a page costs a few hundred kilobytes more from your own link (hls.js 414 KB, Chart.js 206 KB, noVNC 547 KB over 45 requests, before any compression). Cloudflare caches `/static/*` as before.

*Two-factor setup*

- **The two-factor setup shows its QR code again.** The picture came from a script on a CDN that served nothing for a long time, and the third-party script tag was removed (see the libraries entries above). The setup dialog now draws it with a small encoder of its own, `static/js/qr.js` (v1; no library, no network; `index.html` loads it before `admin.js`, now v41). It handles an address of up to 213 bytes (QR versions 1 to 10, error correction level M), which holds a normal `otpauth://` address with room to spare; a longer one, or a page where the script did not load, shows only the secret key with a line saying to enter it by hand, as the dialog always allowed. The picture is black on white with a quiet border whatever the theme. It was checked against published Reed-Solomon, format and version values and an independent decoder written for the test; **no phone camera or scanner app was tried**, so scan it once after upgrading and use the key if it does not read.

*Calls and answers from proxied applications (administrators who proxy applications)*

- **Some calls from a proxied page now answer `403`.** A call that changes something (`POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH`, `DELETE`) or opens a socket (chat, terminal, admin) and carries the session cookie is refused when the browser says it is cross-site (`Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site`), when its `Origin` is `null`, when a browser that sends no fetch headers sends another site's `Origin`, or when its `Referer` is a page under `/proxy/`, `/browser` or `/search`. The portal's own pages are not affected. What was considered: the portal's pages (same-origin, a portal `Referer`); the embedded browser's own controls (they only read `/api/me`); the sign-out and push calls that the portal's script makes on a `/search` page (allowed from `/search` only, for `/logout` and `/api/push/`); OBS and the chat overlay (token-authenticated, `/ws/overlay` is not judged); API clients (a bearer token, API key or `?token=` that actually authenticated the request is never refused, a bad bearer next to a cookie is judged as the cookie, and a client that sends none of these headers is not affected); what an application posts to its own `/proxy/` address is not judged either. **This is a seat belt, not a wall, for trusted pages:** a script that runs on the portal's own address can still call the portal without a `Referer` and open the portal's sockets as the viewer. What keeps untrusted pages away is the sandbox above; a trusted page is trusted, which is why only administrators' connections, the viewer's own and superadmin-marked ones are. **Since 1.24.2 the same refusal also applies to a state change that carries the session cookie and an `Origin` naming another hostname of the portal's own domain** (a sibling subdomain, such as `other.example.com` for a portal at `portal.example.com`). A browser calls such a request same-site, so it was let through before, and a sign-in form posted from a page there could replace a member's session cookie. It now answers `403` `Cross-site request refused`; the refusal is logged with the reason 'same-site foreign origin' (a new wording), and the log line for a refused cross-site `/login` POST now reads 'Refused a cross-site sign-in POST (...)' where it read 'Refused a cookie-authenticated POST /login (...)', so a log alert rule that matches the old `/login` text must be updated. Other refusals keep the old wording. The portal's own pages, requests that send no `Origin` (curl, scripts, OBS) and the configured `HOSTNAME` and `STREAM_HOSTNAME` are not affected. Because a sibling subdomain is same-site with the portal, it must not host content that members control: keep member-controlled content off other hostnames of the portal's domain.
- **An upstream can no longer plant or wipe portal cookies.** A cookie named like the portal session (in any letter case) is dropped from the proxy's and the search proxy's answers, and the session cookie is removed from the `Cookie` header sent upstream under any spelling; a cookie path with a dot segment is treated as missing; `Clear-Site-Data` is not passed on. Other cookie names, `__Secure-` ones included, are the application's own and still pass on a trusted page.
- **Response headers that act on the whole portal address are no longer passed on.** An answer from a proxied application (`/proxy`, `/browser` and `/search`) is stripped of `Strict-Transport-Security`, `Public-Key-Pins`, `Public-Key-Pins-Report-Only`, `Expect-CT`, `NEL`, `Report-To` (error reports of every portal request would go to the upstream's collector), `Origin-Agent-Cluster`, `Accept-CH`, `Accept-CH-Lifetime` and `Critical-CH` (the browser would send client hints to the portal on every request), `Alt-Svc`, and, as before, `Clear-Site-Data` and `Service-Worker-Allowed`. A browser stores each of these per address, so the portal's own value is the only one that may stand. An application that relied on one of them for its own pages gets the portal's behaviour instead; nothing else a member sees changes. `Permissions-Policy` is still passed on: it governs the one page it comes with, and a trusted page runs as part of the portal anyway.

*Trust marks (superadmin)*

- `PUT /api/admin/connections/{id}/proxy-trust` with `{"trusted": true}` marks a member's web connection trusted (not in browser mode, never): its pages then run on the portal's address for everybody who opens it. `GET /api/admin/proxy-trust-connections` lists the members' web connections with their mark and shows no host, port or configuration. Both are superadmin routes and the change is audited (`connection.proxy_trust`, `connection.proxy_untrust`). The mark is tied to the connection's type, host, port, target URL and browser-mode flag: a change of any of them ends it (it is also removed when the connection is edited). **Admin > Settings > Shared web connections** (superadmin only, `proxy-trust-admin.js` v1) lists the web connections that members have shared (owner, name, kind, how many people it is shared with, and whether it is trusted; never a host, port or stored secret) with **Trust** and **Remove trust**. Each asks first on a row of its own, away from the button that was clicked, ignores a double click, and cannot be repeated while a request is in flight. A connection in browser mode has no Trust button.

*Smaller changes (members, moderators, administrators)*

- **Webhooks of a demoted administrator.** Delivery used to ask only whether the hook was enabled, so a member who stopped being an administrator kept receiving the events the hook was subscribed to. Now the owner's role is read at every send, so a role changed by the admin page, by `server.py set-admin --remove` or by anything else stops delivery at once, and every path of the portal that lowers the role also switches the owner's hooks off. At start the portal switches off the hooks whose owner is not an administrator. System hooks have no owner and are not touched. Hooks that were already off stay as they are. The test button (`POST /api/webhooks/{id}/test`) follows the same rule: it answers `400` and sends nothing when the hook's owner is no longer an administrator (before, it checked only the caller and still delivered). A hook of an administrator is tested as before.
- **Hooks of a member who is no longer an administrator are switched off** at the first start (and at every demotion from now on). They are kept, not deleted, and show the reason "The owner is no longer an administrator". Make the member an administrator again and switch the hook on, or delete it.
- **Replacing a stream key ends the live session.** A key replaced while the stream is live used to leave the old publisher connected on the old path. Now the replacement ends the session the way End now does: the publisher is disconnected on the path the session was published on (also if the key was replaced earlier), the relays stop, the recording is finished and kept, and the stream chat goes offline. The disconnect hook finds the stream by the session's remembered path as well as by the current key, so an encoder that stays connected after a replacement no longer confuses the state. Sockets that were opened with the old key are closed, both live and offline. A socket that carries a stream key as its credential is no longer treated as valid (no route signs a stream key in, so none should exist).
- **Viewers who lose access are disconnected from WebRTC playback.** A ban, a stream turned private, a replaced share link, a deleted stream, a deleted account and an account demoted to a role that can no longer read the stream used to cut off HLS and the chat but not a WebRTC (WHEP) playback already flowing from MediaMTX. Now the portal asks its own MediaMTX to disconnect those sessions (`POST /v3/webrtcsessions/kick/{id}`; for a private stream and a deleted stream it also lists the sessions on the stream's paths, so a viewer pushed out of the portal's own record, which holds 64 per stream, is found too). The owner's own playback survives a stream turned private. A demotion (`PUT /api/users/{id}/role`) disconnects only the account's sessions on streams it can no longer read under its new role (an administrator demoted to a member loses private streams of others; a public stream stays watchable); a deletion disconnects all of the account's sessions, including those on other people's streams. A viewer who is still watching when this happens is cut off from the media at once instead of when they close the tab. It is a background task, never delayed or failed by MediaMTX; nothing is contacted by a staged instance. **Two gaps in this are closed.** (1) A deleted stream or account is now found in MediaMTX by the stream's key, read before the row goes, so a viewer the portal has no record of (after a restart, for example) is disconnected too; before, the paths were known only from the portal's records and such a viewer kept playing. (2) When a stream turns private, everybody who may still watch it is kept, decided by the one watch rule from their role now: the owner and administrators (before, an administrator's session was disconnected with the rest, and the owner's was kept only if its record had MediaMTX's session id). What is not covered: a kept member's session whose record has no MediaMTX id cannot be told apart in MediaMTX's list (it names no member), so in that case the portal does not disconnect sessions it has no record of and only disconnects the recorded sessions of members who lost access; and after a portal restart no session has a record, so a stream turned private then disconnects every playback session on it, the owner's and administrators' too (their players reconnect, as they may watch).
- **Deleting an account deletes the chat channel of each of its streams**, as deleting the stream does (a default channel, or one another stream still uses, stays). Before, the channels stayed as ordinary public channels with the history of a stream that may have been private. **Channels already left behind are dealt with once at the first start, repeatably:** only a channel that is not a default one, that no stream uses, whose name is `stream-<username>-<stream id>` with a stream id that no longer exists, with the description "Chat for <stream> by <username>" and whose creator is gone is touched. It is made **private** (administrators still read it, the history is kept, nothing is deleted) and listed in the log and in the setting `orphaned_stream_channels`; there is no page for the list, an administrator finds the channels by their names among the private channels and deletes or keeps them. **Each channel is handled once:** its id and name are recorded in the setting `orphaned_stream_channels_handled`, and no later start touches its visibility again, so a channel an administrator made public again on purpose stays public. A channel that does not match all of this is left alone.
- **Failed sign-ins are counted per exact address, not per IPv6 /64, and rotating through a /64 is not limited.** This is unchanged from 1.24.0 and is stated here because it is a known gap in the optional failed sign-in blocking (off by default). One customer holds a whole /64 and can use a new address for each attempt, so such a client is never blocked by this feature; a block is per exact address, and counting a /64 together would only block the one address that reached the limit while the rest stayed free. Blocking a whole /64 needs a new kind of block and is not part of 1.24.1.
- **A push device on a session token with no expiry goes stale like any other.** Before, such a device (a token that never expires) was never stale. Now one that no session has announced for 30 days gets nothing, and the periodic cleanup deletes it; a phone that opens the portal signed in at least once a month keeps working, and a device on a token that expires later is not affected.
- **6to4 and Teredo addresses are no longer exempt from automatic blocks.** Python calls `2002::/16` and `2001::/32` private; anybody on the internet can send from them. The block list's built-in "private" entry says so. Loopback, private, link-local, own, trusted-proxy, Cloudflare and never-ban addresses are exempt as before. No blocking feature is turned on by this.
- **The API key list has no inline handler.** The Revoke and Delete buttons carry the key's id only; the name is read from the loaded list and shown through `textContent`. Before, a quote or a backslash in a key name (typed by its own owner) could break out of the script string in the page of that owner.
- **The MediaMTX plugin tells a viewer no address.** When the media server cannot be reached the viewer's socket receives the fixed sentence "Failed to connect to MediaMTX"; before it also carried the exception text with the host and port. The detail is in the log.
- **A stream owner or stream moderator is no longer refused on an anonymous post because of who wrote it.** Deleting, timing out or banning by message id, and lifting a ban made from an anonymous post (over the socket and over `DELETE /api/streams/{id}/bans/id/{ban_id}`), are allowed on every anonymous post of that stream chat, whoever wrote it, as the range prune already treated them. Before, a refusal told the actor the hidden author was the owner, another moderator or staff. The consequence: a stream moderator can now time out or ban the author of an anonymous post even if that author is the owner or another moderator; the ban is recorded, but the chat's read check skips the stream's owner and administrators (as the playback check always did), so it does not keep either of them out of the chat; this also means a stream ban recorded against an administrator by another route no longer locks that administrator out of the chat, which 1.24.0 did; a timeout a stream moderator placed through an anonymous post does not bind the stream's owner or an administrator either (the row is recorded and the room is told as for anybody, the owner still gets the "timed out" notification, but the owner's and the administrator's messages, polls and screen shares go through), because the owner has no route to lift a timeout of up to 30 days; a timeout placed by a global moderator or administrator binds as before, and so does any timeout on an ordinary member. A moderator can infer from this that the author was the owner or an administrator when the same anonymous handle keeps posting after the timeout. A timeout placed through an anonymous post by a stream owner or stream moderator no longer ends the author's voice call at that moment, whoever the author is: no one is dropped, so no departure and no new members list reveal them, and a call the author is in goes on until they leave it; the timeout is read when they next try to join the voice chat, where a member is refused as for any timeout, while the stream's owner, its stream mods, administrators and global moderators are let in as always (anyone with a moderation level in that chat). A timeout placed by a global moderator or administrator, or on a named post, still ends the call at once. When a ban made from an anonymous post puts its author out of the room, the departure is announced as an anonymous participant's (no name, no id for the moderator's socket), also when the author was in the room under their name. Not hidden: the room's member list the moderator receives right after no longer holds the author, so a moderator who compared the list before and after can still see who left. Likewise, no departure follows a ban made from the anonymous post of the owner or an administrator (a ban does not remove either), so a moderator who sees no departure after a ban knows the author was absent from the room or was the owner or an administrator. Global moderators and administrators are given the author's identity and keep the rank rules. An action on a named member keeps them for everybody, and nobody acts on their own post this way. A timeout that would replace a penalty the actor may not touch is still refused with the same text as before, which does depend on the existing penalty and not on the author's rank.
- **The anonymous handle changes each time anonymous mode is switched on again.** The handle (`anon_ref`) stays the same while one anonymous period lasts and is different in the next, so two anonymous periods of one member in a channel cannot be matched by other members. A client that cached a handle across a switch sees it change; the handle was already different after a restart.
- **The owner's health dot follows the owner's own filing.** An owned connection shows its status only when the owner pinned it or opened it in the last 7 days. A grantee's pin still makes the portal probe the connection (and the grantee sees the status), but the owner's list shows "unknown" until the owner's own pin or use qualifies. An owner who relied on a grantee's pin to see a dot sees none now.
- **A member whose message could not be checked by a slow automod rule is told only that auto-moderation blocked it.** Error code `automod` and "Message blocked by auto-moderation" (an edit: "Edit blocked by auto-moderation; your message was not changed"), with no rule name; 1.24.0 said "could not be checked". The message is still rejected and no penalty is booked, as before; a rule whose action is a timeout does not time the member out when it only overran. Moderators and administrators, who may read the rules, still get `automod_unchecked` ("could not be checked"). What a member can still infer: an ordinary block names its rule and this answer does not, and a member whose message overran is refused every message for 60 seconds, a short "hello" included (the member's breaker, as in 1.24.0), with the same text. So a probe with long text can still tell that some check could not finish for them, but no longer which rule it was or that a rule of that name exists; the answer is not indistinguishable from normal delivery, which would mean letting an unchecked message through.
- **Reactions made while anonymous stay anonymous.** `chat_reactions` has a new flag set when the reaction is made. History, threads, pins and search read it: the reactor's id is left out of `reactions[].user_ids` for everybody but the reactor and staff, also after the member switches anonymous off, and the live `reaction_update` frame names nobody when it is made and when it is taken back. A reactor who is anonymous at the moment is still hidden, as before. The migration marks the existing reactions of members who are anonymous at the time of the first start; reactions made earlier by a member who had already switched anonymous off cannot be recovered and stay as they are. No route that exports chat messages was found, so there is no export to mask.
- **Stored go-live and reminder notifications are withdrawn.** When a stream is not public after an update, its `stream_live` and `stream_reminder` notifications go for everybody but the owner and administrators; when a member is banned from a stream (HTTP route, websocket ban by account id or by message id) that member's own go. The match is on the stream id inside the notification, in batches of 500. An open page keeps what it already shows until it is reloaded, a push that was already sent is not recalled, and a go-live card in a shared announce channel is still removed only when the stream turns private, not for one banned member. Notifications stored before this version go the next time the stream is edited while private or when the member is banned.
- **The go-live activity row is written from the stream as it is at that moment.** A rename or a switch to private between the start of the go-live and the row is logged under the present name and the private type. The offline row and its webhook already read the stream again, and webhook delivery already re-checks that the stream is public, so nothing else was changed there.
- **The SQL driver's own logger is held at INFO.** Whatever the portal's log level, `aiosqlite` no longer prints statements with their parameter values (calendar feed token hashes, message text) through the portal's handlers.
- **No default of a blocking feature changes.** Failed sign-in blocking is still optional and off, scanner blocking is still off unless you set `PROBE_BLOCK_STRIKES`.

*What stays as it was*

- **An administrator's own applications are unchanged**: a connection owned by an administrator and not in browser mode runs as before, with its cookies, forms, redirects, WebSockets and the existing refusal of service workers (`worker-src 'none'`), for administrators and for the members it is shared with. A member's own connection, opened by that member, is also as before (the upstream can only act as the person who set it up). Both facts were checked against a stand-in application, not the roughly 25 applications of a production portal.
- **Paths that start with `~`** (an Apache or nginx user directory such as `/~alice/index.html`, or an application's own routes) are forwarded to the upstream like any other path, on trusted and isolated pages alike. Only a segment of exactly the minted shape (`~pc1.`, a base64url body, a dot, a 24-character signature) is taken for an isolated page's credential; a well-formed one that does not verify is still a plain `401`. On an isolated page such a path is entered through the same redirect as any other, and keeps its place after the credential.
- **SearXNG at `/search`** is a portal service and stays on the portal's address. Its result links open in a new tab, or in the embedded browser's own tab in browser mode, which is isolated. Its SVG, XHTML and XML answers keep the 1.19.0 policy.

*Known and not changed*

- The WebSocket sockets of the portal are open to a trusted page's script as the viewer (see the seat belt above).
- A page that opens a popup gets a sandboxed popup (it inherits the sandbox).
- The search proxy now passes on every `Set-Cookie` line of SearXNG (it used to keep one). When a connection has `forwarded_host` on, the `X-Forwarded-Prefix` sent upstream for an isolated page contains its token. In the embedded browser the safety net that sends a frame that left the proxy back into it reads the target from the frame's address and does not know the token segment; this was not fixed.

**What you should do after upgrading**

- **Before upgrading, only if one of these applies to you:** a script or API key of yours calls `POST /api/admin/streams/{id}/end` or `.../stop-relays` (add `"confirm": true`), regenerates the key of a stream that may be live (send `{"end_live_session": true}`), reads a stored connection secret back from the API (it cannot any more), signs out with `GET /logout` (use `POST`), or a webhook with `allow_private` is aimed at MediaMTX or another service the portal runs on this machine (it is refused after the upgrade; list the destination in `.env` as `WEBHOOK_LOCAL_ALLOW` if you really want it). A custom page that loaded a library from jsDelivr needs the library under your own `static/`. Take your usual backup first (`sudo venv/bin/python server.py backup-db`); the server has to be restarted for the changes to take effect.
- **Purge the Cloudflare cache once** (or whatever cache sits in front of the portal): the whole cache of the site is the safe choice and `/static/uploads/*` is the least that helps. An old copy of a private stream's thumbnail or of a chat file stays reachable from the cache until it is purged.
- **Look at the webhooks listed as disabled.** The hooks of members who are no longer administrators were switched off at the first start and show the reason "The owner is no longer an administrator". They are kept, not deleted: make the member an administrator again and switch the hook on, or delete it. Also check the hooks you aimed at services on this machine (see above).
- **Re-enter secrets that were cleared.** The upgrade itself clears nothing. From now on, moving a connection to another host, port or URL origin removes every stored secret the same edit does not send again (an SSH private key pasted into the connection included); the edit answer names what was removed, and the owner types them again. Nobody can read a stored secret back, so keep your own copy of the ones you may need to re-enter.
- **Reload proxied tabs after upgrading.** A tab that was open on a shared web connection or in the embedded browser when 1.24.1 was installed has no capability in its address; reload it, or open the connection again from the portal, and tell members who keep such tabs open to do the same.
- **Tell your members that shared web applications now open isolated.** The first time somebody opens a shared web connection a notice asks them to continue (administrators' shares included, once per person and connection); in the isolated page the application has no local storage, no `document.cookie` and no frames, and its cookie logins are kept by the portal; they should open a connection again from the portal when its address stops working. Everything in browser mode is isolated, whoever owns the connection.
- **Review the trust list and what you proxy.** Nothing is marked trusted after the upgrade. A superadmin can look at **Admin > Settings > Shared web connections**, which lists the web connections members have shared. **A trusted page is trusted:** an application an administrator proxies, and a member's own connection opened by that member, runs with that person's session on the portal's own address, and so does a marked connection for everybody who opens it. So proxy only applications you trust, and mark nothing trusted unless you trust that application as much as you trust an administrator.
- **Scan the new two-factor QR code once** with the authenticator app you use (the secret key is still shown beside it); no phone camera was tried during the work.

**Breaking changes**

One group, for API clients and scripts. Everything under it is also described under What you will notice where a person sees it.

- **End now and Stop relays need `"confirm": true`.** `POST /api/admin/streams/{id}/end` and `.../stop-relays` answer `400` ("This action needs {"confirm": true} in the request body") for a bare POST, an empty body or a body without it, and nothing happens. The Live streams tab of the admin page sends it with the answer to its question, so people who use the page notice nothing. A script that sent `{}` or `{"regenerate_key": true}` must add `"confirm": true`.
- **`regenerate_key` of End now no longer defaults to true.** Before, an absent value replaced the stream key. Now only `"regenerate_key": true` does; the admin page still sends the state of its box (checked by default), so the page behaves as before. A script that relied on the default must send `true`.
- **Regenerating the stream key of a live stream asks first.** `POST /api/streams/{id}/regenerate-key` on a live stream answers `409` with `"live": true` and changes nothing, unless the body is `{"end_live_session": true}`. The stream settings ask the question and send it. An offline stream is changed as before and needs no body. A script that regenerates the key of a stream that may be live must send the field.
- **A key limited to some scopes can no longer change or use a connection.** Create, update, delete, pin, prefs, share, unshare and `/connect` under `/api/connections`, and the three ways that actually use the stored credentials (the `/api/sftp/{conn_id}/...` file routes, the `/ws/user-connection/{uuid}` socket, also reached as `/ws/terminal/{uuid}` and the like, and the reverse proxy `/proxy/{conn_id}` and `/proxy/{conn_id}/...`, which sends an HTTP connection's stored `auth_header` and `extra_headers` upstream), now need a credential with the user's whole authority, as the host key route already did: a browser session, an all-scope (`*`) token or key, or a member's default key (`services:read` and `access:*`, which is the same as the member's own session and is not affected). An administrator's key limited to one service, to `services:read` or to `admin` gets `403` there (the socket is closed with code `4003`; `/proxy/...` answers `403`, also to a WebSocket upgrade, instead of passing the request upstream). Reading (`GET`) is unchanged, and a browser session, which is what the pages use for `/proxy/...`, is not affected. Reason: a connection holds stored credentials, and a narrow key could repoint or share it.
- **Hooks of a demoted administrator.** An incoming hook of such an owner answers `404` at its token address, and `PUT` with `enabled: true` on a hook of an owner who is not an administrator is refused with `409`.
- **A webhook with `allow_private` cannot reach the portal's own services.** It already could not reach the portal's own listening ports; now it also cannot reach, on loopback and on this host's own addresses, a port that a managed service row names (its port, its ports list and the `*_port` keys of its config: the MediaMTX control, HLS, WebRTC and RTSP ports, the search backend) or on which a child process of the portal listens (MediaMTX, the search backend). It is checked when the hook is saved and at every connection. An existing hook aimed at such a port is refused at delivery, counts as a failure and, after 10 in a row, disables itself. **The rule:** if you really want such a destination, list it in `.env` as `WEBHOOK_LOCAL_ALLOW=127.0.0.1:8081` (comma or space separated `address:port` entries, IP addresses only, an IPv6 address in brackets), read when the portal starts; it lets through only that exact address and port and never the portal's own port. A service on this machine that is neither a child of the portal nor a managed service row (for example a ntfy server run by systemd) is still reachable, as before.
- **Every stored secret of a connection is write-only (breaking, for a client that read them).** `GET /api/connections`, `GET /api/connections/{id}` and `/connect` used to return, in clear, a Proxmox `api_token`, an http connection's `extra_headers` (the header values), a GitHub `personal_token`, `client_secret` and `webhook_secret`, and a VPN `pre_shared_key` to any credential of the owner, including a key limited to read access; only `password`, `private_key`, `private_key_path`, `auth_header` and `psk` were hidden. Now every one of them is replaced by `has_<field>: true` (no flag when the field is empty), in the list, the details and the connect answer, and there is no other route that returns a connection's config. A connection's own form shows an empty field with the hint "Stored - leave empty to keep it" and an empty field is not sent, so saving keeps what is stored; to change a secret, send a new value. A `has_<field>` flag sent back in a config is dropped, not stored. The GitHub, VNC, Proxmox, SPICE and VPN secret inputs of the form are now password fields, and `/api/connections/types` marks those schema fields `writeOnly`. The list of keys is in `connection_secrets.py`; a test reads every plugin's config schema, so a field added later that looks like a credential fails until it is listed. A script that read one of these values back from the portal (a backup script, for example) no longer can; nothing else changes for it, the value is still used when the connection is opened.
- **A moved connection answers `stored_secrets_cleared`.** The edit of a connection whose host, port or target moved answers the list of stored secrets removed (see the entry above); `stored_password_cleared: true` keeps its meaning.
- **`GET /logout` no longer signs out.** It shows a page with a Sign out button or redirects to the login page; `POST /logout` signs out as documented (`200 {"success": true}`). A cookie-authenticated `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH` or `DELETE`, or a socket upgrade, that the browser marks cross-site or that comes from a proxied or sandboxed page answers `403`; a bearer token, API key or `?token=` is not judged this way.
- **A stream thumbnail of a stream that is not public answers `404` to everybody but the owner and administrators**, and uploads answer only through `/static/uploads/<folder>/<file>` (see Uploads above). A chat attachment sent as `application/octet-stream` with an extension that is not on the allowed list is stored as `<random>.bin`; `filename` still returns the original name.
- **A member whose message overran an auto-moderation rule gets error code `automod`** ("Message blocked by auto-moderation") with no rule name; staff still get `automod_unchecked`.
- **The handle of an anonymous member changes** each time anonymous mode is switched on again (see the entry above).

**Database, static files, rollback and what was not tested**

- **Database.** Added at start, idempotently, and cheap on a large database: the column `chat_reactions.anonymous` (default 0), the columns `user_connections.proxy_trusted_fp` and `user_connections.proxy_trusted_by`, the table `proxy_share_notice` (which person accepted the first-open notice for which connection and target; it is removed with the connection or the account), and the settings rows `reaction_anonymous_marked`, `orphaned_stream_channels` and `orphaned_stream_channels_handled`. Three passes run at start and repeat harmlessly: one `UPDATE` over the `webhooks` table that switches off the hooks whose owner is not an administrator (system hooks have no owner and are not touched, hooks that were off stay as they are); a one-time `UPDATE` over the reactions of the members who are anonymous at that moment, guarded by `reaction_anonymous_marked` (its time on a 250 MB database was not measured; reactions made earlier by a member who had already switched anonymous off cannot be recovered and stay as they are); and the orphaned channel pass, which reads the channels whose name starts with `stream-` and whose creator is gone, looks up the stream of each match, makes the matches that were not handled before private, records them in `orphaned_stream_channels_handled` (id and name, at most 5000) and writes `orphaned_stream_channels` (every match, handled or not), a few rows on a normal database. The two `user_connections` columns hold the superadmin's trust mark of a connection (the type, host, port and target it was given for, and who gave it); the cookies an isolated page's application sets are kept in memory only and never written. There is one optional `.env` entry, `WEBHOOK_LOCAL_ALLOW` (empty by default), and no new Python dependency. The signed address of an isolated page is signed with the portal's `JWT_SECRET`, so changing that secret ends every open isolated page (a page navigation re-enters through the portal).
- **Static files.** The pages now load: `user-connections.js` v62 (the share dialog's notes and the API key buttons; `index.html`), `streams.js` v54 (the key regeneration question; `index.html`), `live-ops-admin.js` v4 (it sends `confirm`; `admin.html`), `push.js` v6 (the Logout link posts; every page that loads it), `admin.js` v41 (the two-factor dialog draws its QR code; `index.html`), `qr.js` v1 (new, the QR encoder; `index.html` loads it before `admin.js`), `proxy-trust-admin.js` v1 (new, the trust screen; `admin.html`) and `shared-app.js` v1 (new, the first-open notice; `shared-app.html`). The libraries are files under `static/vendor/<library>/` loaded with `?v=<library version>`: hls.js 1.5.17, xterm.js 5.3.0 and xterm-addon-fit 0.8.0, Chart.js 4.4.7, highlight.js 11.12.0, marked 18.3.0, DOMPurify 3.5.0 and noVNC 1.4.0. Pages that changed and are served without a version: `about.html`, `admin.html`, `api-docs.html`, `browser.html`, `chat.html`, `files.html`, `github.html`, `guides.html`, `index.html`, `live.html`, `streams.html`, `terminal.html`, `vnc.html`, `vod-player.html` and `watch.html`; new are `logout.html` (the sign-out page) and `shared-app.html` (the first-open notice). No other script or style file changed, so `portal.css`, `theme.js` and the rest keep their versions. A folder under `static/uploads/` (`chat`, `thumbnails`) that is a symbolic link to another disk keeps working: its files are served as before; a link inside it that leads out of that folder to somewhere else answers `404`.
- **Rollback.** 1.24.0 starts on the migrated database and ignores what 1.24.1 added (the three columns, the table and the three settings rows), so rolling back needs no database step: 1.24.0 starts on the same database. What is lost on 1.24.0: every proxied page runs as it did (an application's page on the portal's own address with the viewer's session, the isolated pages, the first-open notice and the trust marks no longer exist); uploads are served as in 1.24.0 (the other spellings answer again and chat attachments carry no `Cache-Control: private`); stored connection secrets are returned in clear again, and a moved connection sends them to the new target; connection writes need no full scope again; `GET /logout` signs out again and cross-site calls are not refused; 1.24.0 ignores `confirm`, so a script that was changed to send only `{"confirm": true}` gets the 1.24.0 default, which replaces the stream key (it should send `"regenerate_key": false` if it does not want that); replacing a stream key while live leaves the publisher connected again; viewers who lose access keep their WebRTC playback; accounts deleted on 1.24.0 leave their stream chat channels behind again; 1.24.0 does not read the owner's role at delivery, so a demoted member's hook that somebody switches on again delivers again, and it ignores `WEBHOOK_LOCAL_ALLOW`, so a hook with `allow_private` can reach the portal's own services again; and the pages load their libraries from jsDelivr again under the old policy. Four more effects: browser-mode connections send their stored `auth_header` and `extra_headers` to every site again (to a site on plain http in clear text); a cookie-less `POST /logout` deletes the cookie again; the answers about a connection show `proxy_trusted_fp` and `proxy_trusted_by` (1.24.1 removes both from every answer); and a tab open on `/proxy/<id>/~/x` asks its upstream for `~/x` until it is reloaded. What does not come back: hooks that were switched off stay off, channels made private stay private, secrets removed because a connection moved stay removed, attachments stored as `<random>.bin` stay under that name, notifications withdrawn are not made again, and the anonymous reactions made under 1.24.1 are named again by 1.24.0's own rule (it only hides members who are anonymous at that moment).
- **Not tested.** Where a browser ran, it was one headless Chromium (156) started with the offline flags of `tools/make_screenshots.py`; Firefox, Safari, mobile browsers, Chromium with other cookie settings and Cloudflare in front were not tried, and the statement that an opaque-origin page sends no cookie is a measurement of that one browser. The roughly 25 applications of a real portal were not run; a stand-in application with scripts, modules, styles, forms, fetch and WebSockets was. No rehearsal on a copy of a production database is part of these notes, and the migration time and the marking of real reactions are not known. The parts:
  - **Proxied pages.** A real application with a cookie login in an isolated page was not tried: a stand-in upstream that sets HttpOnly, Secure and plain cookies was, and the cookies came back on the next request, through a redirect the portal follows, on the WebSocket upgrade and per site in browser mode (and in headless Chromium a later page of the stand-in app received its cookie while the browser held none); that the login of a particular real application works was not shown. Nothing of the jar survives a restart, so after a restart every isolated page asks for its logins again. The signed address is not tied to the network address, so a phone that moves between networks should keep its open page; that was reasoned from the code and not tried on a phone. A tab left open longer than the 6 hours has an address that no longer verifies: its page navigations go back through the connection's entry by themselves, while its scripts, images and other requests get `401` until the page is navigated or the connection is opened again from the portal. Cloudflare in the path was not tried (the address is the one the portal already uses for rate limits). The new notice and the admin routes were tested on a temporary database, not on a copy of a production database. The trust screen ran in a fake DOM, so its layout was not seen in a browser.
  - **Page libraries.** **What ran against fakes only.** `tests/test_vendor_libs.py` reads the files and the README tables and runs the real `security_headers_middleware` in-process on a temporary database; it checks hashes and text, not that a library works. **What ran in a real browser.** Headless Chromium, started with the offline flags of `tools/make_screenshots.py` (every name except loopback and the demo host is not found), against a staged instance on loopback, as an administrator: the live, streams, watch and VOD player pages construct hls.js 1.5.17; hls.js played the first seconds of a generated six second HLS stream (a local test clip, served by the test script) on the live page; the terminal page rendered an `xterm-screen` with the fit addon; the admin page loaded Chart.js 4.4.7; the chat page loaded highlight.js 11.12.0 and highlighted a line of code; the GitHub page loaded marked and DOMPurify (Markdown rendered, an `onerror` attribute removed); the dashboard loaded; and noVNC's `core/rfb.js` and all 45 modules loaded as an ES module on the VNC page. No console error, failed request or policy violation except the ones the test pages cause themselves (a made-up stream id, a made-up service id, and a static page whose `{{WS_PATH}}` was never filled in), and all 54 vendored files answered 200. This script is not part of the repository. **Not proven.** A real VNC session in noVNC (no VNC server was connected), a real terminal session through the vendored xterm on the terminal page (the shell socket was not driven), Chart.js drawing real data, a live stream through the live page from a real encoder, any browser other than that Chromium, Cloudflare in front, and what a first view costs on a slow link. That the CDN URLs of marked and the QR code really returned nothing is inferred from the npm packages, not observed.
  - **Authorisation.** In the authorisation checks there was no browser, no Cloudflare, no real encoder and no WebRTC player. The WebRTC disconnect runs against a fake of MediaMTX's API and of the portal's session records. The fake follows a capture taken earlier on scratch ports against a MediaMTX v1.21.3 started for that purpose, with a real RTMP publisher and two headless browser viewers (`tests/fixtures/mediamtx-1.21.3/webrtc_whep_sessions_kick.json`: the session's API id is in the `ID` header, not in `Location`); this release's code was not run against a real MediaMTX, and a staged instance contacts no media server by design. The MediaMTX error sentence was checked with a fake exception, not with a real refused connection. The webhook rule was checked with a child process that listens on loopback and with a service row; it was not checked against a real MediaMTX or search backend. Failed sign-ins are counted per exact address (see the entry on IPv6 above), and that was not tried with a real client holding a /64. The API key buttons were run under node with a fake page, not in a browser. The regeneration question in the stream settings, the new version of the Live streams script and the page that sends `confirm` were checked by reading the source only: no test runs them. A role changed by `server.py set-admin --remove` was checked through its effect on the database and delivery (the same rule that reads the role at every send), not by running the command in a second process.
  - **Privacy.** There was no Cloudflare and no real MediaMTX. Reactions, moderation, notifications, thumbnails, the health dot, the activity row and the automod answer run against the real handlers with temporary databases and, for moderation, real websockets on loopback; each new test was run against 1.24.0's code and fails there (the edits to `tests/test_automod.py` and `tests/test_chat_mods.py` expect the new answer where 1.24.0 refused or named the rule differently, so they fail there by construction). The tests of the review fix pass (the two added to `tests/test_anonymous_moderation.py` for the owner and administrator and for the moderator's frames, `tests/test_proxy_origin_hidden.py`, and the changed automod answer in `tests/test_automod.py`) were run against `git archive v1.24.0` and fail there (the owner and administrator test, because 1.24.0 refuses the ban itself, the automod one because it expects the unnamed text); the owner and administrator test and the proxied-page test were also run against the code just before their fix and fail there. The script the proxy writes into a plain-mode page was not run in a browser. Fakes only: the health dot (a hand-built probe state), the go-live row (a patched write that renames the stream in between) and the driver-log check (a test handler on the root logger). The proxied page of a shared plain-mode connection is checked through the real proxy handler against a loopback upstream (the address is absent from the body and the headers; the injected script was not run in a browser, and a page that depends on the removed origin rule was not looked for). The embedded browser page was also driven in an offline headless Chromium (no name other than loopback resolved) as a grantee in plain and browser mode: its DOM held no host or port, the address bar showed the name, and typing the name and a path or another origin produced the proxy path; that script is not part of the repository. For the second browser-mode fix (the connection's own site under `~`) an offline Chromium was driven through the real proxy against a loopback upstream as a grantee: the frame, the address bar and every request URL of the browser held no upstream port, a script `fetch` worked, and clicking a link written with the upstream's own origin opened the second page; that script is not part of the repository either, and the stream-timeout exemption was tested for messages and for a running call, over websocket frames on loopback with no audio (the poll and screen-share checks use the same function but were not exercised). Not proven: a real upstream behind a shared connection, Cloudflare caching or purging thumbnails or how it treats the other spellings of an upload address, an installed client keeping an old anonymous handle, a production database (the migration time and the marking of real reactions), the notifications list refreshed in an open browser, and what a stream moderator sees in the room's frames beyond the frames the tests collect on the moderator's socket (all of them, for a ban made from an anonymous post, with the author in the room under their name; the member-list change described above is not hidden).
  - **The follow-up fixes (sign-out, trust screen, address of an isolated page, same-host redirects, QR code).** No scanner or phone camera read the QR code; the re-entry of a stale isolated page from inside a sandboxed frame was exercised by an HTTP client with fetch-metadata headers, not measured in a browser (if a browser does not send the session cookie on that navigation, the entry answers with the login page inside the frame); the trust screen and the QR dialog ran in a fake DOM and the sign-out page with an HTTP client, so their layout was not seen; `/search` dropping the whole-address headers was checked by set membership and the source, not against a running SearXNG.
  - **Whole release.** The full stage smoke and a production-copy rehearsal belong to the release procedure; they were not run for the text of these notes.

### Upgrade notes for 1.24.0

1.24.0 adds a stream health panel for the streamer, a Live streams tab for administrators (Stop relays and End now), and a chat overlay for an OBS browser source. The notes below start with a short list for administrators, then give one group per feature (stream health, live streams, chat overlay), then the breaking changes, the database, the static files, the rollback and what was not tested.

**For administrators, in short**

- **Before upgrading: nothing has to be done, and nothing is switched on or blocked by default.** The health panel and the live controls bring no setting, `.env` entry, command or database change; their numbers are held in memory only, bounded by the number of live streams, and gone at a restart. The overlay adds one empty table, created at start (see Database); no stream has an overlay link until its owner makes one.
- **Who can see health data:** the stream's owner and administrators, nobody else. A stream moderator, another member and an unknown stream get the same `404`, and a stream key is refused. The panel never shows a relay destination, a key or a path on the server.
- **Readings arrive every 30 seconds.** The numbers come from the check that already asks MediaMTX every 30 seconds whether it is running; the first page of that check's answer is reused for them, and the portal reads further pages only when MediaMTX reports more than one. The graph has one point per 30 seconds, a stream has no bitrate until its second reading, and a stopped encoder is reported after 75 seconds, not at once.
- **Live streams tab (administrators only):** a list of the live streams with Stop relays and End now. Each action asks first and is written to the audit log; moderators do not read those entries.
- **What End now does:** it replaces the stream key (unless the box is turned off) and revokes temporary RTMP tokens, disconnects the encoder through MediaMTX where MediaMTX allows it, and ends the session the normal way. The stream chat's live state ends as at any stream end. Recordings are finished and uploaded: no recording is deleted. An encoder that reconnects with a new key starts a new live session, and the end of the old one never marks that one offline.
- **The overlay is broadcast.** Whatever the overlay shows is broadcast to everyone who can see the video, also for a stream that is not public, whose chat on the portal is closed to members; the stream settings say so. An anonymous post is shown as "Anonymous", with no badge.
- **The overlay link is a credential.** Whoever has the whole link can read that one stream's chat, read-only, and nothing else; treat it like a password. It is stored only as a SHA-256 hash and shown once. To revoke it, use Revoke in the stream settings, or Regenerate, which replaces it at once (then put the new link into every browser source that used the old one).
- **The overlay link ends by itself** when the owner's password is changed or reset, when an administrator signs the owner out everywhere (as the calendar link has since 1.23.0), when its stream is deleted and when the owner's account is deleted. It has no end date of its own.
- **Dock:** for OBS's Custom Browser Docks the stream settings show the compact chat window, which is the normal signed-in page: OBS's dock shows the portal sign-in and the member signs in as themselves. It uses no overlay token.
- **Before going back to 1.23.x:** delete the audit entries of End now and Stop relays, or moderators will read them there (see Rollback).

**Stream health**

- **Where the readings come from: the bitrate side of the panel is fed by the existing 30 second MediaMTX health check.** The viewer tick (every 5 seconds) reads only the database and the viewer counts; since 1.17.0 it makes no MediaMTX call and a test keeps `paths/list` out of it, and 1.24.0 keeps that. The service check that asks MediaMTX every 30 seconds whether it is up already fetches its list of paths and used to discard the answer; now each successful answer is also turned into bitrate, a ring of about 30 readings (about 15 minutes), source, codecs and the last time data arrived, once per answer (`_ingest_feed`), so for a list of one page there is no new call to MediaMTX; MediaMTX pages its list (100 paths a page is its documented default, which was not observed here: the 1.9.3 capture paged by 1), so when the first answer reports more than one page (`pageCount`) the check reads the further pages too, one call each, at most 10 pages in all (`MAX_PATH_PAGES`), and when one of them cannot be read nothing is taken from that answer (the health result itself depends on the first page alone); a path after the tenth page gets no reading. The rate is the growth of the received-bytes counter between two answers, taken at the times the answers arrived. Readings are coarse because of that: before its first reading the health route answers `ingest.available: false` with a reason that says no reading exists yet, between the first and the second it answers `available: true` with no bitrate, the Live streams tab shows "not collected" until the second, and nothing is read while MediaMTX is stopped or unhealthy (an entry that gets no new reading ends in the no-data warning). The thresholds are set for readings 30 seconds apart, so they differ from what a five-second feed would use: no data after 75 seconds, and the bitrate-drop baseline is the last five minutes (see Warnings below). Readings every 30 seconds, and a no-data warning that needs 75 seconds, are accepted as they are: a faster read would put more load on the media server. The panel says so in a line of its own.
- **Who may read the health:** the stream's owner and administrators, nobody else. A stream moderator, another member and an unknown id get the same `404`. A stream key is refused like on every stream route. 90 reads a minute per account (`429` with a reference ID, limiter `stream_health`).
- **What the panel never shows:** a relay destination (address, URL, key) or a path on the server. A relay's or the recorder's error is mapped to a fixed sentence ("The destination refused the connection.", "The connection timed out.", and so on) plus at most an exit code.
- **Route.** `GET /api/streams/{id}/health` (see docs/API.md): uptime, the ingest section, each relay's state and sanitised last error, the recorder's state with `waiting_chunks` and `waiting_bytes` (finished chunks not yet uploaded, the chunk being written is not counted; the disk guard's own helper), the recorder's last error, the host's load average and the warnings with the thresholds that produced them.
- **Warnings.** Computed on the server over windows: `no_data` when the received-bytes counter has not grown for 75 seconds (two readings 30 seconds apart and some slack); `bitrate_drop` when each of the newest three samples is under half of the average of the five minutes before them (at least four earlier samples and an average above 50 kbit/s, so it needs a stream that has run for about four minutes); `relay_failing` for an enabled relay that is not running with an error stored since the stream went live; `recording_stopped_by_guard`, `recording_refused_by_guard` and `recording_failed`. A single late sample changes none of them.
- **Ingest numbers.** The counter delta over the time between two readings, set to zero for the sample when the counter goes down or the source id or ready time changes (the encoder reconnected). The counter is read as `bytesReceived` or `inboundBytes` (MediaMTX v1.21.3 sends both; a missing field gives no sample). The stream behind a path is found through the live streams' keys (one database read, kept 30 seconds and dropped when a stream goes live or offline, `rtmp_` token paths included). The entry of a stream is removed when it goes offline.
- **Panel.** `static/js/stream-health.js` (new, version 4): a drawer on the watch page, shown only to the owner and administrators, polled every 4 seconds only while it is open and the tab is visible, sparkline built with DOM calls and as wide as the panel (also on a 320 pixel phone), server text set with `textContent`; a warning is shown in the theme's warning colour and an error in its danger colour, with a bar at its left edge. The stream settings have a link that opens it. `streams.js` is version 53.

**Live streams (administrators)**

- **Who may end a stream:** administrators with a browser session or a full-scope token. There are no other admin stream routes to follow, so the rule is the strictest existing one (the recordings'): API keys, also full-scope ones, and scoped tokens get `403`, a non-administrator `404`. 30 requests a minute per administrator for the two actions (limiter `live_ops`); the list has its own budget of 90 a minute (limiter `live_ops_list`), so a few open Live streams tabs, which ask every 5 seconds each, cannot make a confirmed End now answer `429`.
- **List.** Admin > Live streams (`static/js/live-ops-admin.js`, new, version 3; the tab appears only for an administrator): stream, owner, uptime, bitrate, viewers, running relays, recording; refreshed every 5 seconds while the tab is shown, except that the table is not redrawn while an End now or Stop relays question is open (the list is still read, and shows as read once the question is answered or cancelled).
- **Stop relays** (`POST /api/admin/streams/{id}/stop-relays`) stops the stream's relays and holds the relay monitor, the auto-restart of a single relay and the start after an encoder reconnect back until the stream ends. Audit action `stream_stop_relays`. While the hold is on, the owner's panel shows those relays as "stopped by an administrator" (state `stopped_by_admin`), without the exit code of the stopped process and without the relay warning. **The hold is kept in the portal's memory only and is not stored**: it lasts until the stream ends or the portal restarts, and the confirmation question says so. A restart marks every stream offline when the portal starts, so the next go-live is a new session and its relays start as usual; the relay monitor only restarts relays of a stream that is live, so a restart does not bring stopped relays back while the same session goes on. Two Stop relays requests at once for one stream: the second answers `409` ("The relays of this stream are already being stopped") and writes no audit entry.
- **End now** (`POST /api/admin/streams/{id}/end`, body `regenerate_key` default true): replaces the stream key and revokes temporary RTMP tokens first, asks MediaMTX's control API for the path's source and kicks that connection (RTMP, RTMPS, RTSP, RTSPS and WebRTC sources have a kick route; the RTMP, RTMPS and RTSP ones were run against a real MediaMTX v1.21.3 with an ffmpeg publisher, the RTSPS and WebRTC ones were only shown to exist there (a wrong id answers 404 and 400) and no such publisher was kicked; an SRT, HLS or camera source cannot be disconnected from here and the answer says so), then runs the normal end of a session (the same function as after a lost encoder, without the 7 second wait): relays are stopped at once, the recording is finished and uploaded (when the encoder could not be disconnected the recorder is asked to finish itself, so the stream does not wait for it), viewers are told it is offline. The owner gets a `stream_ended` notification, "An administrator ended your stream.", with two sentences added only when they apply: that the stream key was replaced (the encoder needs the new key from the stream settings) and that the encoder could not be disconnected (it may still be sending; not said when MediaMTX had no publisher on the path). Nothing else: no name, reason or key. With the key box off, the encoder can reconnect at once. Audit action `stream_end_now`, one entry per action, written before anything is done (if it cannot be written, nothing happens). **One End now per stream at a time:** the end is claimed under the stream state lock before the audit entry is written, so a double click or two administrators end the stream once; the second answers `409` ("This stream is already being ended"), writes no audit entry and sends no second offline record or webhook. The claim is released when the end has finished or failed (for a stream with a recording: after the upload). **The path is the one the session was published on:** the portal remembers the MediaMTX path a live session was published on, in full, from its go-live (`live/<key>` or `live/<rtmp_ token>` for an encoder; a browser (WHIP) publish is on the bare path `<key>`, with no `live/` folder, which is the path MediaMTX itself reports to the portal's publish check), and uses it for the ingest reading and for the disconnect, so a browser publish gets readings and an End now that disconnects its WebRTC session like any other source, and an owner who replaces the stream key while live neither loses the panel's numbers nor makes End now aim at the new key's empty path. A publisher that reconnects with the new key sets the new path.
- **Both ask first.** The question appears on its own row below the stream, not under the button that was clicked; its confirm button does nothing within 0.6 seconds of the question opening or on the second click of a double click, and every action is refused while another request is running. The End now question says that the stream chat's live state ends (its messages are cleared, as at any stream end, unless the chat is shared), what happens to the key and that no recording is deleted.
- **A staged instance** (`PORTAL_STAGE_MODE`) never contacts a media server: End now answers `publisher_disconnected: false`.
- **Behaviour changes (stream health and live controls).** (1) The disconnect grace task (7 seconds after a lost encoder) now does nothing when the stream is no longer live when it wakes up, so an administrator's End now is not repeated by a hook that arrives after it; a stream that is still live is ended exactly as before, and the code after the wait is now the function `_stream_end_session`. (2) Stop relays holds the relay health monitor back for that stream until it goes offline. (2a) Every accepted publish that is not a repeat report of a go-live already under way now starts a new live session in memory (`_stream_sessions`); when a browser publish is reported twice (MediaMTX's publish check and the portal's own WHIP proxy), the second report joins the session of the first, with the same identity, and neither logs, records nor announces the go-live again, so the first session's finalisation and an administrator's End now still act on the one session there is. Each step of ending an earlier session (the encoding or offline write, the viewer figures, the offline record and webhook, the notice to the chat room, the chat clean-up, stopping the recorder) applies only while that session is still the current one, so an encoder that reconnects at once after End now (key box off) is no longer marked offline, cleared or announced as ended by the finalisation of the session before; the compare and the write are one locked step, but the relay stop and the chat clean-up check just before they act, so a reconnect inside that very short gap is not excluded. Stopping the relays of the ending session can still stop relays of the new one if the encoder reconnects while that stop is running. The disconnect grace task uses the same claim and session check as End now, so the two cannot end one session twice. At shutdown the server stops accepting new chat overlay sockets first (a `503` with `Retry-After`, which the overlay page retries), then, after the recordings are stopped and before the database closes, waits up to 5 seconds for running session finalisations and End now steps and cancels what is left (the next start marks every stream offline in any case). (2b) A relay that an administrator stopped is reported as `stopped_by_admin` instead of `failed`. (3) New notification type `stream_ended`. (4) New audit actions `stream_stop_relays` and `stream_end_now`, visible to administrators in the audit log and left out for moderators (they name the stream and its owner, and a moderator may not know a private stream exists, so the log answers a moderator as it does for the recordings' actions); they hold the stream id, name, owner and the options, never a key.

**Chat overlay**

The chat overlay for an OBS browser source is new in 1.24.0. The stream's owner makes a secret link in the stream settings; a Browser source in OBS that loads it draws that stream's chat, read-only, on a transparent background.

- **Nothing is switched on by default.** No setting, `.env` entry or command changes; 1.24.0 starts on a 1.23.1 database and adds one table on its own (see Database), and no stream has an overlay link until its owner makes one.
- **What the link is.** A random token (256 bits, prefix `ov_`) per stream, stored only as a SHA-256 hash, shown once when it is made. It reaches exactly one thing: `/ws/overlay`, a read-only view of that stream's own chat as its viewers would see it. No other route or socket accepts it (the sign-in checks do not know it, and the owner routes need a full-access credential), and it opens no page that shows anything about a stream. One link is active per stream: making a new one replaces the old, which stops at once. The link ends with the owner's old credentials, exactly as the calendar link has since 1.23.0: when the owner changes their password, when an administrator resets it, and when an administrator signs the owner out everywhere; the overlay links of all of the account's streams are deleted and their open sockets closed at once, and the owner makes a new link and puts it into OBS. The command-line reset of the `admin` password deletes the links too; a running portal closes their sockets within 30 seconds (its revalidation pass), because the command-line process has none of its sockets. A stream whose chat is not its own (a chat shared with other streams or a default channel) cannot have one.
- **A secret in a URL.** The token is in the part of the address after the `#`, which a browser never sends to a server, and the page's script sends it in the first WebSocket frame. It is in no path or query, so the portal's access log, the request-ID lines and a proxy's log do not have it (the log redaction also removes an `ov_` token anywhere it appears, as a second line). Anyone who has the whole link can read that one chat until it is regenerated or revoked, so treat it like a password; the stream settings say so. OBS keeps the link in its scene file and a screen share of the OBS settings shows it. The test file for the overlay runs the whole overlay suite with logging at DEBUG (the portal's own loggers set to DEBUG) and finds no token in the log file nor in any record as it was written, before redaction; the staged smoke check can only run the portal at its normal INFO level and checks that log for tokens and for the redaction marker, so a token logged at DEBUG by a path the test file does not take would not be seen by either.
- **What it broadcasts, and for a stream that is not public.** Whatever the overlay shows is broadcast to everyone who can see the video.
