For a stream that is not public the link still works, because the owner chose to put their own chat on their own broadcast: people who can see the video but cannot read the chat on the portal then see the chat. The stream settings say so in plain words, in a note that is shown for every stream and a stronger one for a stream that is not public.
- **What a socket gets.** The last 50 messages of that one channel when it connects, then new messages, edits, deletes, prunes and clears of that channel, each reduced to display name, owner or moderator badge, text, time and message id. Never a member list, presence, typing, direct messages, another channel, a moderation detail, an account id or role, a reply, a picture or an attachment. An anonymous post is "Anonymous" with no badge. A message an incoming webhook posted is shown, live and in that history, under the name the chat gives it (`[hook] name`) and with no badge. A message with no text of its own (a picture, a go-live card) is not shown. The history comes on every connect and is the whole set the overlay shows, so after a reconnect a line whose message was deleted while the socket was away is removed.
- **Limits.** The socket must send its token within 5 seconds or it is closed. While the server is stopping, a new socket is refused with `503` and `Retry-After` before the upgrade, and the page tries again later: it waits 1 second after a dropped socket, doubling up to 30 seconds, and goes back to 1 second only after a socket has stayed open for 10 seconds (a socket that is accepted, shown the history and dropped does not reset it). A bad, unknown, revoked or regenerated token closes the socket with the one code 4401 (the overlay page then stops and shows nothing). Failed tokens count per address (10 in 10 minutes, an IPv6 address as its /64); after that the address gets `429` with `Retry-After` and a reference ID on the upgrade, like every other limiter (limiter name `overlay_token`); at most 10 sockets per address can wait for their first frame; at most 3 sockets per token, at most 9 per owner across all their streams (so no single member can take more than 3 percent of the portal's room) and at most 300 on the whole portal, a backstop that takes 34 owners to reach (one more is closed with 1013). Frames from the client are limited to 1 KB and any frame after the first closes the socket.
- **A slow OBS client cannot slow the chat.** `broadcast_to_channel` hands each frame to a bounded queue (100 frames) per overlay socket without waiting; a writer task sends with a 5 second timeout. A socket whose queue is full or whose send does not finish is closed (1013) and forgotten. The chat members' sockets are sent to exactly as before.
- **After Regenerate, replace the link everywhere.** A browser source that still holds the old link is refused (4401) each time OBS reloads it, and every refusal counts as a failed token against the address it comes from (10 in 10 minutes, then `429` on the upgrade). Put the new link into every browser source that used the old one, or an owner's own address can be held back for a while.
- **When sockets end.** At once when the token is regenerated or revoked, when the stream is deleted and when the owner's account is deleted. A pass every 30 seconds closes any socket whose token no longer resolves (a backstop) or whose chat channel was renamed (it then reconnects), and all of them end at shutdown.
- **Dock.** For OBS's Custom Browser Docks the stream settings show `/watch/{id}?chat=only`, the compact chat window that already exists (video hidden). It is the normal signed-in page: OBS's dock browser shows the portal sign-in and the member signs in as themselves. The sign-in page does not remember where the dock was going, so after signing in the dock shows the dashboard and has to be opened again (or reloaded) to show the chat. There is no new credential and nothing about it is new code.
- **Owner routes** (full-access credential, owner only; anyone else, an administrator included, and an unknown stream get the same `404`): `GET /api/streams/{id}/overlay` (status: active, created, last used, whether the stream is public, whether it has a chat of its own, the dock address; never the token), `POST` (make or replace; the answer holds the link once; `409` when the stream has no chat of its own) and `DELETE` (revoke). Making and revoking are written to the audit log (`stream_overlay_create`, `stream_overlay_revoke`). Making has a budget of 20 a minute per account; over it the answer is `429` with `Retry-After` and a reference ID (limiter `overlay_manage`). Revoking has no limit, so a leaked link can always be revoked at once; it writes at most one audit entry per link that existed, and none when there was no link.
- **The page** `GET /overlay/stream/{id}` is the same file for every id and says nothing about whether a stream exists. It has no portal navbar, no `portal.js` and reads no cookie. Its headers: `Cache-Control: no-store`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex, nofollow` (and a robots meta tag and a `Disallow: /overlay` line in `robots.txt`), `X-Frame-Options: DENY` and a Content-Security-Policy of `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; img-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'`, which is stricter than the CSP of the other pages (no inline script or style, no CDN).
- **Rendering and options.** Lines appear at the bottom and fade; text is set with `textContent`; an emoji is the character it is. Options go after the token in the fragment and are checked by the script: `&size=` 12 to 96 (pixels, default 24), `&lines=` 1 to 30 (default 8), `&fade=` 0 to 600 (seconds, default 30, 0 keeps a line until newer ones push it out); a value outside the range is clamped, one that is not a number falls back to the default. The script does not remove the fragment from the address, so OBS's "Refresh cache of current page" keeps working. A dropped connection reconnects with a growing delay; the refusal code 4401 does not.
- **Settings block.** Create link, Copy (the link is shown once and is dropped from the page when the settings window closes), Regenerate and Revoke. Regenerate and Revoke ask first, on a row of their own: a confirm within 0.6 seconds of the question opening, and the second click of a double click, do nothing; Create, Regenerate and Revoke ignore a click while a request is in flight. The block explains how to add a Browser source in OBS and how to add the dock.
- **Where a deleted line comes from.** A delete by the author or a moderator, a prune, a clear, and the wipe of a stream's chat when it goes offline all reach the overlay at once. Messages removed by the retention sweep or by deleting an account that wrote them do not send a frame (the chat page does not get one either): such a line stays on the overlay until it fades or is pushed out, and is gone when OBS reloads the page.
- **Audit trail.** Making and revoking a link are audit actions (`stream_overlay_create`, `stream_overlay_revoke`). They name the stream and its owner, the name of a stream that is not public included, so like the two live-operation actions they are left out for moderators: only an administrator reads them in Admin > Audit log, with or without a filter.

**Breaking changes**

- None for existing clients. No route, field or setting changes meaning; the new routes (`GET /api/streams/{id}/health`, `/api/admin/live-streams`, the two admin stream actions, `/overlay/stream/{id}`, `/ws/overlay` and `/api/streams/{id}/overlay`) are additions, as are one table and the `?v=` numbers under Static files.
- One change a script could notice: `/ws/overlay` is registered before the `/ws/{path}` service relay, so a service whose path is `overlay` is no longer reachable under `/ws/overlay` (the page path `/overlay/stream/{id}` is new too, and `robots.txt` has a new `Disallow: /overlay` line).

**Database, static files, rollback and what was not tested**

- **Database.** One table, created with `IF NOT EXISTS` at start: `stream_overlay_tokens` (`stream_id` primary key with `ON DELETE CASCADE` to `user_streams`, `token_hash` unique, `created_at`, `last_used_at`). It is empty until an owner makes a link, so the migration costs nothing on a large database; no existing table or column changes, and the stream health panel and the live controls add no table or column. Deleting an account deletes the tokens of its streams (explicitly, and again through the foreign key when the streams go).
- **Static files.** `stream-health.js` is new at version 4 (loaded by `watch.html`), `live-ops-admin.js` is new at version 3 (loaded by `admin.html`), `overlay.js` is new at version 3 and `overlay.css` at version 1, both loaded by `overlay.html` only; `stream-overlay-settings.js` is new at version 3 (`index.html`, before `streams.js`); `streams.js` is version 53, up from 51 (the stream settings link of the health panel and the Chat Overlay block of the overlay). `watch.html`, `admin.html` and `overlay.html` are served fresh. No other script or style changes.
- **Rollback.** 1.23.1 ignores the new routes and files and starts on the database 1.24.0 left: it ignores the overlay table (its database layer deletes a stream or an account through the foreign keys and the token row goes with it), and the health panel and the live controls change nothing in the database. Overlay links stop working while 1.23.1 runs (it has no `/ws/overlay`). 1.23.1 does not know the overlay table either, so a password change, a reset or a sign-out-everywhere done while it runs does not end the links: after a later upgrade the old links would read that owner's chat again. Before going back to 1.23.1, delete them (`DELETE FROM stream_overlay_tokens;`, run against `portal.db`), and owners make new links after the next upgrade. One more thing is not safe to leave: the audit entries of End now, Stop relays and the overlay links name a stream (its name, id and owner), and only 1.24.0 hides them from a moderator. 1.23.1 does not know them and shows them to a moderator in Admin > Audit log, private streams included. Before going back to 1.23.1, delete them with this one statement, run against `portal.db`: `DELETE FROM audit_log WHERE action IN ('stream_end_now','stream_stop_relays','stream_overlay_create','stream_overlay_revoke');` (an administrator keeps no record of these actions afterwards), or accept that moderators will read them. A rollback by update snapshot restores the code only, so it does not remove them either. Nothing else needs undoing.
- **Not tested.** Nothing here was run against a live portal with a real encoder, OBS or Cloudflare in front. Grouped by feature:
  - **Stream health and live controls.** Everything in this group was run against fakes and captures, never a live portal with a real encoder. (1) MediaMTX: the fixtures in `tests/fixtures/mediamtx-1.21.3` are real captures of v1.21.3, the version the production instance runs; the fixtures in `tests/fixtures/mediamtx-1.9.3` are real captures of v1.9.3, the release `setup.py` installs on a fresh machine (downloaded with the same URL pattern, run from the binary on scratch ports): the paths list with its `itemCount` and `pageCount`, paging with `page` and `itemsPerPage`, and the kick routes for an RTMP and an RTSP publisher, each run with a real ffmpeg publisher. On 1.9.3 only RTMP and RTSP (tcp) publishers were run; RTMPS, RTSPS, WebRTC, SRT and HLS sources were not, so their kick routes are verified on 1.21.3 (RTMPS) or only shown to exist (RTSPS, WebRTC). Reading more than one page was run against a fake of the API that pages the way the 1.9.3 capture shows, not against a MediaMTX holding more than 100 paths. The RTMP, RTMPS and RTSP publishers were real ffmpeg processes; the WebRTC item is hand-written because ffmpeg 5.1 has no WHIP output, so no WebRTC capture or kick was run; that a browser publish is on the path `<key>` rather than `live/<key>` follows from how the portal's WHIP proxy addresses MediaMTX and was checked only as far as MediaMTX 1.21.3 listing an RTSP publish to a path without a folder under its own name, not with a WHIP publisher. (2) The bitrate figure was checked against the captured counters of an ffmpeg publisher set to 800 kbit/s video plus 64 kbit/s audio (868 kbit/s measured, within 10 percent), and the feed was run through the real `MediaMTXService.health_check` against a fake of MediaMTX's API behind a throwaway TLS certificate on 127.0.0.1 (counters set by the test, 30 seconds apart on a fake clock), and once by hand against a MediaMTX v1.21.3 of its own on scratch ports with an ffmpeg test-pattern publisher (1000 kbit/s video plus 64 kbit/s audio over RTMP), where the real `health_check` fed readings 12 seconds apart (not 30) and the bitrate came out at 1.08 Mbit/s; it was not run in a full portal process with OBS or a real encoder over the internet; the 75 second and five minute thresholds were reasoned from the 30 second spacing and unit-tested, not observed over a long real stream, and the service check's real interval under load (it checks every service in turn) was not measured. (3) End now was run against a fake of MediaMTX's control API (answers copied from the capture) and on a staged portal that contacts no media server, not against a real MediaMTX with a connected encoder through the portal. (4) The panel and the Live streams tab ran against a fake DOM in Node (no layout, no real timers) and, for the pictures, in a headless Chromium 156 against a staged instance. The readings in its picture are made up (the stage has no media server and the feature has no hook for readings, so the picture's browser replaces the ingest part of the portal's answer, uptime and load included): the picture shows how the panel draws, not real ingest numbers, and End now was never confirmed there. In that browser the End now question wraps inside the table on a row of its own (the picture was taken in a window 1280 pixels wide, and the tool fails the picture when the table is wider than its window); only that one window width and that one Chromium were seen, not a phone (the health panel's graph was checked at 320 pixels in a headless Chromium on a stand-alone page that loads the panel's own script and the page's own style rules, not on the real watch page), and the table's other rows still scroll sideways in a window narrower than their eight columns. (5) No OBS, no real encoder over the internet, no Cloudflare in front, no relay to a real platform (a failing relay was simulated with a stored error), no recorder uploading to a real SFTP server for these routes.
  - **No OBS and no browser (overlay).** The page script and the settings block ran under Node against a fake DOM, a fake WebSocket and a scripted fetch, as separate classic scripts. The staged smoke check starts no browser (a headless Chromium contacts its maker's hosts within seconds, and a smoke check dials nothing outside this machine); it checks the page's policy as text (`connect-src 'self'`, no other host). The screenshot tool did load the page in a headless Chromium 156 against a staged instance (`docs/screenshots/overlay-page.png`): the `wss:` connection was allowed under that policy, the lines were drawn as text, and the page came out transparent (the picture is laid over a checkerboard by the tool). That is one Chromium on one machine, not OBS's embedded browser, so the transparency in OBS, the fade, the layout at OBS sizes and OBS's own browser version were not seen. The settings block and the dock note were shown in the same headless Chromium, the dock itself was not tried. The docks were not tried. The screenshot tool starts its Chromium with background networking, component updates, sync, default apps and field trials switched off and with a resolver rule that leaves only the demo host and loopback resolvable; in a net log of a Chromium 156 the only connections were to 127.0.0.1, while the browser still asked for its maker's hosts, and those requests failed with a name that does not resolve.
  - **No real encoder and no Cloudflare.** The sockets were tested in-process and on a staged instance on loopback. Nothing ran through Cloudflare, so how it treats a long-lived WebSocket from OBS (idle timeouts, the 429 on the upgrade) is untested.
  - **Slow clients were tested with a fake that never finishes a send and with a raw TCP client that finishes the handshake and never reads.** The broadcast to a chat member took under a few milliseconds throughout and the stalled socket was dropped; this was run on loopback only, not with a real congested network or a stuttering OBS.
  - **Not tried at scale.** Many overlays on one stream or many streams with overlays; the caps are three sockets per token, nine per owner, 300 on the whole portal and ten waiting sockets per address, and the queue is 100 frames; the caps were tried with a few dozen sockets, not with hundreds.
  - **Message ordering on connect.** The history is read after the socket is registered, so a message posted during the read can appear in both; the script shows a message id once. Not tried under heavy chat. A reconnect's history replaces what is shown, so a line deleted while the socket was away goes; the replacement was tested under Node with a fake DOM, not in OBS.
  - **Webhook lines and the name.** A webhook post is shown under the name stored with the message. The chat's own history route gives a member's chosen nickname in place of the stored name for every message of an account, and a webhook post is one, so a webhook owner who has set a nickname is shown to chat viewers under it in the history, while the live line and the overlay say `[hook] name`. This was left as it is. The command-line password reset ends the overlay links in the database, but its sockets are closed by the running portal's revalidation pass (every 30 seconds), the revalidation pass is tested in-process; a real command-line run against a running portal was not tried.

### Upgrade notes for 1.23.1

1.23.1 changes wording and page metadata only. There is no change in behaviour, no new or changed route, setting, `.env` entry or command, no database change and no change to any script or style file or its logic (`server.py` differs only in the one-line description of the web app manifest), so nothing has to be done before or after upgrading and going back to 1.23.0 needs nothing undone.

- **What a self-hoster sees.** The project now describes itself as a privacy-first, self-hosted streaming and community platform instead of a communication and infrastructure gateway. That shows as new page titles and descriptions on the sign-in, About, live, Guides and API pages (and in the link previews other sites make from them), a new subtitle on the sign-in page ("Sign in to your community's streams and chat"), a new description in the web app manifest, and a new 1200x630 share image (`static/og-image.png`). A site that cached the old share image, or Cloudflare's edge cache, may keep showing it for a while. `robots.txt` and `sitemap.xml` are unchanged.
- **Corrected wording.** Pages and README no longer say or imply that chat or voice is end-to-end encrypted or that nothing is loaded from other hosts. They now state that chat is encrypted at rest but readable by the server and its administrator, which other hosts the pages and the server contact (see Security), and the limits of an administrator, a proxy in front and anonymous posting. Three lines of the connection guides that called a proxied connection end-to-end now say Portal decrypts it in between or that the traffic is encrypted in transit. The sentence that a copy of the database cannot be read is not made: the search index keeps the words of messages in readable form, and the README says so.

### Upgrade notes for 1.23.0

1.23.0 adds two features. The first is a schedule for every stream: the owner plans when they go live, members who may watch see a countdown while the stream is offline and can mark themselves Interested, each of them gets one reminder 10 minutes before the start, and a member can put the schedules of the streams they follow into a calendar application through a personal, read-only calendar link. The second is saved messages and remind-me-later in the chat. The notes are grouped by feature; one Breaking changes group, and one Database, one Static files, one Rollback and one Not tested bullet after it cover both.

**For administrators, in short**

- **Nothing has to be done before upgrading, and nothing is switched on or blocked by default.** No setting, `.env` entry or command changes. 1.23.0 starts on a 1.22.0 database and adds seven tables and their indexes on its own (see Database): no column of an existing table changes and nothing is rewritten. No stream has a schedule until its owner adds one, no member has a calendar link until they make one, and nothing is saved until a member saves it.
- **Who sees a schedule entry:** exactly those who may watch the stream, asked again on every read. For a public stream that is every signed-in member who is not banned from it; for a stream that is not public it is the owner and the administrators only. Nobody else gets the entry, a count, a reminder or a feed line for it, and the answer for a stream they may not watch is the same `404` as for a stream that does not exist. A visitor who is not signed in never sees one. Only the stream's owner edits a schedule.
- **The calendar link is a secret in a URL.** Anyone who has it can read that member's schedule feed until they regenerate or revoke it. Proxies and servers between a calendar application and the portal (Cloudflare included) can log a URL, so the token can end up in their logs. The portal replaces the token with `[REDACTED]` in its own access log and request-ID lines. To end a link, the member opens User settings > Calendar link for stream schedules and presses Revoke (the link stops working at once) or Regenerate (the old link stops and a new one is shown once); the page says the same. The link also ends, with no action of the member, when their password is changed or reset (by themselves, by an administrator or by `server.py init`) and when an administrator signs them out everywhere; the member makes a new link afterwards. (The portal has no way to disable an account other than deleting it, and deleting a member removes the link with the rest of their rows.)
- **What a reminder says.** A stream reminder says "<streamer> starts in 10 minutes" with no slot title and no stream title, and names the streamer only for a public stream: for a stream that is not public (an administrator's mark on one) the stored notification, the desktop alert and the Web Push all say "A stream you are interested in starts in 10 minutes", and the notification still links to the stream by its id. A saved-message reminder says "Reminder for a saved message" in both places (the push body is "Open the portal to see it."). Neither carries message text, an author or a channel name.
- **Saved entries are references.** The portal stores which message a member saved, never a copy of it. Every time the Saved view or a reminder looks at an entry it asks the chat's own read rules again, so a member sees only what they could read in the chat right then. An entry that cannot be read any more, or whose message is gone, is shown as "No longer available" with just the time it was saved. Nobody else sees a member's saved entries, Interested marks or reminders, an administrator included.
- **Two new background tasks.** One sends the stream reminders and one sends the saved-message reminders; each ticks every 30 seconds, runs one query on an indexed column and does nothing else while no reminder is due, and both are stopped at shutdown (the saved-message one is cancelled and awaited before the push sender and the database are closed).
- **Web Push.** Both reminders use the existing push path and its rules: push switched on, a registered device, no portal tab open. A stream reminder is covered by the member's existing go-live switch, whose label now reads "Streams you follow going live, and reminders for streams you are interested in". A saved-message reminder is a fixed `reminder` kind that every device-owning member gets (the three switchable kinds of 1.19.0 are unchanged; there is no per-kind push switch for reminders, because the member asked for the reminder). Neither push has a new switch.

**Stream schedule, reminders and the calendar feed**

- **Who edits a schedule:** only the stream's owner, the rule of the stream settings (`PUT /api/streams/{id}` is owner only today; an administrator and a chat moderator get `404`). Stream moderators moderate the stream's chat and have no schedule rights.
- **Who sees an entry:** exactly those who may watch the stream (`_stream_read_allowed`, the watch rule), asked again on every read: the upcoming list, the stream's countdown, the Interested mark, the reminder and the calendar feed. A member who may not watch gets the same `404` as for an unknown stream, and no list, count or reminder reveals the entry. The owner sees a count of interested members, never who.
- **Visitors.** `/watch/{id}` sends anyone who is not signed in to the sign-in page, so a visitor never sees the offline page; nothing changes for visitors.
- **Entries.** A title of at most 100 characters, a start as local time in an IANA zone (`2026-12-04T21:00`, `Europe/Berlin`), a duration of 5 to 1440 minutes and optionally `weekly`. A weekly entry keeps its wall-clock time: Friday 21:00 in Berlin is 20:00 UTC in winter and 19:00 UTC in summer. A start that does not exist (the hour skipped in spring) is read as zoneinfo reads it, an hour later. Caps: 20 entries per stream, 60 per owner, 200 upcoming Interested marks per member, 60 cancelled occurrences kept per entry. Cancel one occurrence or delete the whole entry; moving an entry's time drops the marks and cancellations of occurrences to come.
- **Interested** refers to one occurrence (the one the countdown shows). It is dropped when that occurrence is cancelled or moved. A member who may no longer watch the stream or is banned from it sees nothing of it at once (every read asks the watch rule again), and the mark itself is dropped when its reminder falls due, in the first tick that finds the occurrence within 10 minutes of its start, without a reminder being sent. The Interested button on the countdown goes away once the start has passed, as the route refuses a mark from then on. Past marks are pruned after 7 days, and the hourly prune also removes the started marks of occurrences more than a day old (nothing reads them any more).
- **Reminder.** One tick every 30 seconds sends the in-app notification (`stream_reminder`) and the push to each member with a mark whose occurrence starts within the next 10 minutes: between 10:00 and 9:30 before the start, so "Friday 21:00" reminds at about 20:50. The wording is under "For administrators, in short". The reminder is claimed in the database (the sent mark is stored with the mark and also in a small table that outlives it) before it is sent, so it is never sent twice, also across a restart and also when the member takes the mark back and marks again, or the owner cancels and restores the occurrence or moves the entry and back; a start that passed while the portal was down sends nothing. Before the claim the occurrence must still exist as scheduled (not cancelled, moved or started). Right after the claim the stream is read again and the member's right to watch it is checked again, and only then is anything sent: a stream made private in between, or a member who lost access, gets no notification, no push and no streamer name, the mark is dropped and the reminder is not owed again. **A mark made inside the 10 minutes** gets its one reminder at the next tick (at most one, never none, never two). **An occurrence start in a URL** is a whole number from 1 to 4102444800 (1 January 2100); anything else, however large, is refused (`404` on a mark, `400` on cancel and restore) instead of failing with a server error.
- **A time zone the host lost.** If a tzdata update removes a zone name an entry was stored with, that one entry has no upcoming occurrence anywhere it is read: it is left out of the Upcoming list, of a stream's countdown, of the calendar feed and of the reminder pass (its marks stay and are not dropped, so the reminder works again if the zone does), a mark or a cancel on it is refused with `400`, and every other entry and every other member is unaffected. Its owner sees it in the editor with "time zone no longer available". The editor has no control that changes an entry, so the owner deletes it and adds it again with another zone; through the API a `PUT` with a new `tz` fixes it in place (a change of the start without a new zone is refused with `400`). Either way the entry's Interested marks are dropped, as for any change of the time. The portal logs it once per entry per day.
- **Go-live.** A stream that goes live from 15 minutes before to 30 minutes after a slot's start belongs to that slot: the occurrence is marked started (the countdown moves to the next one, no reminder is sent for it) and the slot's title is offered to the owner in the stream settings (Use this title or Dismiss). Nothing is renamed by the server.
- **Pages.** While a stream is offline the watch page shows the next occurrence with its title, a countdown and, for a signed-in member who is not the owner, an Interested toggle. The Streams page has an Upcoming strip, the stream settings a Schedule section, User settings a calendar link block. In the Schedule section Delete the whole entry asks first, because it drops every Interested mark on the entry, and Cancel this one and Restore take one click each: a second click of a double click, or a click while a request is still in flight, does nothing.
- **Calendar feed.** `GET /api/streams/schedule/feed/{token}.ics` needs no login. The token is made for one member (256 random bits, prefix `sf_`), stored only as a SHA-256 hash, shown once when it is made, replaced by Regenerate and ended by Revoke, and accepted by this route alone (it is no session, API key or bearer token anywhere else, and this route reads no cookie or header credential). The feed holds the entries of the streams the member follows, has marked Interested on, or owns, and of those only what they may watch at the moment of the fetch. Making a stream private removes it from a non-member's feed at the next fetch. In User settings, Regenerate and Revoke ask first and say that they end the link in use (Yes, revoke, like Yes, regenerate, ignores a click within 0.6 seconds of the question opening and the second click of a double click), and the address that was shown is taken off the page when the settings dialog is closed.
- **Daylight saving in the feed.** A weekly entry is one `VEVENT` with `DTSTART;TZID=<zone>:<local time>`, `RRULE:FREQ=WEEKLY`, `EXDATE;TZID=<zone>` for cancelled weeks and a `VTIMEZONE` built from the zone's real transitions (explicit observances, no rule parsing needed by the client). A UTC `DTSTART` would keep the UTC hour and drift by an hour at a change. Each cancelled week is printed at the wall-clock time the rule itself produces for it, also for a week whose time falls in the hour a spring-forward change skips (02:30 in Berlin stays 02:30, not 03:30), and the autumn fold. The `VTIMEZONE` block is built once per zone and year range, not once per fetch. The test expands the rule from the file alone across the Berlin change of 25 October 2026.
- **Feed format and limits.** UIDs are `schedule-<entry id>@<host name>`; text is escaped, lines are folded at 75 octets, line ends are CRLF. Answers are `no-store` with `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`; an unknown, malformed or revoked token is one identical `404`; `429` with `Retry-After` after 30 requests per token or 120 per address in 10 minutes (an IPv6 address counts as its /64 network, as in every other limiter; when the table of counters is full the entry used least recently is dropped, never all of them). That `429` goes the way of every other rate limit: its text carries a reference ID (kept per address, with the limiter names `schedule_feed_address` and `schedule_feed_token` for the administrator's lookup), so the reference rows stay bounded, and neither the token nor its hash is stored with it.
- **Routes.** `GET /api/streams/schedule/upcoming` (30 requests a minute per member, its own budget separate from the chat history; `429` with `Retry-After` and a reference ID, limiter name `schedule_upcoming`), the entry and occurrence routes under `/api/streams/{id}/schedule`, `PUT`/`DELETE .../rsvp/{start}`, the feed and `/api/me/schedule-feed`, described in docs/API.md and `static/api-docs.html`.

**Saved messages and reminders**

- **A saved entry is a reference, not a copy.** The table `saved_messages` holds the member, the kind (`channel` or `dm`), the channel or conversation id, the message id and three epoch times (`saved_at`, `remind_at`, `fired_at`). No message text, author or channel name is stored anywhere for it, and the reminder notification holds none either. Everything the Saved view shows is resolved when it is asked for, through the same read rule as the chat itself (`_can_read_channel`, active participation in a direct message, `_enrich_channel_messages` for masking), so what a member sees there is what they could read in the chat at that moment.
- **No longer available.** A message the member can no longer read (removed from a private channel, left a conversation, a stream chat made private or a stream ban) or that is gone (deleted, pruned, aged out by retention, its author removed) is listed as `"available": false` with only its entry id, the saved time and the reminder state: no kind, no channel, conversation or message id, no author and no text. If access comes back the entry reads again. An anonymous post stays `Anonymous` with no account id, exactly as in the channel. Chat message ids are never reused (`AUTOINCREMENT`), every lookup also matches the kind and the stored channel or conversation id, and an entry refuses a message that was created after it was saved (a restored or hand-rebuilt database), so an entry can never show a different message. `GET /api/saved/marks` leaves out the same entries, so it never names a message the list hides. Setting a reminder on such an entry is refused with `409` and one fixed text whatever the reason, so it does not say why; clearing a waiting reminder still works. `DELETE /api/saved/unavailable` removes every one of the member's own entries that is unavailable at that moment, those with a waiting reminder included, and answers only the number removed (`{"removed": 3}`), which is a count the member can already make in their own Saved view; it needs the same full-scope credential as the other saved routes and spends the chat history budget like the list and the marks, because it resolves the entries the same way.
- **Clean-up.** There is none for gone messages: an entry stays until its member removes it (an entry whose message is gone or unreadable shows only "No longer available", the same for both), because a clean-up that removed the entries of deleted messages would tell a member who lost access which message was deleted. The list is capped at 500 entries per member (a full list is cleared by hand) and rows are deleted with the member.
- **Private to the member.** Every route is the caller's own and takes a browser session or a full-access token (API keys and stream keys get `403`). No frame is sent on any socket when an entry is saved, changed or removed, no audit or log line is written about one, an administrator sees nothing (not even a count), and answers are `Cache-Control: private, no-store`. A save of a message that does not exist and of one the member may not read give the same `404`.
- **Limits.** 500 saved entries and 100 waiting reminders per member (both enforced inside the SQL statement, not by a read followed by a write), a reminder at most 366 days ahead, at most 200 `reminder` notifications kept per member (when a firing would exceed that, the member's oldest `reminder` notifications are deleted in the same transaction, so re-arming reminders over and over cannot grow the notifications table without bound), the list in pages of 30 (at most 50) sharing the chat history budget of 30 a minute. A reminder time is an absolute UTC instant with a `Z`, in the future; the page works out "tomorrow 9:00" and "next week" in the member's own time zone and sends the instant.
- **Reminders.** The task ticks every 30 seconds (the first tick 5 seconds after the start) and reads a partial index of the waiting reminders. A tick fires at most 50 due reminders, and at most 10 of one member (their oldest first, so a member with many due reminders cannot use the whole tick; the rest fire in the following ticks, none is lost and none fires twice); each one is marked fired and its `reminder` notification written in one database transaction, so it is never sent twice, also across a restart, and a reminder that came due while the portal was down is fired once at the first ticks after the start (50 per tick, so a long outage drains over a few ticks). The notification title is always "Reminder for a saved message" with no text; its data holds ids only, and clicking it opens the message through the existing jump-to-message, or the Saved view (`/chat?saved=1`) when the message cannot be read any more. A reminder for a direct message opens the Saved view; Jump to message there opens the conversation, not the message in context, because there is no per-message jump for direct messages. The in-app frame and the push are sent after that commit: a crash between the two would lose that one push, never repeat it. The push payload is the fixed title, the body "Open the portal to see it." and the jump URL: never message text, an author or a channel name.
- **Chat page.** "Save message" / "Remove from Saved" and "Remind me..." in the message menu (Remind me on a message that has a reminder opens with its time and a Clear reminder button; on a message that is not saved yet it saves it and says "Saved"), a "Saved" marker on saved messages (only in the member's own browser), a **Saved** button above the chat that opens the list with Jump to message, Remind me (quick choices, a date and time picker, Clear reminder) and Remove, a **Remove unavailable entries** button (shown only while a listed entry is unavailable; it asks first, then calls `DELETE /api/saved/unavailable` and reloads the list), a full-screen layout with 44 px controls on a phone, and the keyboard path: the message list takes focus, the arrow keys move between messages, the menu key (or Shift+F10) opens that message's menu, arrow keys, Enter and Escape work in it, and Escape closes the Saved view and returns focus to its button. `/chat?saved=1` opens the Saved view. Saved entries are shown with text nodes only.
- **Phones and tablets.** Every action of the message menu (reply, react, copy, save, remind, edit, pin, delete) opened only on a right click or the menu key, and iOS Safari does not fire a context menu on a long press, so they were out of reach there. Where the device has no hover or a coarse pointer, every message now shows a small **...** button (a real button, 44 px, labelled "Message actions", made by `chat-saved.js`, no inline handler) that opens the same menu; the menu key and the arrow keys still work, and on a laptop or desktop the button is hidden. How it looks and whether it feels right on a real phone was not seen (see Not tested). The Saved view, its buttons and a reminder that arrives work on iOS.
- **Routes.** `GET /api/saved`, `GET /api/saved/marks`, `POST /api/saved`, `DELETE /api/saved/{id}`, `DELETE /api/saved/unavailable`, `PUT /api/saved/{id}/remind`, described in docs/API.md and `static/api-docs.html`.

**Breaking changes**

None for existing clients and nothing stops working. The behaviour changes an upgrade adds, which a script could notice:

- **New notification types.** `GET /api/notifications` can now return `stream_reminder` (the schedule) and `reminder` (a saved message); a client that lists notifications by type should show unknown types as text. `portal.js` v52 opens the Saved view for a notification whose data has `saved`.
- **The go-live push switch has a new label** ("Streams you follow going live, and reminders for streams you are interested in") and now also covers stream reminders; its meaning for go-live pushes is unchanged.
- **`/api/streams/schedule/upcoming` is registered before `/api/streams/{id}`**, so the word `schedule` is not read as a stream id on that path.
- **Additions only** otherwise: the schedule, saved-message and calendar-feed routes, seven tables and the `?v=` numbers under Static files.

**Database, static files, rollback and what was not tested**

- **Database.** Seven tables are created with `IF NOT EXISTS` at start, each with its indexes: the six of the stream schedule (`stream_schedule` with `idx_stream_schedule_stream`, `stream_schedule_cancelled`, `stream_schedule_started`, `stream_rsvps` with `idx_stream_rsvps_occ` and `idx_stream_rsvps_entry`, `stream_rsvp_sent` (the sent marks that make a reminder go out once) with `idx_stream_rsvp_sent_occ`, and `schedule_feed_tokens`) and `saved_messages` (id, user_id with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, kind with a `CHECK`, scope_id, message_id, saved_at, remind_at, fired_at, `UNIQUE (user_id, kind, message_id)`, `idx_saved_messages_user (user_id, id)` and the partial index `idx_saved_messages_due (remind_at) WHERE remind_at IS NOT NULL AND fired_at IS NULL`). No column of an existing table is added, nothing is copied or rewritten, so the start is as cheap on a 250 MB database as on an empty one. `push_prefs` is unchanged. Deleting a member removes their marks, feed token and saved entries (foreign keys; `delete_user` removes the saved rows explicitly as well); deleting a stream removes its schedule. Past marks are pruned after 7 days.
- **Static files.** The versions the pages load: `stream-schedule.js` is new at version 8 (`watch.html`, `streams.html` and `index.html`; version 2 hides the Interested button once the start has passed, version 3 tells the owner when the time zone of an entry is no longer available, version 4 gives the owner's editor the form styling of the stream settings, version 5 takes the underline off the Upcoming link to the stream, version 6 makes the Yes, regenerate button ignore a click within 0.6 seconds of the question opening and the second click of a double click, version 7 makes Cancel this one and Restore in the owner's editor ignore a click while a request is in flight and the second click of a double click, and makes Delete the whole entry ask first, version 8 makes Revoke on the calendar link ask first); `streams.js` is version 51 (`index.html`; it mounts the schedule editor in the stream settings and, after Use this title, brings the stream's name field, heading and list up to date); `push.js` is version 5 (every page that loads it; the new switch label); `chat-saved.js` is new (v6, loaded by `chat.html`; v2 refreshes the saved markers at most once a minute when you return to the tab, v3 adds the per-message actions button for touch screens, v4 shows the current reminder of a message that has one when you choose Remind me, says when Remind me saved the message, gives every message its actions button even when the saved markers could not be loaded, v5 adds the Remove unavailable entries button to the Saved view, puts its Remove them and Keep them buttons on their own row below the question, and ignores a Remove them click within 0.6 seconds of the question opening or as the second click of a double click, so a double click on the button cannot remove anything, v6 makes the Remove button of an entry ignore the second click of a double click, any click while a removal is in flight and any click within 0.6 seconds of the list being drawn, so a double click removes one entry and not the one that moved under the pointer); and `portal.js` is now v52 on every page that loads it. No stylesheet changed.
- **Rollback.** Going back to 1.22.0 runs on the migrated database and ignores the seven tables. Stream schedule: 1.22.0's database layer (its `database.py`, run from a checkout of the 1.22.0 commit) opens a migrated database, leaves the new tables as they are, and deleting a member or a stream under it removes their schedule rows through the foreign keys; the integrity check was ok. Schedules, marks and calendar links are kept and become active again with a later upgrade; while 1.22.0 runs, no reminder is sent and no feed is served. Saved messages: the 1.22.0 tree was started twice on a copy of a staged database that holds saved entries (healthy and clean both times, `/health` 200, schema unchanged by the second start); the saved entries and waiting reminders stay stored, no reminder fires while 1.22.0 runs, and one that came due fires at the first ticks after a later upgrade. Notifications of type `reminder` that were already created show in 1.22.0 as ordinary notifications: one that names a message still jumps to it, the others open nothing. The 1.22.0 server process was not started on a database that holds schedule rows; only its database layer was opened on one.
- **Not tested.**
  - **Stream schedule.** Proven by tests on a real database and server with fakes only: the push delivery (a recorder replaces the push service), the clock (tests move it; the staged smoke check uses the real clock and the real 30 second tick), the sign-in (a stand-in replaces the credential check in the in-process tests; the smoke check uses real logins). No phone received a push, no calendar application (Apple Calendar, Google Calendar, Thunderbird) read the feed, and nothing ran behind Cloudflare. The .ics file is checked by a parser written for the test, not by a calendar client.
  - **Saved messages.** The chat page was exercised under Node against a small fake DOM (separate classic scripts sharing only the global object, in two time zones, one of them across a clock change), and a headless Chromium on a staged demo instance drew the message menu and the Saved view (the pictures in Screenshots, desktop size only). Not seen: the real `datetime-local` picker, the context-menu event from the keyboard, a long press on Android and a tap on the **...** button on iOS and Android.
  - **No real push service and no Cloudflare.** The push path was tested with a fake sender and with the staged loopback sink; delivery to a phone, a lock-screen notification and its tap were not seen, and nothing was tried through Cloudflare.
  - **No calendar and no daylight-saving edge beyond the tested ones.** "Tomorrow 9:00" was checked in Europe/Berlin across the March clock change and in America/New_York; other zones, a 9:00 that does not exist, and a device whose clock is wrong were not tried. A client that sends a time with another offset is refused by design.
  - **Not tried at scale.** The clean-up and the tick were run on small databases; the partial index and one-query idle tick are asserted with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` and a query counter, not timed on a 250 MB database.
  - **Desktop Chromium only, and no phone.** The tests draw nothing. The pictures in Screenshots were taken once with a headless Chromium at desktop size on a staged demo instance with made-up data: they show that the countdown, the Interested toggle, the Upcoming strip, the schedule editor, the calendar link block, the message menu and the Saved view render. The schedule editor's form uses the form classes of the stream settings, with every label linked to its field. Not seen: any other browser (Firefox, Safari), a phone or tablet, the focus ring, the full-screen layout and the 44 px targets on a phone, and a calendar application reading the feed.

### Upgrade notes for 1.22.0

1.22.0 gives every request a short ID and replaces the bare error text for an unreachable proxied service with a page that shows the service name, that ID and a retry countdown, and adds maintenance mode (per service, per connection and a global flag) and administrator announcements, reference IDs on the block and rate-limit answers with an administrator lookup, a personal way to organise the dashboard (folders, tags, instant filter, recently used, pins and an open mode per member), and opt-in connection health checks with a status dot on the dashboard. The notes are grouped by feature; one Breaking changes group, and one Database, one Static files, one Rollback and one Not tested bullet after it cover all of them.

**For administrators, in short**

- **Nothing has to be done before upgrading, and nothing new is on or blocking by default.** Connection health checks are off until a superadmin switches them on (Admin > Settings > Connection health checks), no maintenance flag and no announcement exists until an administrator makes one, and scanner blocking and the failed sign-in ban are unchanged and stay off. No setting, `.env` entry or command has to change. 1.22.0 starts on a 1.21.0 database and adds columns and tables on its own (see Database).
- **Request IDs.** Every answer carries an `X-Request-ID` header and every log line written while a request is handled carries `[req=...]`. When a member reads out the ID of an error page, `journalctl -u portal | grep 'req=abcd2345'` finds the line, including the traceback of an unhandled error.
- **Maintenance.** An administrator marks one service or one of their own connections in its edit form (a box, an optional end time and a message); a superadmin can switch on a global flag under Admin > Settings > Maintenance mode that stops only `/proxy`, the embedded browser and `/search`. Administrators always pass. `server.py maintenance-off` clears the global flag from the command line, with the server running or stopped, and Admin > Settings > Maintenance mode lists the connections in maintenance with a Clear button. The global flag cannot lock anybody out of sign-in, the admin pages, the API, chat or the terminal (a connection in maintenance does refuse its own terminal and sockets to members).
- **Announcements.** Admin > Settings > Announcements (administrators): info, warning or outage, up to 1000 characters, optional start and end, shown as a banner on every page until a member dismisses it. They are not shown before sign-in unless that is ticked per announcement.
- **Reference IDs.** A visitor who sees a block or rate-limit page gives you a reference such as `ABCD-EFGH`; Admin > System > Network > Reference lookup shows the stored event and an Unblock button. References are kept for 7 days and bounded.
- **Connection health checks.** Switching them on makes the portal process (as root) open a TCP connection or send a `HEAD` request to the targets of connections members pinned or opened in the last 7 days, through the same host policy as a real connection (loopback, private, link-local and metadata addresses are never dialled). Nothing is written to the target. Read What is probed before you switch them on if members may have saved targets you do not want touched.
- **Alerts.** The global alert cap is now 33 per 10 minutes instead of 30, because member connection health alerts have a bucket of their own (3 per 10 minutes); the room left for every other alert is unchanged.
- **Going back.** 1.21.0 runs on the migrated database, but it has none of the maintenance checks: a service or connection that was in maintenance is open to members again. Clear maintenance first if that matters (see Rollback); the flags stay stored while 1.21.0 runs, so a later upgrade makes them active again.

**Request IDs and the upstream error page**

- **Request ID.** A new outermost middleware makes an 8 character ID from the `secrets` module for every request and sets it as the `X-Request-ID` response header, on normal answers, errors, redirects, the IP-block refusal and proxied responses (a header of the same name sent by an upstream is replaced). A WebSocket handshake gets none. A request ID sent by the client (`X-Request-ID` or similar) is never read, echoed or logged; a malformed request that aiohttp itself refuses (a bare line feed in a header, say) is logged with the part from such a header on omitted and is answered with the fixed text `Bad Request` (aiohttp used to quote the refused bytes back; `server.py` now wraps `RequestHandler.handle_error` for status 400, pinned aiohttp 3.14.5, re-verify before bumping).
- **Log lines.** Every line written while a request is handled reads `<time> [LEVEL] <logger>: [req=abcd2345] <message>`; a line outside a request is unchanged, the `[LEVEL]` token stays where it was, redaction still runs over the whole line, and the JSON log has a `request_id` field. aiohttp's own access line is written after the request and carries no ID. The one log line of an unhandled exception (message and traceback) is written inside the request and does carry it. That holds also when the handler fails after a streamed or proxied response has started: one line under the ID, not a second one without it (the client answer is unchanged: the body breaks off and the connection closes). A task or thread a request starts keeps no ID once that request has ended; a WebSocket keeps it for as long as the socket is open. To find the line for an error a member read out: `journalctl -u portal | grep 'req=abcd2345'`.
- **The page.** `_upstream_error_page` now answers every 502, 503 and 504 for an upstream that cannot be reached or did not answer: the HTTP proxy (`/proxy/{id}/...`, including the embedded browser; 502 and 504), the search service (`/search/`, 502 when it does not answer and 503 when it is not running) and the stream proxies (HLS, WHEP, WHIP and session, which are JSON). The page shows the escaped connection name, the request ID and "Trying again in N s", then reloads (`static/js/upstream-error.js` v2, no inline script, the Content-Security-Policy is unchanged); a POST is not repeated by itself. Inside the embedded browser's frame (no top navigation allowed) the Back to Dashboard link opens in a new tab. It never shows the upstream host, address, port or the exception text. Those go to the log once, as `Upstream error 502 for service '<name>': <reason>`, under the same ID.
- **JSON.** A request under `/api`, an XHR or an `Accept` for JSON without HTML gets JSON with `request_id` and `retry_after` and keeps its old `error` text where it had one.
- **Unchanged.** Other 502s (an upstream response over the size cap, SFTP and storage errors, link previews) keep their text; a redirect loop or a blocked redirect hop in the HTTP proxy is a 502 and now shows the page. `Retry-After` and `Cache-Control: no-store` are set on the page.

**Maintenance mode and announcements**

- **Per service and per connection.** New columns `maintenance`, `maintenance_until` and `maintenance_message` on `services` and `user_connections` (see Database). Only an administrator sets them: the service forms and `POST`/`PUT /api/services`, and the connection form and `POST`/`PUT /api/connections` (a member's maintenance fields on a connection are ignored and the stored values kept, not an error and not a 403, the same way as the `recording` flag, so a form that reads a connection and sends it back keeps working; an administrator who sends back an end time that is unchanged is not told it is in the past even when it has passed; the connection routes only reach connections the caller owns, so an administrator marks the connections they own, shared or not; a member's personal connections are the member's own. Any administrator can clear the maintenance of any connection, though: `GET /api/admin/connections/maintenance` lists the connections that have the flag, with owner and message and never the host, and `POST /api/admin/connections/{id}/maintenance/clear` clears it, audited as `connection.maintenance_clear`, so an open-ended maintenance of an administrator who was demoted never stays stuck; Admin > Settings > Maintenance mode shows the list with a Clear button). It is checked right after each entry point's own access check, so a member without access gets exactly what they got before and nothing says the service exists. A member with access gets 503 with `Retry-After` (the time left, at most 300 seconds, 300 when open-ended; the page reloads itself at that rate), `Cache-Control: no-store` and the page of the previous change with "Down for maintenance until 20:00 UTC" (the page script shows it in the viewer's time zone) and the message, escaped; a JSON answer for API-style requests. A WebSocket is refused with 503 before the upgrade. An end time that has passed stops applying with no job. Administrators (role read from the database) pass.
- **Entry points covered.** `/proxy/{id}/...` HTTP and its WebSocket upgrade (`_proxy_connection_authed`), `/browser` (`http_browser_page`), `/search` (`http_searxng_proxy`, using the service row named `searxng`), the pages `/terminal`, `/vnc`, `/media`, `/spice`, `/proxmox` and `/github` (connection and service branches), `GET /api/connections/{id}/connect`, the SFTP helper `_get_sftp_connection` (`/api/sftp/{id}/...`), and the WebSocket routes `/ws/user-connection/{id}`, `/ws/{terminal,vnc,media,spice,proxmox}/{id}` (a service or a connection id) and `/ws/{service path}` through `_ws_maintenance_refusal` before the upgrade (the two relay handlers keep a second check and close with code 4503). Not covered, on purpose: the HLS, WHEP and WHIP stream routes and `/api/sftp/vod/...` (streams and storage, not services or connections), `/ws/chat` and the local terminal. `tests/test_maintenance.py` walks the registered routes: a route under one of those prefixes, or a handler that looks up a connection or service, without the check or a stated reason fails.
- **The global flag.** One value in the settings table (`maintenance_global`: message, optional end time, who set it), set and cleared by a superadmin with `PUT /api/admin/maintenance` or Admin > Settings > Maintenance mode. It is looked at only by `/proxy`, `/browser` and `/search` and never by `/login`, `/logout`, `/api`, `/admin`, `/health`, static files, chat, terminal sockets or any page (a test compares what a member gets on each of them with and without the flag), so nobody can be locked out; it is off unless a superadmin turns it on. The portal re-reads it every 3 seconds. `server.py maintenance-off` (standard library only, no migration, never creates a database) clears it while the server runs or is stopped. Every set and clear is an audit entry (`maintenance.global_set`, `maintenance.global_clear`) and a `maintenance_changed` security event; the command-line clear writes both, the audit row under the oldest superadmin's account with the actor name `(command line: server.py maintenance-off)` (that table needs an account, and its details are encrypted with a key the tool does not load, so the row has none).
- **Announcements.** New table `announcements`. Administrators (role admin or superadmin) create, edit, end and delete them (`/api/admin/announcements`, each change audited as `announcement.create`, `.update`, `.end`, `.delete`); `GET /api/announcements` gives a signed-in member the active ones; level `info`, `warning` or `outage`; text up to 1000 characters; optional start and end; at most 200 rows. `static/js/announce.js` (loaded by `portal.js` on every page that loads it, outside frames, and by the sign-in page) shows each as a banner built with DOM nodes: **bold**, *italic*, `code`, `[text](link)`, bare links and line breaks only, links only for `http` and `https` with `rel="noopener noreferrer"`, everything else (a script tag, an image with a handler, a `javascript:` link) as plain text. A dismissal is stored in the browser (`localStorage`) by the announcement's id and the time of its last change, so an edit shows it again. A signed-out visitor sees nothing unless the administrator ticked "Show before sign-in" for that announcement (`GET /api/announcements/public`, default off; the answer is kept in memory for 5 seconds, so any number of visitors cost one database read, and any change by an administrator drops it at once). The public live page (`/live`) does not load `portal.js`: the server adds the `announce.js` script tag to it only when the request is signed in (same-origin script, the content security policy is unchanged, and a visitor gets no extra script and so no request every minute that would be answered with 401); the unauthorized page shows no banner; pages that cannot scroll (chat, watch, terminal, VNC, media, SPICE, Proxmox, GitHub, browser, VOD player) and the sign-in page show it floating over the top, so it cannot push the message input or the player controls out of view; a floating banner is at most 40 percent of the window tall and scrolls inside itself, so a long text cannot cover the sign-in form. The script asks for the list every minute and when a hidden tab is shown, makes no request while the tab is hidden, and stops asking after a `401` (signed out).
- **The page and its helper.** `upstream-error.js` also shows the maintenance end time in the viewer's zone. `_upstream_error_page` takes the extra arguments `title`, `note`, `quiet`, `extra_json` and `until_iso`.

**Reference IDs on block and rate-limit pages**

- **What a client sees.** Every `403` made by an IP block (manual block, failed-sign-in ban, scanner block; `_blocked_answer` in the global middleware) and every `429` made by a rate limiter (`_limited`: sign-in, `POST /api/token`, registration and its one-account-a-day rule, the wrong-second-factor limit, incoming webhooks per address and per token, the WebSocket connect and per-member socket limits, the logged-out `/proxy`, `/browser` and `/search` limit, push subscribe, chat history, chat search, the viewer heartbeat and the anonymous-name toggle) carries a reference ID: 8 characters from the `secrets` module (an alphabet without 0, O, 1 and I), shown as `ABCD-EFGH`. It is random: it is not derived from an address or an account. A browser that asks for HTML (`Accept: text/html`, not under `/api`, not an XHR) gets a small page with the ID and the one sentence "Give this reference to an administrator." (a `429` page adds "Wait a moment before trying again." and keeps `Retry-After`); every other client (an `/api` path, an XHR, `Accept: */*` such as curl or a script) keeps its JSON with the old `error` text and one extra field, `ref`. The block page is the same bytes for a manual permanent block, a manual timed block, a sign-in ban and a scanner block once the ID is removed (a test compares them): it names no reason, rule, address or expiry, loads no script, has no link and sets no `Retry-After`. The friendly page of `_upstream_error_page` was not reused for it, because that page reloads itself, loads a script and counts down; both pages are built from the same style.
- **Storage and bounds.** One new table, `block_refs` (ID, kind `block`, `limit` or `overflow`, limiter name, block type, address, reason, account if known, hits, first and last seen). One ID is kept for one client and limiter for 1 hour in memory (capped at 5000 entries), so 10,000 requests from one blocked address are one row, one database read and no log line after the first; the hit count is written at most once a minute and is approximate. At most 60 new IDs are made a minute for IP blocks and, separately, 60 more for rate limiters (so a flood of anonymous requests over a limiter, for example rotating addresses against the sign-in limiter, cannot use up the budget of the blocks): further new clients of that kind in that minute share one `overflow` ID (one row a minute at most for each kind, its reason says why). A rate limiter's ID is kept per address, an IPv6 address as its /64 network. An ID is dropped when its block is removed or written again, so a later block of the same address gets a new ID and a new row, never the old one with the old source and reason. Unblocking from the lookup notes the reference in the audit entry only when it is a block of that very address; any other `?ref=` is ignored. The table keeps at most 5000 rows (the oldest go on insert) and nothing older than 7 days (removed on insert at most every 10 minutes and by the data cleanup, and never returned by the lookup even before that). A failed write gives an answer without an ID, never an error. A block's address and reason are read once, when the row is written, so a later edit of the block does not change the stored event; the lookup also shows the live block row.
- **Lookup.** `GET /api/admin/blocks/ref/{ref}` (administrators: the admin scope rule of the neighbouring block routes, so API keys follow it, plus the account's role read from the database). The order is sign-in, scope and role, a limit of 20 lookups a minute per administrator, then one primary-key read, so a member or a signed-out client gets `401` or `403` before the ID is looked at, and an unknown, malformed or expired ID gets the same `404` and body. The ID is accepted in any case, with spaces or the hyphen. Admin > System > Network has a Reference lookup field (`static/js/block-ref-admin.js`, DOM nodes and `textContent`, no inline handler) that shows the event and an Unblock button, which calls the existing `DELETE /api/admin/blocked-ips/{ip}?ref=...`; that route's checks, its activity entry and its `ip_unblocked` security event apply, and the entry now names the reference. Every lookup that passes the role check is one audit entry, `block_ref_lookup`, with the administrator, the reference as shown (`ABCD-EFGH`, or `(not an ID)` for text that is not an ID; what was typed is never stored) and whether a row was found; a request refused with `401` or `403`, and one refused by the 20 a minute limit, leaves none.
- **Guarded.** `tests/test_blockref.py` fails on a `429` built by hand.
- **Not covered, on purpose.** A `429` that a proxied service itself sends passes through unchanged; the `rate_limited` frame sent over the chat WebSocket is a message, not an HTTP answer; Cloudflare's own errors cannot carry an ID. The `429` for "too many chunks are being prepared" on VOD playback is a cap on one member's running jobs, not a rate limiter, and keeps its text; the limiters that only write to the log (a webhook delivery's own limit) are not answers to a client. The lookup's own limit is a `429` with a reference like the others (limiter `ref_lookup`).

**Dashboard organising: personal folders and tags, instant filter, recently used**

Each member files the connections they can reach (their own and the ones shared with them) in their own folders and tags, and the My Connections tab finds them as you type.

- **Filing is personal.** A new table `connection_user_prefs` holds, per member and connection, a `folder`, `tags`, an `open_mode`, a `pinned` flag and `last_opened_at`. A member who is given a shared connection files it under "Games" and nobody else sees that: not the owner, not another member the connection is shared with, not an administrator (the list API returns only the caller's own filing, as `prefs` on each item). **Connections only:** the admin-defined cards on the Services tab are the same for everybody, so there is nothing personal to file there; that tab only gets a plain text filter.
- **The API.** `PUT /api/connections/{id}/prefs` with any of `folder`, `tags`, `open_mode`, `pinned` (fields not named keep their value). It is authorised like every other connection route (`get_accessible_connection`): a caller with no access gets exactly the answer of a connection that does not exist (`404`, the same body, before the body is read), and nothing is stored. Limits: a folder of at most 40 characters (letters and digits of any language, space and `_ - . & ( ) + '`), at most 10 tags of at most 24 characters (same letters and digits and `. + # - _`, no spaces; stored lower-case and without duplicates), `open_mode` one of `default`, `same`, `new`, `embedded`, `pinned` a real boolean. Anything else is `400` and nothing is stored. A request body over 8 KB (8192 bytes) is refused with `413` before it is parsed, by its `Content-Length` or, when it has none, while it is read. `GET /api/connections` carries `prefs` with each item (`folder`, `tags`, `open_mode`, `pinned`, `last_opened_at`).
- **Pin.** The pin is now per member, so a member can pin a shared connection too. An owner's older pin (`is_pinned`, the `POST /api/connections/{id}/pin` toggle) still counts until the member's own filing says otherwise, and the two stay in step.
- **Last opened.** `last_opened_at` is set when a connection is opened through the connect route, the terminal socket or the proxy, at most once a minute per member and connection (an in-memory guard; 200 proxied requests in a minute are one write; when that write fails the slot stays taken for the minute, so a failing database is not asked again by every sub-request, and the failure is logged at most once a minute). **Behaviour change:** a proxied request no longer updates `use_count` and `last_used_at` of the connection on every request; they are updated once a minute per member and connection, like the new field.
- **Filing ends with access.** The rows are deleted when a share is revoked, when a member leaves, when a share expires (an expired share is refused from the second it runs out, its filing is never returned, and the sweep deletes the rows), when the same member is given access again after an expiry the sweeper had not removed yet (they start empty), when the connection is deleted (everybody's rows of it) and when a user is deleted (their rows and everybody's rows of their connections). The 60 second sweeper also deletes any row that neither an owner nor a live share explains.
- **The dashboard.** The My Connections tab has a filter box over name, type, folder, tags and the host (only for items whose host the member is already shown); `/` focuses it from anywhere on the page that is not a field and has no dialog open, Enter opens the first match (nothing happens when no word is typed and no tag is chosen, nor while an input method is confirming text), Escape clears it. Folders are flat and collapse (kept in this browser, per member); a filter shows matching items even in a collapsed folder. Tag chips filter (all chosen tags must match). A "Recently used" row shows the last 5 opened items and is hidden while a filter or a tag is active. Each card has a pin, an "Open in" choice (default, same tab, new tab, and embedded browser for the HTTP types that have one) and a small dialog for folder and tags. It works with a keyboard only (after a pin, an open mode, a tag chip or the saved dialog redraws the cards, the focus returns to the same control of the same card; Tab stays inside the dialog while it is open) and has a phone-width layout. The Services tab filter works the same way and shows "No service matches the filter." when it hides every card.
- **Open modes.** "Default" keeps what each type has always done (a sized window for terminals, desktops and the embedded browser). "Same tab" replaces the dashboard, "New tab" opens a normal tab, "Embedded browser" (offered only for the HTTP types, which open in the portal's own browser page) opens that page in a tab. A type with no such page treats "embedded" as "new tab".

**Connection health checks: status dots on the dashboard (off by default)**

A superadmin can switch on Admin > Settings > Connection health checks. While it is on, each card of a pinned or recently opened connection shows a small dot: up, down since a time, or unknown.

- **Off by default.** Nothing is probed until a superadmin switches it on (`PUT /api/admin/connection-health`, `enabled` and `interval`, 60 to 900 seconds, default 60); it is read at the start of every round, so switching it off stops all checks within one interval. It cannot lock anyone out: it never changes access, only reads.
- **What is probed.** Only connections that are pinned or were opened in the last 7 days by somebody who still has access (the owner, or a member whose share has not ended). ONE background task, a round every interval (plus or minus 10 percent), at most 100 probes per round and at most 10 targets per owner in a round (filled in turn across members, a member's least recently probed connection first; a member who pins hundreds of connections only gets their own checked less often, and those read unknown once stale; a member looks at no more than 100 of their connections per round), 5 at once, 6 seconds each, never two probes of one target at once, and connections that share a target share one probe. A TCP connect (closed again at once: nothing is written, no banner is read) for the ssh, vnc and tunnel types (`tcp_tunnel`, `secure_tunnel`, with the database types on them); a `HEAD` of `/` for the HTTP types (a `GET` with the body read only up to 1 KB after a `405` or `501`), redirects are **not** followed, no cookie, header or stored secret is sent, TLS verification is the connection's own `verify_ssl`. There is no user-supplied check URL, port or path: the target is what the connection already holds (its `target_url`, else host and port by the proxy's own scheme rule). `vpn_tunnel`, `github`, `mediamtx`, `spice`, `proxmox` and a `browser_mode` connection are never probed and read unknown.
- **Same host policy as a real connection.** Every probe resolves through `resolve_checked_host` (the same refusals: loopback, private, link-local, metadata, shared address space) and dials one of the checked addresses (the HTTP probe's resolver can only answer with those addresses, so there is no second lookup). A name that resolves to a refused address is never contacted and shows **unknown**, not down; a name that does not resolve at all is a failure, as it is for a real attempt.
- **Hysteresis.** Down after 3 failures in a row, up after 2 successes in a row, unknown until then; an HTTP answer below 500 counts as up (a 401 or 404 means the service is there), a 5xx, a refused or reset connection and a timeout are failures. A probe that raises changes nothing. Only the status class and the latency of a response are kept.
- **Stored and alerted.** The state lives in memory; only changes of status are stored (new table `connection_health_log`, pruned after 14 days, the latest row of each connection always kept) and read back at start, so "down since" survives a restart. The log is bounded: at most 24 stored changes per connection per UTC day (after that the status still changes in memory and is shown, but nothing more is stored or alerted until the next day) and at most 50000 rows in all, the oldest removed first. A stored `down` older than 30 minutes is not announced as "back up" after a restart or after the feature is switched off and on. `service.health_changed` fires once per change between up and down (never for a connection that was never up), at most once per connection every 10 minutes, in a bucket of its own (3 per 10 minutes) so a flapping upstream cannot hold back moderation and automod alerts (the member bucket of 5 is theirs alone). To make room for that bucket the global alert cap is now 33 per 10 minutes instead of 30 (the reserve of 10 and the room for the other alerts are unchanged). Third-party formats get "A member's connection is down" with no name and no host.
- **Maintenance.** A connection or a service in maintenance is not reported as down: its transitions send no alert while the maintenance lasts, and if it is still in that state when the maintenance ends the one alert is sent then (a down and a recovery inside the maintenance cancel each other). Checking itself goes on. The dot reads **maintenance** for the owner and for everybody who can reach the connection (no more than the maintenance page already says; no message, no end time).
- **Refused targets.** A name the host policy refuses is not resolved or dialled again for 10 minutes (doubling to an hour) and is logged once when it starts and once when it ends, not once per round.
- **What members see.** `GET /api/connections/status` (registered before `/api/connections/{id}`) answers `{connection id: {status, latency_ms, since}}` for the connections the caller owns or holds a live share of, the same shape for owner and grantee, never an error text, host, address or port, and `{}` while the feature is off. The dashboard polls it every 60 seconds only while the tab is visible and draws no dot when the answer is empty.
- **Staged instances.** A staged instance (`PORTAL_STAGE_MODE`) probes nothing, except a literal `127.0.0.1` target whose port is in the setting `stage_health_target_ports`, under the LAN policy (loopback allowed, metadata and link-local still refused); the setting is read only when the instance is staged, so the production rule is unchanged. A staged instance makes a round every second so a check does not wait minutes.

**Breaking changes**

No route, field, setting or table of 1.21.0 was removed or renamed, and 1.22.0 reads a 1.21.0 database as it is. Behaviour changes an upgrader can notice:

- **A browser gets an HTML page instead of JSON on a block or a rate limit.** A browser navigating to a blocked address sees a small page with a reference ID where it saw the JSON `{"error": "Access denied"}`; a `429` made by a rate limiter does the same, with a sentence to wait and `Retry-After` kept. Scripts and API clients (an `/api` path, an XHR, `Accept: */*` such as curl) keep their JSON and the old `error` text and get one extra field, `ref`. These 403 and 429 answers now carry `Cache-Control: no-store`. The `429` of the WebSocket connect limit used to be an HTML page or the text `Rate limit exceeded`; it is now plain text with the reference for an upgrade request. Scanner blocking and the sign-in ban are unchanged and stay off by default; nothing here can block anyone.
- **Client request-ID headers are blocked.** The portal makes its own request ID and never uses one a client sends. `X-Request-ID`, `X-Correlation-ID` and `Request-Id` from a client are never forwarded to the search service nor to an HTTP connection's own service, also when its `forward_headers` list names one or has a pattern such as `x-*` or `*` that matches (they are on the proxy's blocked list), and an `X-Request-ID` sent by an upstream is replaced in the response. A request that aiohttp itself refuses as malformed is answered with the fixed text `Bad Request` instead of quoting the refused bytes back.
- **Log lines changed.** Inside a request a line reads `<time> [LEVEL] <logger>: [req=abcd2345] <message>` and the JSON log has a `request_id` field, so anything that parsed log lines by position after `<logger>: ` sees the `[req=...]` prefix. A handler that fails with an unhandled exception still answers the same 500, and its one log line (message and traceback) now carries the request ID the response gave. While search is simply not enabled the log gets one INFO line at most every 10 minutes instead of an ERROR line per request.
- **Upstream error pages look different.** The HTTP proxy's 504 page and the search 502 and 503 pages are the new page (same status codes); the proxy's redirect loop or blocked redirect hop (502) shows it too; a hung search service answers 504 through it instead of a bare 500. Other 502s (an upstream response over the size cap, SFTP and storage errors, link previews) keep their text.
